# Synthesis of mixing and resolution

This synthesis digests a collection of papers on the mixing of cold clouds with hot gas and on the numerical resolution that this mixing demands, kept in `reference-works/Mixing and Resolution/Docs`. The collection is gathered around the review of [Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) (2026, [arXiv:2601.16566](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16566)) on simulations of multiphase gas in and around galaxies (1.3). Additions found by web search, which fill gaps in the papers or check their claims, are marked **[GK]** and cited. A **[GK]** mark covers the rest of its paragraph or list item. A paper of the collection is cited by author and year, with the numbers of its sections, equations, figures and tables in the local copy, as in [Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) (2026, §2.1, eq. 2), and its arXiv identifier, or else its DOI or ADS bibcode, is given at its first citation. Within a paragraph, later citations of the same paper give the numbers alone. Works that the papers cite are cited as the papers cite them, with slips in their years corrected, and the papers of the collection keep the year of their published version throughout. The list of sources (4) adds the published record of works that the papers cite as preprints.

## 1. Overview

### 1.1 Origin and publication

[Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) (2026) contributed equally to their review. Gronke is affiliated with the Centre for Astronomy of Heidelberg University, at its Astronomisches Rechen-Institut, and with the Max Planck Institute for Astrophysics in Garching, Schneider with the Department of Physics and Astronomy and PITT-PACC of the University of Pittsburgh. The local copy is the first version on arXiv, of 23 January 2026. Its 92 pages hold the text up to p. 55 and some 370 references from there to p. 92, with 12 figures, 17 numbered equations and no tables, although §2.6 refers to a Table 2 (p. 33). The authors thank the participants of the Multiphase Workshops of 2023 and 2025, Jonathan Stern, Peng Oh and the referees (acknowledgements). The Max Planck Society supported Gronke through a Max Planck Research Group, and the European Union through the grant ERC-2024-STG 101165038 (ReMMU). NASA supported Schneider through the ATP grant 80NSSC22K0720, as did the David and Lucile Packard Foundation. Giana Deskovich drew the illustrations of Fig. 3. **[GK]** By the comments on its [arXiv page](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16566), the review was resubmitted to Living Reviews in Computational Astrophysics after the referee reports. arXiv holds no later version, and no published record was found by November 2026.

### 1.2 Aims and scope

The review gives an overview of numerical simulations of multiphase gas in and around galaxies ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, abstract). It calls gas multiphase when gas at several temperatures lies roughly in the same place (§1). The classical theory of the interstellar medium (ISM) meant by phases the cold, neutral clouds of molecular and atomic hydrogen and the warmer, partly ionised medium that fills the volume around them ([Field](#field-1965) 1965; [McKee and Ostriker](#mckee-ostriker-1977) 1977). Work on the circumgalactic medium (CGM) and the intracluster medium (ICM), and simulations that do not track ionisation, often mean instead a fully ionised plasma with several equilibrium temperatures. Two long-lived phases, hot and cold, often coexist with transient gas at intermediate temperatures, made by mixing or by cooling, the two main themes of the review. Their temperatures depend on the system. Cold means neutral or molecular gas below about 300 K in the ISM, but photoionised gas near $10^4$ K in the CGM, which studies of the ISM call warm. The review follows the usage of each field and states the temperatures in each section. Hot means gas of low density at $T \gtrsim 10^6$ K unless stated otherwise.

The review leaves out the small scales of particle-in-cell simulations, the cosmological scales and the intergalactic medium, the solar corona, protostellar and protoplanetary discs, and the accretion discs of active galactic nuclei (AGN) ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1). It focuses on multiphase systems that act directly on the formation and evolution of galaxies, and on the techniques and results of simulations, connecting their scales. Other reviews cover parts of the field: processes on the scale of haloes, thermal instability among them ([Donahue and Voit](#donahue-voit-2022) 2022), mixing layers and the crushing of clouds ([Faucher-Giguère and Oh](#faucher-giguere-oh-2023) 2023), galactic winds ([Thompson and Heckman](#thompson-heckman-2024) 2024) and cosmic rays ([Ruszkowski and Pfrommer](#ruszkowski-pfrommer-2023) 2023).

Multiphase gas occurs in the ISM of the Milky Way and of other galaxies, from a few kelvin in the coldest molecular clouds to several $10^{10}$ K inside supernova remnants ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.1). In the CGM of large galaxies, a virial shock heats the infalling gas into the hot phase that fills the volume, while thermal instability, the stripping of satellites by ram pressure and winds from the central or the satellite galaxies seed the cooler phases. The ICM, at higher pressures, spans a still broader range. The interface between disc and halo, where the hotter and thinner CGM meets the warm and cool phases of the ISM, is multiphase too, driven among others by outflows. Outflows such as that of the starburst galaxy M82 show temperatures over eight orders of magnitude in the same place, and the winds of AGN carry multiphase gas under strong radiation and fast kinematics. Fig. 1 shows examples in galactic winds, gas stripped by ram pressure, high-velocity clouds and the CGM.

Thermal instability (TI) is the classical explanation for multiphase gas ([Field](#field-1965) 1965, after [Parker](#parker-1953) 1953). Gas in thermal equilibrium, whose heating does not depend on temperature, is unstable where its radiative losses grow as its temperature falls ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.1). This holds where the cooling function falls with temperature, from about $10^5$ to $10^7$ K, and the heating comes from photoionisation. Fig. 2, left, shows the rates of cooling and heating of gas of solar metallicity at two densities under the ultraviolet background at redshift zero, computed with the code Cloudy ([Ferland et al.](#ferland-et-al-2013) 2013). In gas near $10^7$ K at constant pressure, an overdense perturbation cools faster, is compressed by the surrounding pressure and cools faster still, since the losses scale as $n^2$. It settles where heating balances cooling again, near $10^4$ K for the pressure of the halo of the Milky Way, $P/k_\mathrm{B} \sim 10^2$ K cm$^{-3}$, at a temperature that depends on the density, the ionising radiation and the metallicity. Two stable phases then coexist in pressure equilibrium, $n_\mathrm{cool} T_\mathrm{cool} = n_\mathrm{hot} T_\mathrm{hot}$, as long as thermal pressure alone supports them. Magnetic fields or cosmic rays lower the density of the cool phase. The denser cool gas forms small clumps in the hot medium ([McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) 2018, Fig. 4). This origin is often called precipitation. Fig. 2, right, shows its regimes, from hot, thin gas through unstable gas to the dense, cold gas of the ISM, in the phase diagram of a simulated isolated galaxy.

Origins in the dynamics of the gas matter as much. Turbulent mixing at interfaces, through the Kelvin–Helmholtz (KH) and Rayleigh–Taylor instabilities ([Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961), makes gas of intermediate temperature and density from gas that is already multiphase ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.1). Shocks compress and heat the gas that they sweep, and thermal conduction makes gas of intermediate temperature at interfaces between hot and cold gas. In real systems all of these act at once. Analytic work has taught much about each of them, but their combination and the non-linear evolution of the instabilities over long times drive the simulations. Most simulations that the review discusses assume solar metallicity. The cooling rates depend strongly on metallicity, above all between $10^5$ and $10^7$ K, but many studies express their results in dimensionless form or through cooling times, which can be rescaled, so that their conclusions apply more generally (footnote 3).

The review closes by placing its set-ups on one ladder of scales ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3, Fig. 3). Idealised simulations, from below a parsec to tens of parsecs, isolate single processes at high resolution and allow controlled studies of parameters. Larger simulations of bubbles, patches of discs, isolated galaxies and cosmological zooms capture the global context, but rely on limited resolution and on models below the scale of the grid. The review sees the two as complementary parts of one framework. Large simulations set the pressures, densities, metallicities, turbulence, radiation and kinematics in which small ones should run, and small ones interpret the processes in large ones, test analytic theory and calibrate models below the grid. Criteria of survival are thus being tested on ensembles of clouds in realistic outflows, and criteria of thermal instability in haloes with realistic sources of turbulence. Observations remain the ground truth against which simulations at all scales are judged.

### 1.3 The collection

The review of 2026 stands at the centre of the collection. §1 introduces multiphase gas, where it occurs (§1.1), the challenges of computing it (§1.2) and the quantities that characterise its dynamics (§1.3) ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026). §2 describes the simulations, ordered roughly by scale and complexity (§2, Fig. 3): mixing layers (§2.1), clouds in a wind (§2.2), streams and jets (§2.3), thermal instability and turbulent boxes (§2.4), bubbles driven by supernovae and winds in the ISM (§2.5), stratified patches of discs (§2.6), isolated galaxies (§2.7) and haloes (§2.8). §3 summarises, with the technological advances (§3.1), models below the scale of the grid (§3.2) and the open questions (§3.3). The collection digests §1, §2.1 to §2.4 and §3. §2.5 to §2.8 lie outside it.

The further papers fall into four groups. Two older, seminal works treat the crushing of clouds and turbulent mixing layers. Studies of radiative mixing layers and of the survival, growth and characteristic scale of cold gas follow, then studies of resolution. Two studies of fountain clouds moving through a galactic corona come last. They complement subject 2.8 of the collection on conduction and cooling. Each paper is digested in the subsections of the subjects that it serves (2), in the order of the steps of `plan.md`, and its numbers of sections, equations, figures and tables refer to its local copy.

## 2. Numerical methods

Seven subjects group the physics and the numerics of the collection. Clouds in hot winds without cooling take 2.1, turbulent radiative mixing layers 2.2, the survival and growth of cold gas 2.3, the characteristic scale of cooling 2.4, measures of mixing 2.5, numerical resolution and convergence 2.6, and fountain clouds and the condensation of the corona 2.7. `plan.md` lists the subjects, with the steps that write them.

### 2.1 Clouds in hot winds without cooling

#### 2.1.1 The crushing of clouds

Simulations of the crushing of clouds are central to the study of multiphase flows ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). In their simplest form, a spherical cold cloud of density $\rho_\mathrm{cold}$ and radius $r_\mathrm{cl}$ is exposed to a wind of density $\rho_\mathrm{hot}$ and velocity $v_\mathrm{wind}$. The set-up goes back to the picture of the ISM of [McKee and Ostriker](#mckee-ostriker-1977) (1977), which asks how cold clouds interact with the hot bubbles of supernovae, and so how cold high-velocity clouds can be accelerated. The acceleration of cold gas in galactic winds is still an active question ([Thompson and Heckman](#thompson-heckman-2024) 2024, among other reviews), and more than 50 papers have treated the set-up and its variants (footnote 11 points to a list of them kept by W. Banda-Barragán).

That the interaction destroys the cloud was clear early on ([Nittmann, Falle and Gaskell](#nittmann-et-al-1982) 1982). [Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994) established that the shear and the acceleration by ram pressure raise KH and Rayleigh–Taylor instabilities, while a shock crosses the cloud ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2; see also [Murray et al.](#murray-et-al-1993) 1993). The three processes destroy the cold gas on the time of cloud crushing, $t_\mathrm{cc} \sim \chi^{1/2} r_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{wind}$ (eq. 5), which equals the growth times of the instabilities (2.2.1). The wind needs a longer time, $t_\mathrm{drag} \sim \chi\, r_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{wind}$ (eq. 6), to give the cloud enough momentum to accelerate it. Since $t_\mathrm{drag}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim \chi^{1/2} \gg 1$, the cloud is destroyed before it is accelerated. How cold gas nevertheless reaches the observed velocities of hundreds to a thousand km s$^{-1}$ is the problem of entrainment ([Zhang et al.](#zhang-et-al-2017) 2017).

High-resolution simulations in three dimensions followed the mixing and destruction in detail ([Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009; [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2015) 2015; [Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016) ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). They extended the set-up to higher overdensities ([Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009), higher Mach numbers ([Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) 2015), other shapes of clouds and further physics ([Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016). Magnetic fields change the morphology of the cold gas, suppress the mixing and speed up the acceleration. [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2015) (2015) argued that the drag of magnetic fields could solve the problem of entrainment. [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a) showed that magnetic fields shorten the time of acceleration only by a factor of a few. That suffices for overdensities $\chi \lesssim 30$, while the overdensities $\gtrsim 100$ that matter most in astrophysics would need fields stronger than the thermal pressure, with a plasma beta $\beta < 1$. **[GK]** The review cites this result, and the pulsations, expanding winds and transfer of momentum of 2.3, as Gronke and Oh (2020b), the letter that its list gives to their paper on shattering, Is multiphase gas cloudy or misty? ([arXiv:1912.07808](https://arxiv.org/abs/1912.07808)). The abstract of their paper on entrainment, 2020a ([arXiv:1907.04771](https://arxiv.org/abs/1907.04771)), names these subjects, that of the paper on shattering none of them.

#### 2.1.2 Related set-ups

The review also names wind tunnels into which the cold gas enters by other means than hydrodynamic interaction ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). In galaxies stripped by ram pressure, gravity holds the gas against the wind. Some studies treat the physics of stripping in small boxes, others the long-term evolution and the multiphase structure of the stripped tail in large domains. A CGM around the infalling galaxy can promote further condensation in the tail, and magnetic fields suppress structure on small scales, so that the tails come out smoother and less clumpy. Other wind tunnels blow cold stellar winds, $\lesssim 10^4$ K, into a hot and possibly moving medium of $\gtrsim 10^6$ K, such as the wind of the AGB star Mira in the Local Bubble, whose observed tail they reproduce well, or the faster and hotter winds of Wolf–Rayet stars. Such studies focus on global outcomes rather than on the multiphase interface, but the same mixing, cooling and surface instabilities act, and the scalings of 2.2 and 2.3 often apply.

#### 2.1.3 The shock–cloud problem of Klein, McKee and Colella

[Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994, [doi:10.1086/173554](https://doi.org/10.1086/173554)) wrote from the Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory and the University of California at Berkeley, Colella from its Department of Mechanical Engineering. The paper was received on 28 May 1993 and appeared on 1 January 1994. It presents a comprehensive numerical study of the simplest interaction of a shock with an interstellar cloud: a steady, planar shock strikes an isolated, spherical cloud, and radiative losses, thermal conduction, magnetic fields and gravity are neglected (abstract, §1). The problem then depends on two numbers, the Mach number $M$ of the shock and the density contrast $\chi \equiv \rho_\mathrm{c0}/\rho_\mathrm{i0}$ of cloud and intercloud medium (eq. 2.1), and for strong shocks the dependence on $M$ scales out. Variations treat a softer equation of state in the cloud, $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 1.1$ instead of $5/3$, as a hint of radiative losses, a cylindrical cloud, and a cloud in a wind without a shock. The authors meant the study as a benchmark for more realistic calculations. They name six open questions: the rate of stripping and the mass stripped, the transfer of momentum, the appearance of the shocked cloud, the effect of a radiative intercloud shock, the onset of gravitational instability, and the effect of magnetic fields.

In the ISM, diffuse atomic clouds of $T \sim 10^2$ K and $n \sim 40\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and molecular clouds of $T \sim 10$ K and $n \sim 10^3\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ lie in warm gas of $T \sim 10^4$ K and $n \sim 0.3\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and in hot coronal gas of $T \sim 10^6$ K and $n \sim 3 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §1). The contrast $\chi$ is therefore of order $10^2$ for cold atomic clouds in warm gas and for warm clouds in coronal gas, $10^3$ for molecular clouds in warm gas and $10^4$ for cold atomic clouds in coronal gas (§2.1). Earlier calculations were underresolved. [Nittmann, Falle and Gaskell](#nittmann-et-al-1982) (1982) used flux-corrected transport, others about 30 zones per cloud radius, with numerical diffusion at the boundary that hid the mixing by instabilities, or about 40 zones with the piecewise parabolic method. [Stone and Norman](#stone-norman-1992) (1992) ran the first three-dimensional calculation, found the vortex rings of two dimensions unstable, but confirmed that the cloud is disrupted. Apart from one study, all treated cloud and intercloud medium as one fluid that differs only in density.

#### 2.1.4 Timescales and limits

When the intercloud shock of velocity $v_\mathrm{b}$ meets the cloud, it drives a shock into it, whose velocity follows from pressure balance with the shocked intercloud gas, $v_\mathrm{s} \simeq (\rho_\mathrm{i0}/\rho_\mathrm{c0})^{1/2} v_\mathrm{b} = v_\mathrm{b}/\chi^{1/2}$, as long as the intercloud shock is not radiative ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §2.1, eq. 2.2). The intercloud shock sweeps across the cloud in $t_\mathrm{ic} \equiv 2a_0/v_\mathrm{b}$ (eq. 2.3), with the initial radius $a_0$. The cloud is crushed in the cloud-crushing time $t_\mathrm{cc} \equiv \chi^{1/2} a_0/v_\mathrm{b} = a_0/v_\mathrm{s}$ (eq. 2.4), the basic timescale of the shocked cloud. The blast wave accelerates the cloud in two stages: the cloud shock gives it $v_\mathrm{s}$, and the shocked intercloud gas then drags it along until it moves with that gas at $\frac{3}{4} v_\mathrm{b}$, for $\gamma = 5/3$. For a large contrast the second stage dominates, with the equation of motion $m_\mathrm{c}\,dv'_\mathrm{c}/dt = -\frac{1}{2} C_\mathrm{D}\,\rho_\mathrm{i1}\,v_\mathrm{c}'^2 A$ (eq. 2.5). Here $v'_\mathrm{c}$ is the velocity of the cloud relative to the shocked intercloud gas, $C_\mathrm{D} \sim 1$ the drag coefficient, $\rho_\mathrm{i1} \simeq 4\rho_\mathrm{i0}$ the density of the shocked intercloud gas and $A$ the cross-section of the cloud. A constant cross-section gives the drag time $t_\mathrm{drag,0} = \chi a_0/(C_\mathrm{D} v_\mathrm{b}) = \chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{cc}/C_\mathrm{D}$ (eq. 2.6). The KH instability of wavenumber $k$ grows in $t_\mathrm{KH}/t_\mathrm{cc} = (v_\mathrm{b}/v_\mathrm{rel})/(k a_0)$ (eq. 2.7), with the relative velocity $v_\mathrm{rel}$, and the Rayleigh–Taylor instability, driven by the deceleration $g \simeq a_0/t_\mathrm{cc}^2$, in $t_\mathrm{RT}/t_\mathrm{cc} \simeq (k a_0)^{-1/2}$ (eq. 2.8). Short waves grow fastest, but waves with $k a_0 \sim 1$ disrupt most, and both grow in about $t_\mathrm{cc}$, so that the cloud is destroyed in a few $t_\mathrm{cc}$.

The pressure behind a blast wave changes on a time $t_P \simeq 0.1 R_\mathrm{c}/v_\mathrm{b}$, for a cloud at the distance $R_\mathrm{c}$ from the explosion (eq. 2.10). Clouds are small when $t_\mathrm{cc} \ll t_P$, medium when $t_\mathrm{cc} \gtrsim t_P \gtrsim t_\mathrm{ic}$, and large when $t_\mathrm{ic} > t_P$ (§2.2). The paper treats small clouds, which for a contrast of 100 must be smaller than 1 per cent of the radius of a supernova remnant. Its neglect of physics has limits (§2.3). The cooling time behind a strong shock is $2.54 \times 10^{10}\,\beta_\Lambda^{-1}\,v_{\mathrm{s}7}^3/n_0$ s (eq. 2.11), with $v_{\mathrm{s}7} = v_\mathrm{s}/(10^7\,\mathrm{cm\,s^{-1}})$, the density $n_0$ ahead of the shock and a factor $\beta_\Lambda$ of order unity. A cloud shock with a cooling time ten times $t_\mathrm{cc}$ needs $v_{\mathrm{s}7} > 3.33\,(n_\mathrm{c0}\,a_\mathrm{pc})^{1/4}$, with $a_\mathrm{pc} = a_0/(1\,\mathrm{pc})$ (eq. 2.12). Thermal conduction is governed by the saturation parameter $\sigma'_0$ (eq. 2.13), whose ratio to $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{cc}$ in the shocked intercloud gas is $2 \times 10^{-4} \chi^3$ (eq. 2.14). Where cooling is slow, the heat flux is thus saturated for $\chi > 10$, and saturated evaporation ablates a cloud of $\chi \sim 10^2$ in about $t_\mathrm{cc}$ (eq. 2.16). Neglecting conduction therefore assumes magnetic fields too weak to matter for the dynamics but strong enough to inhibit conduction. Gravity is negligible, since a non-radiative shock raises the largest stable mass of the cloud as $M^3$ (eq. 2.17).

#### 2.1.5 The evolution of the shocked cloud

The equations are invariant under $t \to tM$, $v \to v/M$ and $P \to P/M^2$ (eq. 4.3), so that for $M \gg 1$ the evolution in units of $t/t_\mathrm{cc}$ does not depend on $M$ ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §4). The global quantities of runs with $M = 10$ and $M = 1000$ agree to within 15 per cent at $5.05\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Table 2), and their morphologies at $2.95\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ closely (Fig. 2).

The interaction passes through four stages (§5). In an initial transient, the blast wave drives a shock into the cloud and reflects a shock that settles into a bow shock or bow wave within about $t_\mathrm{ic}/2$. In the compression, the flow around the cloud converges on the axis behind it after about $t_\mathrm{ic}$, raising a strong reflected shock and driving a shock into the rear of the cloud. The side shocks are weaker, so the cloud is compressed into a pancake. In the re-expansion, the main cloud shock reaches the rear, a strong rarefaction runs back into the cloud, and the cloud expands downstream and, against the low pressure at its sides, laterally until a time $t_\mathrm{m}$ of a few $t_\mathrm{cc}$. In the destruction, which overlaps the re-expansion, instabilities and differential forces fragment the cloud. In the standard case, $\chi = M = 10$, the intercloud shock converges behind the cloud at $0.94\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, or $1.5\,t_\mathrm{ic}$, for other contrasts too. A double Mach reflection and a supersonic vortex ring form behind the cloud, and the shock from the rear meets the main cloud shock at about $1.25\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, which raises the pressure to six and the density to 2.5 times their values behind the main cloud shock (eqs. 5.5, 5.6). The mean pressure of the cloud peaks near $t_\mathrm{cc}$ and settles into rough equilibrium with the intercloud gas after about $4\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Fig. 3), and the radial expansion slows by $t_\mathrm{m} \simeq 3.8\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. The final mean density, $2.13\,\rho_\mathrm{c0}$ in the simulation against $1.92\,\rho_\mathrm{c0}$ from an adiabatic estimate (eq. 5.7), is about half the naive factor of 4, so the cloud radiates less than expected and the condition of eq. 2.12 may be relaxed.

The Richtmyer–Meshkov instability of the impulsively accelerated boundary grows linearly in time and so matters only for large perturbations. In the simulations the mesh supplies them, and they shrink as the resolution grows, but real clouds are not smooth ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §5.1). The KH and Rayleigh–Taylor instabilities dominate the destruction, visibly by $2.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Fig. 5). By $3.8\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the cloud consists of a flattened core and a plume of fragments that holds over 70 per cent of its mass, with vortex rings along the shear layer where it fragments most (Fig. 6). By $9.6\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ it is completely fragmented, spread over twice its transverse and five times its axial dimension, with no fragment above 2 per cent of its mass (Fig. 7).

For $\chi = 3$, 10, 30 and 100 at $M = 10$, the drag times are 1.53, 2.58, 2.96 and $3.79\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, the destruction times 8.16, 3.79, 3.17 and $3.90\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, the mixing times above 10, 5.13, 3.93 and $3.91\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, and $t_\mathrm{m}$ is 4.00, 3.70, 3.90 and $4.20\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (§5.2, Table 3), as 2.5.2 defines these times. The scaling with $t_\mathrm{cc}$ thus holds to lowest order, with weak deviations. The drag time grows slowly with $\chi$, since the initial drag scales with $\chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{cc}$ (eq. 2.6), and the higher relative velocity at large $\chi$ speeds the instabilities, so that $t_\mathrm{dest}/t_\mathrm{cc}$ falls from $\chi = 3$ to 30. Its rise at $\chi = 100$ may reflect a fragile definition of the largest fragment. The cloud is compressed along the axis and then stretches steadily, more so at larger $\chi$, expands radially to about $2a_0$, and becomes prolate. Its axial velocity dispersion is $(0.1$–$0.2)\,v_\mathrm{b}$ for $\chi = 10$, somewhat more for $\chi = 100$, and its radial dispersion $(0.06$–$0.16)\,v_\mathrm{b}$, which persists after the radial growth stops and so measures the vorticity (Figs. 8–10). With $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 1.1$, the cloud is compressed much more, by factors of 2.0 across and 4.4 along the axis against 1.2 and 2.3, re-expands more slowly and presents a smaller cross-section, so that the drag is weaker and the instabilities more violent (§5.3, Fig. 11). Its pencil-like core still holds 55 per cent of the mass at $3.8\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, against 30 per cent for $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 5/3$. Its mean compression reaches 7.1 against 2.2 at $6.75\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, and it survives longer, $5.7$ against $3.8\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Table 4).

#### 2.1.6 Drag, vorticity and fragmentation

With a constant cross-section, the cloud would move with the shocked gas after $t_\mathrm{drag,0} \simeq \chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{cc}$, the time it takes to sweep up a column density equal to its own ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §6). Its lateral re-expansion, modelled as $a(t) = [a_0^2 + (C_\mathrm{c} t)^2]^{1/2}$ until $t_\mathrm{m}$ (eq. 6.1), with the sound speed $C_\mathrm{c}$ of the shocked cloud, raises the drag considerably. It enters the solution for the velocity of the cloud through a factor $1 + 0.104\,(t/t_\mathrm{cc})^2$ for $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 5/3$ (eqs. 6.2, 6.3). If the expansion went on, the characteristic time would grow as $\chi^{1/6}$, but it stops at $t_\mathrm{m} \sim 4\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, after which the relative velocity falls as $1/t$. With $C_\mathrm{D} = 1$ and the mass-weighted velocity of all cloud material, the model agrees well at first. Near $t_\mathrm{m}$ it overestimates the velocity by up to a factor of 1.5 for $\chi = 10$ and 2 for $\chi = 100$, and it converges with the simulations soon after (Fig. 12). For $\chi = 30$ it stays within 12 per cent between 5.85 and $12.2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, by when the relative velocity has fallen to 7.7 per cent of its initial value. Internal shocks in the cloud, which the model leaves out, cause part of the discrepancy.

Vortex rings play an important part in the destruction ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §7). An ideal isentropic fluid cannot make vorticity, but the shocked flow is not isentropic, and the scheme has numerical viscosity, so the authors show that the vorticity is not an artefact. The baroclinic term $\nabla P \times \nabla(1/\rho) = \nabla T \times \nabla s$ makes the vorticity (eqs. 7.6, 7.7), while gravity cannot and viscosity only spreads it. Three sources matter. The shock sweeping over the cloud gives a circulation $\Gamma_\mathrm{shock} \simeq -2.25\,v_\mathrm{b} a_0 (1 - \chi^{-1/2})$ (eq. 7.9). The flow past the cloud gives $\Gamma_\mathrm{post} \simeq -\frac{9}{64}(\chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{drag}/t_\mathrm{cc})\,v_\mathrm{b} a_0$ (eq. 7.12). The supersonic vortex ring behind the cloud gives $\Gamma_\mathrm{ring} \simeq +\frac{3}{4} v_\mathrm{b} a_0$ (eq. 7.13), of opposite sign. Inside the cloud the vorticity is smaller by about $\chi^{-1/2}$. For $\chi = M = 100$ the simulation gives $-2\,v_\mathrm{b} a_0$ by $0.3\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ against $-2.03$ predicted, a ring of $+0.73$ against $+0.75$, and a total of $-7.5\,v_\mathrm{b} a_0$ against $-6.7$ from shock and flow (Fig. 13). For $\chi = 10$ the agreement is weaker, $-2.1$ against $-2.7\,v_\mathrm{b} a_0$, since the vorticity of the cloud itself is not estimated. The circulation of the case with $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 1.1$ is 1.34 times larger, against 1.5 predicted. The authors conclude that numerical viscosity does not affect the circulation significantly. The peak vorticity, set by the thickness of the shear layer and so of the grid, is not converged and scales as $(\Delta x)^{-1}$ (2.6.7).

The fragmentation destroys the cloud on large and small scales ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §8). For $10 \lesssim \chi \lesssim 100$ the destruction time is $t_\mathrm{dest} \simeq 3.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, in which the blast wave advances $3.5\,\chi^{1/2} a_0$ (eq. 8.1), so that a cloud of $\chi = 10$ is largely destroyed after ten cloud radii. Without viscosity, surface tension or magnetic fields, the KH and Rayleigh–Taylor instabilities have no smallest scale, and the cloud fragments down to the grid, where zones hold both fluids (2.5.2). Mixing proceeds faster at larger $\chi$, through the higher relative velocity (Fig. 17). The KH instability, whose growth rate scales as $k$ against $k^{1/2}$ for the Rayleigh–Taylor instability, dominates the small-scale fragmentation. In a hierarchical model, in which the cloud breaks into a few large fragments and these again, the cloud travels $\Delta z'_\mathrm{c} \propto -\chi^{1/2} a_0$ in the frame of the shocked gas before it is fragmented completely (eqs. 8.2, 8.3). The simulations give $-1.68\,\chi^{1/2} a_0$ at $t_\mathrm{mix}$ for $3 \le \chi \le 100$ (eq. 8.4, Fig. 18), with 25 per cent more at the largest $\chi$, where the core is stripped from its surface rather than broken up. For $\chi = 3$ to 30 the cloud fragments on large scales before it mixes, while at $\chi = 100$ both happen together. In the frame of the unshocked gas, the cloud moves only about $(6$–$8)\,a_0$ by $t_\mathrm{mix}$ for $10 \lesssim \chi \lesssim 100$ (eq. 8.5), so that a small non-radiative cloud is displaced no more than about eight radii before it is destroyed and mixed.

#### 2.1.7 Related problems and the Cygnus Loop

A cylinder whose axial size is three times its radius, $c_0 = 3a_0$, evolves much like the sphere when time is measured in the modified crushing time $t'_\mathrm{cc} \equiv (\chi a_0 c_0)^{1/2}/v_\mathrm{b}$ (eq. 9.2), with a destruction time of $4.64\,t'_\mathrm{cc}$ against $3.79\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §9.1, Table 5). Modest changes of shape thus do not alter the conclusions. A cloud in a wind, without the shock, was set up as a compressed, flattened ellipsoid of axial radius $c_1 \simeq a_0/4$ and density $\chi$ times that of the shocked intercloud gas, moving at the relative velocity $0.505\,v_\mathrm{b}$ that the shock would leave, a Mach number of 0.90 (§9.2). It is decelerated and destroyed only about 50 per cent more slowly than the shocked cloud, with drag, destruction and expansion times of 3.79, 6.15 and $6.17\,t'_\mathrm{cc}$ (Table 5). Transient bow and transmitted shocks still form, but weaker, and the cloud expands laterally through the Venturi effect and stretches more along the axis. These results agree with those of [Murray et al.](#murray-et-al-1993) (1993) at 25 zones per radius. Its circulation, measured at $-3.0\,|v'_\mathrm{c0}|\,a_0$ against $-1.5$ from eq. 7.12, is less than in the shocked case, in line with its longer destruction. Taking $m_\mathrm{c}/t_\mathrm{dest}$ as the rate of entrainment gives a coefficient of about 0.5 in the scaling of mass loading of [Hartquist et al.](#hartquist-et-al-1986) (1986) (eq. 9.3), consistent with their estimate of order unity.

The case with $\gamma_\mathrm{c} = 1.1$ and $\chi \simeq 10$ resembles a shocked cloud in the Cygnus Loop that [Fesen, Kwitter and Downes](#fesen-et-al-1992) (1992) observed in $\mathrm{H}\alpha$, for $a_0 \simeq 0.5$ pc and $v_\mathrm{b} \simeq 400\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, so that $t_\mathrm{ic} = 2440$ yr and $t_\mathrm{cc} = 3900$ yr ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §10). At $1.26\,t_\mathrm{cc} = 1.99\,t_\mathrm{ic}$, close to the observed age of 1.7 to $1.9\,t_\mathrm{ic}$, the computed intercloud shock bends behind the cloud and reattaches through a Mach disk, and a flattened core with swept-back arms forms, as in the observations (Figs. 20, 21). The paper predicts velocity dispersions of about $60\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ along and $40\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ across the axis. It argues that the feature taken for the bow shock is unrelated, since at $\chi \simeq 10$ the bow shock soon becomes a bow wave, and it reads X-ray observations as showing a cloud elongated along the line of sight.

The authors conclude that clouds are decelerated and destroyed in several $t_\mathrm{cc}$, independently of the parameters over the range studied, of the spherical shape and even of the initial blast wave ([Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) 1994, §11). This is much shorter than the time to sweep up the column density of the cloud. The high pressure inside the shocked cloud and the low pressure at its sides expand it laterally and hasten its deceleration and destruction. Since the drag time is in many cases shorter than the destruction time, clouds can be accelerated effectively, contrary to [Nittmann, Falle and Gaskell](#nittmann-et-al-1982) (1982). The principal limitation is the restriction to two dimensions, since the prominent vortex rings are unstable in three, but three-dimensional calculations bear out the results by and large ([Stone and Norman](#stone-norman-1992) 1992). §11 gives the range of the drag time as 1.6 to $3.4\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ for $\chi$ from 3 to 100, where Table 3 gives 3.79 at $\chi = 100$, and destruction times falling from 4 to about $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ between $\chi = 10$ and 400.

### 2.2 Turbulent radiative mixing layers

#### 2.2.1 The shear layer and the Kelvin–Helmholtz instability

Shear layers, or turbulent mixing layers, are among the simplest and most idealised set-ups for multiphase gas ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). Two phases of densities $\rho_\mathrm{hot}$ and $\rho_\mathrm{cold}$ lie on either side of a perturbed interface and move along it with a relative velocity $v_\mathrm{shear}$. The KH instability develops and grows on a time $t_\mathrm{KH} \sim \chi^{1/2} \ell / v_\mathrm{shear}$ ([Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961), with the overdensity $\chi \equiv \rho_\mathrm{cold}/\rho_\mathrm{hot}$ and the length $\ell$ of the perturbation. It drives the mixing of the phases and their exchange of mass, momentum and energy. A sharp interface, smooth only on scales much smaller than $\ell$, is unstable at all wavelengths that the system allows. Such interfaces are common in multiphase media, where the cold phase cools much faster than on any dynamical time, so that shear between phases always brings mixing and an exchange of mass. Four effects can weaken or suppress the instability, and with it the mixing ([Landau](#landau-1944) 1944; [Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961):

- Gravity. With an acceleration $g$ that points from the hot to the cold medium, only scales $\ell < v_\mathrm{shear}^2/(g\chi)$ are unstable, so a strong effective gravity suppresses the instability ([Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961).
- The thickness of the shear layer. A velocity that changes smoothly over a width $d$ suppresses the modes below about $10d$, which do not feel the shear ([Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961; [Berlok and Pfrommer](#berlok-pfrommer-2019b) 2019b). In a viscous flow, the instability is thus suppressed where the viscous time $t_\nu \sim (\ell/10)^2/\nu$, with the kinematic viscosity $\nu$, is shorter than $t_\mathrm{KH}$ ([Roediger et al.](#roediger-et-al-2013) 2013; [Marin-Gilabert et al.](#marin-gilabert-et-al-2022) 2022).
- Supersonic flow. Above a critical Mach number, shocks that follow the growing perturbations damp them. With $\mathcal{M} \equiv v_\mathrm{shear}/c_\mathrm{s,hot}$, the threshold is $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{crit} = (1 + \chi^{-1/3})^{3/2}$ ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, eq. 1; [Landau](#landau-1944) 1944; [Mandelker et al.](#mandelker-et-al-2016) 2016).
- Magnetic fields. By the review, a field component parallel to the interface stabilises the flow for $v_\mathrm{shear} < (v_\mathrm{A,hot}\,v_\mathrm{A,cold})^{1/2}$, with the Alfvén velocity $v_\mathrm{A} = B_\parallel/\rho^{1/2}$ of each medium ([Chandrasekhar](#chandrasekhar-1961) 1961). A perpendicular field can suppress mixing as well, since the shear bends it into a stabilising parallel component ([Ryu, Jones and Frank](#ryu-et-al-2000) 2000; [Das and Gronke](#das-gronke-2023) 2023). **[GK]** The linear theory of an incompressible vortex sheet with parallel fields gives stability for $\rho_\mathrm{hot}\rho_\mathrm{cold}\,v_\mathrm{shear}^2 < (\rho_\mathrm{hot} + \rho_\mathrm{cold})(B_\mathrm{hot}^2 + B_\mathrm{cold}^2)$ in these units. A uniform field thus stabilises the flow for $v_\mathrm{shear} < [2(v_\mathrm{A,hot}^2 + v_\mathrm{A,cold}^2)]^{1/2}$, close to $\sqrt{2}\,v_\mathrm{A,hot}$ at large $\chi$. For a uniform field the geometric mean of the review equals $v_\mathrm{A,hot}\,\chi^{-1/4}$, below this threshold by a factor of about $\sqrt{2}\,\chi^{1/4}$.

The linear growth of the KH instability is well understood and serves as a benchmark for codes ([Lecoanet et al.](#lecoanet-et-al-2015) 2015). Its non-linear stage, in which turbulence and secondary instabilities develop the mixing layer in full, needs simulations ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). Radiative cooling changes the nature of the instability, since it lets mass pass continuously between the phases, and the review turns to this case.

#### 2.2.2 The model of Begelman and Fabian

The first simulations of radiative turbulent mixing layers ([Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) 2006; [Kwak and Shelton](#kwak-shelton-2010) 2010; [Kwak, Shelton and Henley](#kwak-et-al-2015) 2015; [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019) followed the analytic work of [Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990), which [Slavin, Shull and Begelman](#slavin-et-al-1993) (1993) extended ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). Begelman and Fabian predicted that the mean temperature of the layer is the average weighted by the fluxes of mass into it across the interface, $\bar T = (\dot m_\mathrm{hot} T_\mathrm{hot} + \dot m_\mathrm{cold} T_\mathrm{cold})/(\dot m_\mathrm{hot} + \dot m_\mathrm{cold})$ (eq. 2). Most of the gas belongs to the hot or the cold phase, but the gas at $\bar T$ dominates the cooling, so that a mixing layer is usefully treated as a medium of three phases. The hot gas enters at $\dot m_\mathrm{hot} \sim \eta_\mathrm{hot}\,\rho_\mathrm{hot}\,v_\mathrm{mix} A$ (eq. 3), with the area $A$ of the layer parallel to the flow, a characteristic velocity of mixing $v_\mathrm{mix}$, and an efficiency $\eta_\mathrm{hot}$ for the imperfect entrainment of hot gas. It carries in enthalpy at the rate $\dot e \sim (5/2)\,\eta_\mathrm{hot} P v_\mathrm{mix}$, at the pressure $P$. The model assumes that this inflow forms eddies, on scales $l_\mathrm{cold}$ up to the size $l_\mathrm{t}$ of the largest eddies, which pull cold gas in at $\dot m_\mathrm{cold} \sim \eta_\mathrm{cold}\,\rho_\mathrm{cold}\,l_\mathrm{cold} A/t_\mathrm{KH}$ (eq. 4), with an efficiency $\eta_\mathrm{cold}$ and the growth time $t_\mathrm{KH} \sim (\rho_\mathrm{cold}/\rho_\mathrm{hot})^{1/2}\,l_\mathrm{cold}/v_\mathrm{mix}$. With efficiencies of similar size, $\eta_\mathrm{cold} \sim \eta_\mathrm{hot}$, eq. 2 gives the geometric mean of the two temperatures, $\bar T \sim (T_\mathrm{cold} T_\mathrm{hot})^{1/2}$. Derivations from geometry or from the fluxes of mass and enthalpy reach the same temperature ([Hillier and Arregui](#hillier-arregui-2019) 2019; [Faucher-Giguère and Oh](#faucher-giguere-oh-2023) 2023, §3.4; footnote 7). **[GK]** The review writes eq. 3 with the area $A$ and eq. 4 without it; Begelman and Fabian give both as rates per unit area (2.2.5). The two fluxes then stand in the ratio $\dot m_\mathrm{cold}/\dot m_\mathrm{hot} \sim \chi^{1/2}$, and with pressure equilibrium, $\chi = T_\mathrm{hot}/T_\mathrm{cold}$, eq. 2 yields the geometric mean exactly.

The model does not predict the efficiencies, so even with $\eta_\mathrm{hot} \sim \eta_\mathrm{cold}$ it cannot predict the inflow of eq. 3, on which the existence and growth of the phases depend ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). It also relies on rapid mixing and leaves out cooling and viscosity, which may act back on the turbulence ([Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) 2006). Studies differ on its validity, but agree within a factor of about five that it predicts the abundances of the ions of intermediate temperature ([Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) 2006; [Kwak and Shelton](#kwak-shelton-2010) 2010; [Kwak, Shelton and Henley](#kwak-et-al-2015) 2015), while [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019) may find larger deviations. Such simulations must reach a steady state in a box wide enough across the flow, which some runs of [Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) (2006) do not ([Kwak and Shelton](#kwak-shelton-2010) 2010; [Kwak, Shelton and Henley](#kwak-et-al-2015) 2015; footnote 8). Later work points out that the abundances of these ions depend on the distribution of temperature, which needs thermal conduction (2.2.3).

#### 2.2.3 Mixing with cooling

Later work maps how the inflow of hot gas, and the structure and dynamics of the layer, depend on the geometry of the flow and on other parameters ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). The hot gas flows in at about the sound speed of the cold gas, $v_\mathrm{mix} \sim c_\mathrm{s,cold}$. The inflow scales as $\dot m \propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$ when cooling is slow ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019; [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021), and as $\dot m \propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$ when it is fast ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a; [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021; [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020). Slow and fast refer to the time of mixing, $t_\mathrm{mix} \sim \ell/u'$ with the local turbulent velocity $u'$, and the Damköhler number $\mathrm{Da} \equiv t_\mathrm{mix}/t_\mathrm{cool}$ separates the two regimes at unity. [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021) drew the analogy with the fronts of turbulent combustion, in which gas burns on a time of reaction where in a mixing layer it cools on $t_\mathrm{cool}$. Fig. 4 shows a schematic of a mixing layer after [Slavin, Shull and Begelman](#slavin-et-al-1993) (1993), a surface of constant temperature in a simulation of [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020), and the two regimes of [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021), which differ in morphology and in the energy that they radiate.

The interface of a mixing layer is fractal ([Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020; [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021). [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) showed this in simulations and built an analytic model on the fractal shape ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). Its scalings differ slightly from those of the model of [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021), which draws on combustion, but the simulations show that mixing, followed by cooling, is the process that matters under approximate pressure equilibrium ([Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020; [Chen, Fielding and Bryan](#chen-et-al-2022) 2022).

The turnover time of an eddy grows with its size, as $t_\mathrm{eddy} \propto \ell^{2/3}$ in a Kolmogorov cascade, so the largest scales are the bottleneck of mixing and cooling ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). The morphology of the layers is therefore generally not converged, while the rates of mass transfer are ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021). Since turbulent diffusion dominates, thermal conduction matters little for the transfer of mass and for the total luminosity of the layer ([Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) 2006; [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021). It matters much for the full distribution of temperature and for the emission of the single ions of intermediate temperature ([Tan and Oh](#tan-oh-2021) 2021). 2.6.3 draws the consequences for resolution.

#### 2.2.4 Higher Mach numbers and magnetic fields

Most of these studies treat subsonic and transonic shear ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). [Yang and Ji](#yang-ji-2022) (2022) extended them to higher Mach numbers, for slow cooling, $\mathrm{Da} < 1$. The turbulent velocity saturates at $\mathcal{M} \gtrsim 1$, and the flux of mass reverses, so that cold gas evaporates. How more efficient cooling changes this has not yet been explored. Streams, which share much with mixing layers, become unstable above the critical Mach number of eq. 1 through body modes (2.3.6). [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019), also for $\mathrm{Da} < 1$, and more recently [Das and Gronke](#das-gronke-2023) (2023) studied magnetic fields in radiative mixing layers. The fields grow to equipartition and, depending on their orientation, can strongly suppress the flux of mass from the hot to the cold medium. They do so above all for fast cooling, $\mathrm{Da} > 1$, where mixing is the bottleneck.

#### 2.2.5 Turbulent mixing layers after Begelman and Fabian

[Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990, [1990MNRAS.244P..26B](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/1990MNRAS.244P..26B/abstract)) wrote a short communication from the Joint Institute for Laboratory Astrophysics in Boulder and the Institute of Astronomy in Cambridge, accepted on 26 March 1990. Cold clouds in hot turbulent gas, common in the ISM and the ICM, grow mixing layers whose column density cooling limits and whose mean temperature the flux of momentum sets (summary). The ISM is turbulent, so the varying momentum flux tears the surface layers of embedded clouds, and the ICM, with turbulent velocities of $\gtrsim 100\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, should grow thicker layers (§1). Even a quiescent hot phase makes turbulent boundary layers wherever the phases move against each other.

Hot gas of density $\rho_\mathrm{h}$ and temperature $T_\mathrm{h}$ meets cold gas of $\rho_\mathrm{c}$ and $T_\mathrm{c}$ in pressure balance at a plane interface ([Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) 1990, §2). Instabilities driven by the turbulence intermingle the two in a layer of thickness $l$, and the mixing cascades to ever smaller scales until the phases share energy on a molecular level. They form a third phase at an intermediate temperature $\bar T$. The stretched surface of the cold phase grows so much that energy diffuses quickly between the phases even when conduction is suppressed by large factors, as tangled magnetic fields in turbulent gas would suppress it (footnote). Without radiative losses the layer would thicken indefinitely. In a steady state the intermediate gas cools and rejoins the cold phase. The layer is thus a medium of three phases: a porous, convoluted web of cold filaments or sheets, lifted from the interface and broken down by the turbulent hot gas that percolates through them, and the intermediate gas, which contains all temperatures between $\bar T$ and $T_\mathrm{c}$, but in which gas near $\bar T$ dominates the cooling.

The largest eddies of hot gas, of size $l_\mathrm{t}$ and velocity $v_\mathrm{t}$, deposit a fraction $\eta_\mathrm{h}$ of their mass and energy in the layer, so that hot gas enters at $\dot m_\mathrm{h} \sim \eta_\mathrm{h} \rho_\mathrm{h} v_\mathrm{t}$ per unit area, carrying the enthalpy flux $\frac{5}{2}\eta_\mathrm{h} p v_\mathrm{t}$ at the pressure $p$ ([Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) 1990, §2). They drive the KH instability on all scales $l_\mathrm{c} \le l_\mathrm{t}$, which pulls cold gas into the layer at $\eta_\mathrm{c} \rho_\mathrm{c} l_\mathrm{c}/t_\mathrm{KH}(l_\mathrm{c})$, with $t_\mathrm{KH}(l_\mathrm{c}) \sim (\rho_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{h})^{1/2} l_\mathrm{c}/v_\mathrm{t}$ and an efficiency $\eta_\mathrm{c} \le 1$. Hence $\dot m_\mathrm{c} \sim \eta_\mathrm{c} (\rho_\mathrm{c}\rho_\mathrm{h})^{1/2} v_\mathrm{t}$ per unit area, whatever the scale $l_\mathrm{c}$. The mixed gas, before it cools, has the temperature $\bar T = (\dot m_\mathrm{h} T_\mathrm{h} + \dot m_\mathrm{c} T_\mathrm{c})/(\dot m_\mathrm{h} + \dot m_\mathrm{c}) \equiv \xi\,(T_\mathrm{c} T_\mathrm{h})^{1/2}$, with $\xi = [\eta_\mathrm{h} + \eta_\mathrm{c}(T_\mathrm{c}/T_\mathrm{h})^{1/2}]/[\eta_\mathrm{c} + \eta_\mathrm{h}(T_\mathrm{c}/T_\mathrm{h})^{1/2}]$ (eq. 1). It depends on the details of mixing and cooling only through the efficiencies, and for efficiencies of comparable size $\xi$ is of order unity and $\bar T$ near the geometric mean. In the steady state the cooling of the intermediate gas balances the enthalpy brought in, $l f_\mathrm{i} \sim \eta_\mathrm{h} v_\mathrm{t}\,t_\mathrm{cool}(\bar T)$ (eq. 2), with the volume filling factor $f_\mathrm{i}$ of the intermediate phase. The thickness $l$ is thus not fixed but varies inversely with $f_\mathrm{i}$. If mixing took the time an eddy needs to leave its energy behind, $t_\mathrm{mix} \sim \eta_\mathrm{h}^{-1} l/v_\mathrm{t}$, then $f_\mathrm{i} \sim t_\mathrm{cool}(\bar T)/t_\mathrm{mix}$, but the authors find this choice not compelling and leave the dimensionless parameters to numerical experiments. The observable properties depend mainly on $\bar T$, $p$ and $v_\mathrm{t}$, not on $l$ or $f_\mathrm{i}$ separately. The review describes $l_\mathrm{t}$ as the full thickness of the layer, which in the paper is $l$ (2.2.2).

The layer radiates about $\frac{5}{2}\eta_\mathrm{h} p v_\mathrm{t}$ per unit area, with $\eta_\mathrm{h}$ of order $l/l_\mathrm{t}$, near unity when the largest eddies are as large as the layer is thick ([Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) 1990, §3). The spectrum is that of gas at $\bar T$, about $10^{5.5}$ K in the centre of a cluster with $T_\mathrm{h} \sim 10^7$ K and $T_\mathrm{c} \sim 10^4$ K, and rich in extreme-ultraviolet lines that the cold phase absorbs and turns into optical and ultraviolet lines. Treating the emission as bremsstrahlung at $\bar T$ that irradiates a cold slab, computed with Cloudy, and taking $v_\mathrm{t} \sim 100\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ from the widths of lines, the authors find surface emissivities of up to about $1.3 \times 10^{41}\,\mathrm{erg\,s^{-1}\,kpc^{-2}}$ for $p/k = 10^7\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}\,K}$. That suffices for the luminosities and sizes of the optical filaments of cooling flows, and the mixing draws on a power of order $p v_\mathrm{t}$ rather than $p v_\mathrm{f}$, with the velocity $v_\mathrm{f}$ of the cooling flow. In the ISM, with lower pressures and turbulent velocities of $\lesssim 30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, mixing layers are best seen in the ultraviolet absorption lines of highly ionised species. For $T_\mathrm{h} = 10^6$ K and $T_\mathrm{c} = 10^4$ K, $\bar T \sim 10^5 \xi$ K, and O VI is a major constituent of the layer for $10^{5.5} \lesssim \bar T \lesssim 10^{5.7}$ K. The column density of a layer is $N \approx 2.7 \times 10^{17}\,\eta_\mathrm{h} \bar T_5 \Lambda_{-21.3}^{-1} v_{6.5}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$ (eqs. 3, 4), with $\bar T = 10^5 \bar T_5$ K, $\Lambda = 10^{-21.3}\Lambda_{-21.3}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}}$ and $v_\mathrm{t} = 10^{6.5} v_{6.5}\,\mathrm{cm\,s^{-1}}$. With an oxygen abundance of $8 \times 10^{-4}$, the column of O VI becomes $2 \times 10^{14} f_\mathrm{O\,VI}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$, with the fraction $f_\mathrm{O\,VI}$ of oxygen in that ion, which matches the observed values for $\xi \sim 2$, that is $\bar T \sim 10^{5.3}$ K. **[GK]** The paper writes the range of O VI as $5 \gtrsim \xi \gtrsim 2$, but $10^{5.5}$ to $10^{5.7}$ K corresponds to $\xi$ from about 3.2 to 5. The matching value $\xi \sim 2$ lies below that range.

The authors name three caveats ([Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) 1990, §4). The intermediate gas may be out of ionisation equilibrium, since mixing heats and radiation cools it quickly. The plane-parallel treatment needs clouds much larger than $l$: for smaller clouds geometry may change the mixing, ablation may dominate over cooling, and the clouds may tap smaller scales of the turbulence and so grow thinner layers. Magnetic fields complicate the details. While their pressure stays below $p$ the argument should survive roughly, but an Alfvén speed above $v_\mathrm{t}$ may change the geometry of the mixing. Mixing layers in the ISM should make warm gas and columns of O VI like those observed. A mixing layer may also surround a whole galaxy where it meets hotter halo gas, stirred by the Galactic fountain, by the rotation of the galaxy relative to its halo or by its motion through the intergalactic medium. The authors see mixing layers as common throughout astrophysics, among others as an alternative to conduction for the exchange of mass in old supernova remnants ([McKee and Ostriker](#mckee-ostriker-1977) 1977).

#### 2.2.6 Radiative mixing layers in three dimensions

[Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019, [arXiv:1809.09101](https://arxiv.org/abs/1809.09101)) wrote from the University of California at Santa Barbara, Ji also from TAPIR at the California Institute of Technology. Their simulations of radiative turbulent mixing layers in three dimensions, with MHD, non-equilibrium ionisation (NEI) and photoionisation, test the simple analytic models, motivated by the high ions such as O VI seen in high-velocity clouds and the CGM (abstract, §1). A cooling flow through O VI temperatures would need about $30\,M_\odot\,\mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ ([McQuinn and Werk](#mcquinn-werk-2018) 2018), far above the star formation of the host galaxies, unless cold gas is recycled to high temperatures quickly. Mixing layers do that. In steady state, with $l/v_\mathrm{t} \sim t_\mathrm{cool}$, a layer of thickness $l \sim v_\mathrm{t} t_\mathrm{cool}$ should give $N_\mathrm{O\,VI} \sim 8 \times 10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}\,\eta_{0.1} v_2$ (eq. 2), with $\eta = 0.1\,\eta_{0.1}$ and $v = 100\,v_2\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, independent of density and metallicity, since $t_\mathrm{cool} \propto 1/(nZ)$ near $10^{5.5}$ K. That is 10 to 100 times below the observed columns. Earlier simulations, in two dimensions by Kwak and collaborators ([Kwak and Shelton](#kwak-shelton-2010) 2010, among others) and in three by [Esquivel et al.](#esquivel-et-al-2006) (2006), were not compared with analytic models, and the latter did not run long enough to reach equilibrium. The authors' eq. 1, the mean temperature of [Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990), writes $\dot m_\mathrm{hot}$ twice in the denominator where $\dot m_\mathrm{hot} + \dot m_\mathrm{cold}$ is meant.

The set-up places cold gas of $10^4$ K and $n = 1.6 \times 10^{-2}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ above hot gas of $10^6$ K and $1.6 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$, which moves at $100\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, a Mach number of 0.69 in the hot gas ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §2, Table 1). The domain is $100 \times 100 \times 300$ pc on $128 \times 128 \times 384$ cells, periodic along the interface and open across it (3.4). The variants, named by elements such as Z0.1 or Bx-lgr, change the metallicity to $0.1\,Z_\odot$, the normalisation of the cooling function by factors of 0, 0.1 and 10, the pressure by a factor of 10, the shear to 50 and $200\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, the density contrast from 100 to 1000, the box, the wavelength of the perturbation and the resolution, and add magnetic fields of $0.035$ or $0.35\,\mu$G, with $\beta = 430$ or 4.3, NEI and photoionisation (Table 2).

In purely hydrodynamic simulations in collisional ionisation equilibrium (CIE), the columns settle at about $10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$ in C IV and O VI and $10^{11}$ in Si IV, so that eq. 2 holds within an order of magnitude for $\eta \sim 0.1$ ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.1, Fig. 2). Its scalings fail, however. The columns grow by an order of magnitude from $0.1\,Z_\odot$ to $Z_\odot$, fall only threefold when the cooling function is ten times stronger, fall by a factor of a few when the pressure drops tenfold, depend only weakly on the shear, and do not depend on the density contrast in steady state (Figs. 3–6). The main reason is that the turbulent velocities are one to two orders of magnitude below the shear velocity, not of order it as eq. 2 assumes: the hot gas slows as it entrains cold gas, radiative shocks dissipate motions that are supersonic in the cool gas, and the transverse velocities are only about $5\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ at solar metallicity.

An analysis of orders of magnitude, with Favre averages and the Reynolds stress after [Tennekes and Lumley](#tennekes-lumley-1972) (1972) and [White et al.](#white-et-al-2015) (2015), gives the scales of a thin layer, of thickness $h$ much smaller than the length $L$ over which the flow changes ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.1.1). Continuity gives $\tilde v_z \sim (h/L)\,\tilde v_x$ (eq. 16), the streamwise momentum $v_\mathrm{t}^2 \sim (h/L)\,v_x^2$ (eq. 18), and the transverse momentum a constant sum of thermal and turbulent pressure, $\bar P + \overline{\rho v_z'^2}$ (eq. 20). The equations are not closed, and the entrainment velocity far from the layer is a missing boundary condition, which analytic models of jets take from experiments on adiabatic layers. The paper's text names the two scales the other way round, and writes $y$ for the coordinate across the layer in eq. 20, in its profiles and in its conclusions, where §2 calls it $z$.

Three terms can offset the radiative losses: the advection of enthalpy, compression and turbulent dissipation ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.1.2, Fig. 7). The emissivity is much lower and flatter in temperature than for single-phase gas cooling at constant pressure, and grows only threefold for a tenfold stronger cooling function. The gas stays highly multiphase across the layer, and a skin of mixed gas around finger-like structures dominates the emission. Enthalpy flux and dissipation balance the cooling above a critical temperature $T_\mathrm{crit}$, below which the gas cools passively, and $T_\mathrm{crit}$ rises with stronger cooling, as does the turbulence, which the drop of thermal pressure in cooling gas drives like a piston. The shrinking of the cooling column, communicated at about the sound speed at $T_\mathrm{crit}$, sets the entrainment of hot gas. Energy balance, $\tilde v_z^\mathrm{hot}\,\frac{5}{2} P (1 + \mathcal{M}^2) \approx \Sigma_\mathrm{L} = \int_0^h n^2 \Lambda\,dz$ (eq. 22), with the fitted surface brightness $\Sigma_\mathrm{L} \approx 6 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{erg\,s^{-1}\,cm^{-2}}\,v_{100}^{1/4} (\Lambda_{-21.4}^{T_\mathrm{mix}})^{1/2} P_{-13.7}^{3/2}$ at solar metallicity (eq. 23), gives the inflow $\tilde v_z^\mathrm{hot} \sim 8\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}\,v_{100}^{1/4} (\Lambda_{-21.4}^{T_\mathrm{mix}})^{1/2} P_{-13.7}^{1/2} [(1 + \mathcal{M}^2)/1.5]^{-1}$ (eq. 24). Here $v_{100} = v_x/(100\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})$, $\Lambda_{-21.4}^{T_\mathrm{mix}} = \Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})/(4 \times 10^{-22}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}})$ at $T_\mathrm{mix} \sim 10^5$ K, and $P_{-13.7} = P/(2.2 \times 10^{-14}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^{-3}})$, or $P/k_\mathrm{B} = 145\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}\,K}$. The inflow is about the sound speed at $T_\mathrm{crit} \sim 1.5 \times 10^4$ K and independent of the density contrast and nearly of the shear, so cooling, not the KH instability, drives it, with $\tilde v_z^\mathrm{hot} \propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$. The ratio $\alpha \sim \tilde v_z/\tilde v_x \sim h/L \sim c_\mathrm{s}(T_\mathrm{crit})/\tilde v_x$, about 0.05, sets width and column (eq. 25). Cold gas still enters through the KH instability, at $\dot m_\mathrm{hot}/\dot m_\mathrm{cold} \sim (\rho_\mathrm{hot}/\rho_\mathrm{cold})^{1/2}$ (eq. 26), so that hot gas dominates the enthalpy brought in and cold gas the mass. The width $h \sim \Sigma_\mathrm{L}/\langle n_\mathrm{e} n_\mathrm{i} \Lambda \rangle \sim 50\,\mathrm{pc}\,(\Lambda_{-21.4}^{T_\mathrm{mix}})^{-1/2} P_{-13.7}^{-1/2}$ (eq. 27) grows as $t_\mathrm{cool}^{1/2}$, not as $t_\mathrm{cool}$, and exceeds $\tilde v_z^\mathrm{hot} t_\mathrm{cool}(T_\mathrm{mix})$, since the mixed gas fills little volume. **[GK]** With $P = 2.2 \times 10^{-14}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^{-3}}$ and $1 + \mathcal{M}^2 = 1.5$, eqs. 22 and 23 give $7.3\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, consistent with eq. 24.

The shear velocity follows the sound speed across the layer at a nearly constant Mach number of about 0.6, even where both vary tenfold, which points to efficient dissipation of bulk motion and a constant Prandtl number ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.1.2, Fig. 9). The ansatz $\tilde v_x \propto c_\mathrm{s} \propto T^{1/2}$ closes the equations. In mixing-length theory, turbulent diffusion of entropy balances cooling, $\nabla \cdot [\kappa_\mathrm{mix} \rho T \nabla s] = n_\mathrm{e} n_\mathrm{i} \Lambda(T)$ (eq. 28), with $\kappa_\mathrm{mix} \sim \Sigma_\mathrm{L} h/(\frac{5}{2} P) \sim 10^{26}\,\mathrm{cm^2\,s^{-1}}$ (eq. 29), but the single-phase picture behind it is poor. The columns of O VI follow $N_\mathrm{O\,VI} \approx 3 \times 10^{12}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}\,(Z/Z_\odot)^{0.8}\,v_{100}^{1/4} P_{-13.7}^{1/2}$ (eq. 30), and those of C IV, O VI and Si IV scale with the cooling function as $\Lambda^{-0.45}$ to $\Lambda^{-0.55}$, in line with $N \propto h$.

Magnetic fields act as in the adiabatic KH instability ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.2; [Ryu, Jones and Frank](#ryu-et-al-2000) 2000). Fields along the flow grow to equipartition with the turbulence, most strongly at the interface, until magnetic tension makes the flow laminar and suppresses mixing. With the weak field this happens after about 20 Myr, the field growing tenfold, and with the strong field from the start (Fig. 11). The columns grow at first, then decay to about their initial values, so that the layer no longer adds to them (Fig. 12). Transverse fields, which leave adiabatic layers hydrodynamic, also end the mixing once cooling is on, probably because small-scale motions from cooling tilt the field into components that are then amplified. In short, fields shut down turbulent mixing whatever their initial strength and geometry, and magnetic pressure replaces turbulent pressure in the cooling gas.

Photoionisation by the background affects the layer mainly by lowering the cooling of overionised gas, which matters little at solar metallicity, within a factor of 2 in the columns, but raises O VI fivefold at $0.1\,Z_\odot$ with MHD, where hydrogen and helium dominate the cooling at a few $10^4$ K ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §3.4, Fig. 15). NEI raises the columns by factors of 1.5 to 3, also with fields and photoionisation, because the ions shuttle between warm and hot gas faster than they ionise or recombine (§3.5, Figs. 16, 17). The layers thus contribute to the columns even when fields suppress the mixing. The suppression of cooling by NEI, gauged with the curves of [Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) (2007) for time-dependent isobaric cooling, changes little at solar metallicity (Fig. 18).

The authors conclude that the analytic scalings fail, that cooling seeds the turbulence and drives the entrainment of hot gas, that fields suppress mixing, and that photoionisation and NEI change the columns by factors of a few ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019, §4). A sightline must pierce hundreds or thousands of mixing layers to explain the observations, which a fog of tiny cloudlets of size $(c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool})_\mathrm{c} \sim 0.1\,\mathrm{pc}\,(n/\mathrm{cm^{-3}})^{-1}$ could provide, with covering fractions $f_A \sim (D/l_\mathrm{cloudlet}) f_V$ of $10^2$ to $10^3$ for $D/l_\mathrm{cloudlet} \sim 10^5$ to $10^6$ and a volume filling fraction $f_V \sim 10^{-3}$ ([McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) 2018; eq. 31). Anisotropic conduction and cosmic rays, with their Field length and their own scale, could alter the layer and remain to be studied.

#### 2.2.7 The fractal cooling layer

[Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020, [arXiv:2003.08390](https://arxiv.org/abs/2003.08390)), of the Center for Computational Astrophysics of the Flatiron Institute, Princeton University and Columbia University, submitted their letter to ApJ Letters in March 2020. Cold gas moving through hot gas grows a turbulent mixing layer through the KH instability, and the intermediate gas it makes cools fast (abstract). The advection of hot, high-enthalpy gas balances the losses, so the cold phase grows and is accelerated. The cooling happens in a thin, corrugated sheet whose area has the fractal dimension $D = 5/2$ and whose thickness adjusts so that the time of mixing of the hot gas equals the cooling time. [Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990) assumed an energy flux of about $P v_\mathrm{turb}$, [El-Badry et al.](#el-badry-et-al-2019) (2019) found $P(\kappa_\mathrm{diff}/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$ for diffusive interfaces, with an effective diffusivity $\kappa_\mathrm{diff}$, and simulations found growth at a rate $\propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$ when the mixed gas cools faster than it mixes ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, [2020a](#gronke-oh-2020a); [Mandelker et al.](#mandelker-et-al-2020) 2020), but a complete model was missing (§1).

Three numbers govern the layer: $\xi = t_\mathrm{sh}/t_\mathrm{cool} = L/(v_\mathrm{rel} t_\mathrm{cool})$, $\chi = \rho_\mathrm{cold}/\rho_\mathrm{hot}$ and $\mathcal{M} = v_\mathrm{rel}/c_\mathrm{s,hot}$ (eq. 1), with the relative velocity $v_\mathrm{rel}$, the streamwise length $L$, the shear time $t_\mathrm{sh}$ and the minimum cooling time $t_\mathrm{cool}$, at intermediate temperatures ([Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020, §2). In a quasi-steady state, hot gas flows into the cooling layer at $v_\mathrm{in}$, which sets the cooling, the transfer of mass and the transfer of momentum together: $\dot E_\mathrm{cool} \approx (E_\mathrm{th} + P) L^2 v_\mathrm{in}$, $\dot M \approx \rho_\mathrm{hot} L^2 v_\mathrm{in}$ and $\dot p \approx \rho_\mathrm{hot} v_\mathrm{rel} L^2 v_\mathrm{in}$ (eq. 2). The balance of advected enthalpy and isobaric cooling in a sheet of thickness $w$ and area $A_w \gg L^2$ gives $v_\mathrm{in}/v_\mathrm{rel} = \xi\,(w/L)(A_w/L^2)$ (eq. 3). Subsonic turbulence carries equal energy densities in both phases, so the hot gas moves faster, $v_\mathrm{turb,hot} = \chi^{1/2} v_\mathrm{turb}$, where $v_\mathrm{turb}$ is that of the cold gas. A Kolmogorov cascade, $v_\mathrm{turb}(\ell) = v_\mathrm{turb,L}(\ell/L)^{1/3}$, then fixes the thickness at which the hot gas mixes in as fast as it cools, $w/L = \xi^{-3/2} \chi^{3/4} (v_\mathrm{turb,L}/v_\mathrm{rel})^{3/2}$ (eq. 4). The turbulence draws only on the shear, since the thermal free energy is inaccessible in subsonic flow, so $f_\mathrm{turb} \equiv v_\mathrm{turb}/v_\mathrm{rel}$ is about 0.1 to 0.2. A fractal surface has an area that grows towards small scales, $A_\lambda/L^2 = (L/\lambda)^{D-2}$ at the scale of measurement $\lambda$. By analogy with the Koch surface, with Brownian surfaces and with isocontours in compressive turbulence, the authors adopt $D = 5/2$, so $A_w/L^2 = (w/L)^{-1/2}$ (eq. 5). Together these give $v_\mathrm{in}/v_\mathrm{rel} = \chi^{3/8} \xi^{1/4} f_\mathrm{turb}^{3/4}$ (eq. 6), and for turbulence driven in any way $v_\mathrm{in} = C\,\chi^{3/8} (L_\mathrm{turb}/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/4} v_\mathrm{turb,L}^{3/4}$ (eq. 7), with the outer scale $L_\mathrm{turb}$ and a constant $C$ that depends on geometry and driving. **[GK]** Eqs. 3 to 5 give eq. 6 directly: $\xi\,(w/L)^{1/2} = \xi\,(\xi^{-3/2}\chi^{3/4}f_\mathrm{turb}^{3/2})^{1/2}$.

The simulations confirm the picture ([Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020, §4). The turbulence broadens the profile of the shear velocity, but the cooling keeps the gradients of temperature and density sharp, in a thin corrugated sheet with a net inflow of hot gas (Fig. 1). The cooling is isobaric: the pressure shows no dip where it cools fastest, and its fluctuations follow the turbulence, $\Delta P/P \propto \mathcal{M}_\mathrm{turb}^2$, so that turbulent mixing limits the cooling and the transfer of mass and momentum, and cooling does not drive the turbulence when the layer is resolved. The thermal and momentum layers part: the temperature profile steepens with $\xi$ while the velocity profile hardly changes (Fig. 2). Blurring the isosurface of minimum cooling time on scales $\lambda$ gives $A_\lambda \propto \lambda^{-1/2}$, the predicted $D = 5/2$, and the flux of thermal energy stays constant through hotter isosurfaces and drops sharply below $T_\mathrm{mix}$ (Fig. 3). The measured inflow, cooling, mass and momentum fluxes agree with each other and with eq. 7 for the measured turbulent velocity, over nearly 100 simulations with $C (L_\mathrm{turb}/L)^{1/4} = 0.15$ (Fig. 4). The mean cooling between 20 and $40\,t_\mathrm{sh}$ follows $\dot E_\mathrm{cool} = 0.04\,(\frac{5}{2} P L^2)\,\chi^{3/8} (L/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/4} v_\mathrm{rel}^{3/4}$ for $\xi > 1$, over four orders of magnitude in $\xi$, while slowly cooling layers have not yet reached equilibrium and would in reality be disrupted first.

The authors read the results of others in this light ([Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020, §5). [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019) averaged horizontally, which mixes cooling and inert gas, and treated the cooling volume as a flat sheet, which misses the area of the fractal surface and leads to their other scaling with $t_\mathrm{cool}$ (2.2.6). They took pressure dips for a sign of rapid cooling, where Fielding et al. see a sign of insufficient resolution (2.6.3). Clouds that grow in radiative cloud crushing do so at an inflow $\propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$ ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, [2020a](#gronke-oh-2020a)), as do shear flows with cooling ([Mandelker et al.](#mandelker-et-al-2020) 2020). These studies ascribe the inflow to pressure gradients from cooling, but in resolved simulations the cooling is isobaric and the turbulence drives the inflow, with hints of the dependences on $v_\mathrm{rel}^{3/4}$ and $\chi^{3/8}$ in their results too. The set-up is microscopic and cannot follow the expansion or destruction of the cold phase, assumes a laminar hot background, and leaves out magnetic fields, viscosity and conduction. The authors take heart that growth hardly depended on the field in [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a) and that condensation occurred with appreciable conduction in [Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) (2016). Growth and entrainment of the cold phase should thus be fastest at high relative velocity, high density contrast and fast cooling.

#### 2.2.8 Mixing layers as turbulent combustion

[Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021, [arXiv:2008.12302](https://arxiv.org/abs/2008.12302)) wrote from the University of California at Santa Barbara, Gronke also from the Johns Hopkins University as a Hubble fellow. They name three puzzles of radiative mixing layers (§1). The inflow found in clouds in winds, $v_\mathrm{in} \approx 0.2\,c_\mathrm{s,cold}\,(t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{sc,cold})^{-1/4}$, with the sound-crossing time $t_\mathrm{sc,cold} = L/c_\mathrm{s,cold}$ ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a; eq. 1), contains neither the shear nor the density contrast, and disagrees with the $t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$ of [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019). The hot gas delivers its enthalpy at about $c_\mathrm{s,cold}$, far below the $c_\mathrm{s,hot}$ that saturated conduction would reach. And the inflow is robust to resolution, although fronts are thought to need explicit conduction and a Field length resolved by at least four cells ([Koyama and Inutsuka](#koyama-inutsuka-2004) 2004), and although the fractal cooling surface of [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) should make the cooling volume grow with resolution. Plane mixing layers at high resolution even agree with whole clouds that are far less resolved. The authors turn to turbulent combustion, with hot gas as the fuel, cold gas as the oxidiser and more cold gas as the ash, a field rich in theory and experiment.

In one dimension a front between the stable phases is steady when $j_x = \rho v_x$ and $P + \rho v_x^2$ are constant and $\kappa\,d^2T/dx^2 = j_x c_p\,dT/dx + \rho\mathcal{L}(T)$ (eqs. 9–11), with the conductivity $\kappa$, the specific heat $c_p$ at constant pressure and the net cooling $\rho\mathcal{L}$ ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §3). The mass flux is an eigenvalue fixed by the boundary temperatures, $j_x = Q/[c_p (T_2 - T_1)]$ with the surface brightness $Q = -\int \rho\mathcal{L}\,dx$ (eqs. 12, 13), so it depends on the structure of the front, and its sign, condensation or evaporation, on the pressure. Conduction balances cooling over most of the front, and advection matters little, so that the Field length $\lambda_\mathrm{F} = [\kappa T/(n^2\Lambda)]^{1/2}$ is the relevant scale (eq. 14; [Kim and Kim](#kim-kim-2013) 2013). The front of the fiducial case draws in hot gas at $2\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, with $j_x, Q \propto (\kappa/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$. Since $Q \approx \int \rho\mathcal{L}\,\lambda_\mathrm{F}\,d\ln T \propto (\Lambda\kappa/T)^{1/2}$ (eq. 15) and $\lambda_\mathrm{F} \propto (T^{3+n}/\Lambda)^{1/2}$ at fixed pressure for $\kappa \propto T^n$ (eq. 16), the surface brightness draws on a broad range of temperatures, above $10^5$ K for Spitzer conduction, although the emissivity per volume peaks near $10^4$ K (Fig. 4). What its resolution demands is set out in 2.6.4.

In combustion, a flame burns at the laminar speed $S_\mathrm{L} \sim (D_\mathrm{L}/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$ for a diffusion coefficient $D_\mathrm{L}$, which numerical diffusion, $D \sim v\,\Delta x$, makes $\propto (\Delta x)^{1/2}$ ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §4.1). Turbulence wrinkles the flame and multiplies its area, so the front moves at a turbulent speed $S_\mathrm{T} \gg S_\mathrm{L}$, the inflow $v_\mathrm{in}$. The Damköhler number $\mathrm{Da} = \tau_\mathrm{turb}/t_\mathrm{cool} = L/(u' t_\mathrm{cool})$, with the turbulent velocity $u'$ at the outer scale $L$ (eq. 17), rises across a mixing layer as the gas cools (§4.2). Near the hot side, $\mathrm{Da} < 1$, the gas is mixed before it cools, a well-stirred reactor, and with the turbulent diffusivity $D_\mathrm{turb} \sim u' L$ the front moves at $S_\mathrm{T} \approx (u' L/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$ (eq. 18). Nearer the cold side, $\mathrm{Da} > 1$, the gas cools before it is mixed, in corrugated flamelets, and fragments into a multiphase medium. The flame then moves at $S_\mathrm{T} \approx S_\mathrm{L} A_\mathrm{T}/A_\mathrm{L}$ (eq. 19; [Damköhler](#damkohler-1940) 1940), with the turbulent and laminar flame areas $A_\mathrm{T}$ and $A_\mathrm{L}$. Fits give $A_\mathrm{T}/A_\mathrm{L} \approx (u'/S_\mathrm{L})^n$ (eq. 20), and Damköhler's $n = 1$, with wide experimental support, gives $S_\mathrm{T} \approx u'$ (eq. 21), independent of $S_\mathrm{L}$ and so of resolution ([Kuo and Acharya](#kuo-acharya-2012) 2012). Near the Klimov–Williams line, where the Karlovitz number of diffusive and Kolmogorov scales is about one, as in codes where both equal the cell, [Gülder](#gulder-1991) (1991) found $S_\mathrm{T}/u' = \mathrm{Da}^{1/4}$ (eq. 23). This is the scaling of eq. 1 for $u' \approx c_\mathrm{s,cold}$, now backed by experiments.

The authors justify the scaling by an effective cooling time ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §4.4). In the multiphase regime, cold gas diffuses as a scalar over $\lambda_\mathrm{cool} \sim (L u' t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$ in a cooling time (eq. 24), so only that fraction of an eddy mixes and cools each time. The eddy takes $\tilde\tau_\mathrm{cool} \sim (L t_\mathrm{cool}/u')^{1/2}$ (eq. 25), the geometric mean of the turnover and cooling times, to cool entirely, as an absorbing and scattering medium has an effective optical depth $(\tau_\mathrm{a}\tau_\mathrm{s})^{1/2}$, and as the Field length is the geometric mean of the elastic and inelastic mean free paths of an electron (eq. 27). Hence $S_\mathrm{T} \sim (D_\mathrm{turb}/\tilde\tau_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2} \sim u'\,\mathrm{Da}^{1/4}$ (eq. 28), an effective emissivity $\tilde\epsilon \sim P/\tilde\tau_\mathrm{cool} \propto u'^{1/2} t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$ (eq. 29) and a width of the multiphase region $h \propto L\,(u' t_\mathrm{cool}/L)^{1/4}$ (eq. 30). With the fractal area $A_\mathrm{T}/A_\mathrm{L} = (\lambda_\mathrm{G}/L)^{2-D}$ down to the Gibson scale $\lambda_\mathrm{G}$, where the eddies move at $S_\mathrm{L}$, a Kolmogorov cascade gives $n = 3(D - 2)$ in eq. 20 (eqs. 31, 32). The experimental $D = 2.35$ gives $n = 1.05$, Damköhler's value, and the $D = 2.5$ of [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) gives $n = 1.5$, though fractal arguments have not proved robust in combustion. The turbulence of the KH instability, $u' \sim v_\mathrm{shear}/\chi^{1/2} \sim \mathcal{M}_\mathrm{hot} c_\mathrm{s,cold}$, explains why the inflow is of order $c_\mathrm{s,cold}$ for transonic shear, as in the CGM (§4.6).
