The inflow does not depend on resolution, because the cascade from the outer scale limits the rate at which gas reaches the diffusive scales, where it then mixes and cools at once, much as stirring, not molecular diffusion, sets how fast cream mixes into coffee. **[GK]** Eq. 25 in eq. 28 gives $(u' L)^{1/2} (u'/(L t_\mathrm{cool}))^{1/4} = u' (L/(u' t_\mathrm{cool}))^{1/4}$, as stated, and $Q \sim P S_\mathrm{T} \sim \tilde\epsilon h$ gives eq. 30.

The three-dimensional shear layers bear the model out ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §5, Figs. 6–12). The Damköhler number rising to one marks where the gas turns multiphase. With weak cooling much of the emission comes from the single-phase part, while with strong cooling all of it comes from the multiphase part, where the mean temperature follows the cold fraction, $\bar T \approx f_\mathrm{cold} T_\mathrm{cold} + (1 - f_\mathrm{cold}) T_\mathrm{hot}$, and the temperatures split into two peaks near $10^4$ and $10^6$ K. The turbulence is nearly isotropic, follows the shear, $u' \approx l\,\nabla v_y$, and holds the pressure within 10 per cent. The emissivity has a nearly Gaussian profile, as for a randomly walking front, peaks where the cold fraction is one half, and with strong cooling tracks the turbulence. The surface brightness scales as $t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$ and $u'^{1/2}$ for weak cooling, and as $t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$ and $u'^{3/4}$ for strong cooling, turning over where the Damköhler number of gas at $2 \times 10^5$ K is about one, without further dependence on $\chi$ or $\mathcal{M}$. The fits are $Q = Q_0\,(P/160\,k_\mathrm{B}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}\,K})(u'/30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})^{1/2}(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{1/2}(t_\mathrm{cool,min}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{-1/2}$ for slow and $Q = Q_0\,(P/160\,k_\mathrm{B}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}\,K})(u'/30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})^{3/4}(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{1/4}(t_\mathrm{cool,min}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{-1/4}$ for fast cooling, with $Q_0 \approx 8.8 \times 10^{-8}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^{-2}\,s^{-1}}$ and the cooling time at the peak of the cooling curve (eqs. 35–37). The effective emissivity follows eq. 29, with a Gaussian of effective cooling time $\tilde\tau_\mathrm{cool} = 2.5\,\mathrm{Myr}\,(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{1/2}(u'/30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})^{-1/2}(t_\mathrm{cool,min}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{1/2}$ and width $\sigma \sim 16\,\mathrm{pc}\,(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{3/4}(u'/30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})^{1/4}(t_\mathrm{cool,min}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{1/4}$, derived without free parameters (eqs. 38–40). The turbulent velocity of their set-up scales as $u' \propto v_\mathrm{shear}^{4/5}(L/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/5}$ for $\chi \gtrsim 100$ (eq. 42), only weakly with cooling. At low $\chi$ the mixed gas cools so fast that temperature and velocity decouple, as [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) saw, a regime the authors find astrophysically less relevant.

A one-dimensional model of mixing length reproduces the mean profiles ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §5.4). At constant pressure the turbulent heat flux $-D_\mathrm{eddy}\rho T\nabla s$ becomes $-\kappa_\mathrm{turb}\nabla T$ with $\kappa_\mathrm{turb} = D_\mathrm{eddy}\rho c_\mathrm{P}$ (eqs. 44–46), and the steady equation of the laminar front with $\kappa_\mathrm{turb}$ and the Gaussian emissivity, solved by shooting, matches the simulated temperature profiles of strong cooling with $\kappa_\mathrm{turb} = 5 \times 10^6\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^{-1}\,s^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$ (eq. 47, Fig. 13). In adiabatic runs, $\kappa_\mathrm{turb}$ measured from the Reynolds-averaged heat flux is nearly constant and matches $\bar\rho c_\mathrm{P} l u'$ for a mixing length $l = 4$ pc (eqs. 48, 49, Fig. 14), and the Reynolds stress matches $\bar\rho\,u'_x l\,\nabla v_y$ with the same $l$ in adiabatic and cooling runs (eq. 51, Fig. 15), so the turbulent Prandtl number is near one. Isotropic conduction changes nothing until it exceeds the turbulent diffusivity, which for their constant $\kappa$ is of the same order. It then lowers $Q$ towards the laminar speed, which by then is close to the turbulent one, and broadens the interfaces, so that the layer becomes less multiphase (§5.5, Fig. 16). As long as turbulent diffusion dominates the transport of heat, conduction can be ignored, in line with studies of clouds in winds ([Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016; [Armillotta et al.](#armillotta-et-al-2017) 2017; [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) 2020).

The authors read the earlier work as special cases ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §6.1). The $t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/2}$ of [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019) is the weak-cooling regime, and their mixing length failed at strong cooling because the emissivity of the multiphase gas differs. Eq. 1 of [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a) is eq. 28 for $u' \sim c_\mathrm{s,cold}$, with turbulence driven there by the pulsations of the cloud, so that the scaling holds although pressure gradients from cooling drive it. The entrainment does depend on the shear, and $u'$ hardly on cooling, so turbulence, not low pressure from cooling, drives it in shear layers, as [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) argued. Their model, $v_\mathrm{in} = (w/t_\mathrm{cool})(w/L)^{-1/2}$ (eq. 52), would however make the inflow $\propto \Delta^{1/2}$ when $w$ is unresolved, which no simulation shows. Magnetic fields suppress mixing in the shear layers of Ji et al., but not the growth of clouds in Gronke and Oh, probably because the driving of turbulence differs. The fits for the cold gas are $v_\mathrm{in} \approx 11.3\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{turb}^{1/2}(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{1/2}(t_\mathrm{cool,cold}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{-1/2}$ for $\mathrm{Da} < 1$ and $v_\mathrm{in} \approx 9.5\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{turb}^{3/4}(L/100\,\mathrm{pc})^{1/4}(t_\mathrm{cool,cold}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{-1/4}$ for $\mathrm{Da} > 1$, with $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{turb} = u'/c_\mathrm{s,cold}$, and $u' \approx 50\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}\,\mathcal{M}^{4/5}(c_\mathrm{s,c}/15\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}})^{4/5}(t_\mathrm{cool,cold}/0.03\,\mathrm{Myr})^{-0.1}$ for shear layers with $\chi \gtrsim 100$ (§6.2, eqs. 53–55). Open remain models below the grid like those of turbulent combustion, cooling out of ionisation equilibrium, Karlovitz numbers well above one, and the one-dimensional linear eddy model.

#### 2.2.9 Resolved and numerical mixing layers

[Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) (2026, §1, §2) ask when a turbulent radiative mixing layer is resolved. Turbulence of velocity $v_\mathrm{t}(\ell)$ on a scale $\ell$, the square root of the second-order structure function, has an eddy turnover time $t_\mathrm{eddy}(\ell) = \ell/v_\mathrm{t}(\ell)$ and, against a cooling time $t_\mathrm{cool}$, a Damköhler number $\mathrm{Da}(\ell) = t_\mathrm{eddy}(\ell)/t_\mathrm{cool}$, taken at the integral scale $L_\mathrm{int}$ unless stated otherwise (eqs. 1 to 3). A thermal diffusivity $\alpha_T$ brings gas into the cooling layer at $v_\mathrm{diff} \approx (\alpha_T/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$ (eq. 4; 2.2.8). In a steady state, the enthalpy flux through the interface of area $A_\mathrm{int}$ balances the cooling, $\dot E_\mathrm{cool} = \frac{\gamma}{\gamma - 1} P v_\mathrm{diff} A_\mathrm{int}$ (eq. 5). For a turbulent diffusivity $\alpha_T = v_\mathrm{t}(\ell)\ell$, the time to resupply a volume $\ell^3$ by diffusion becomes $t_\mathrm{diff}(\ell) \approx [t_\mathrm{eddy}(\ell) t_\mathrm{cool}]^{1/2}$, the geometric mean of the two times (eqs. 6, 7; Fig. 1). The scale where they meet defines a turbulent Field length, $t_\mathrm{eddy}(\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}) = t_\mathrm{cool}$, by analogy with the Field length of conduction (eq. 8; 2.6.3). It is the scale $w$ of [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020; 2.2.7) and the scale where $\mathrm{Da} = 1$, a cooling length with the sound speed replaced by the turbulent velocity at that scale, below which rapid mixing keeps the gas nearly isothermal. Only where a simulation resolves scales with $t_\mathrm{diff} \ll t_\mathrm{cool}$ does turbulent diffusion compete fairly with cooling and smooth the temperature across the interface, over a width of order $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb} \gg \Delta x$. Where $\Delta x \gg \lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$, numerical diffusion mixes the phases and cooling at once removes the mixed gas, which keeps the interface one cell thin: a numerical mixing layer, not a turbulent one. Even resolved layers mix through numerical diffusion here, since no explicit diffusion is included (footnote 5).

The simulations confirm that the total cooling hardly depends on resolution, with $\dot E_\mathrm{cool} \propto \mathrm{Da}^{1/2}$ that turns to $\mathrm{Da}^{1/4}$ only at $\mathrm{Da} \gtrsim 50$ ([Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) 2026, §5.1, Fig. 5; 2.2.7, 2.2.8). A companion paper ascribes the turn to the ram pressure of the inflowing hot gas, which suppresses $A_\mathrm{int}$. Measured on the grid, $v_\mathrm{diff}$ and $A_\mathrm{int}(\Delta x)$ reproduce the cooling through eq. 5 across three decades in $t_\mathrm{cool}$, a factor of 8 in resolution and about 30 in $\chi$, with one calibrated length of $1.2\,\Delta x$ (Fig. 6). Both depend on resolution, in opposite directions, as [Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2024) (2024) proposed: $v_\mathrm{diff} \propto N_\mathrm{res}^{-1/2}$ and $A_\mathrm{int}(\Delta x) \propto N_\mathrm{res}^{1/2}$, so that their product stays fixed (§5.2, Fig. 7). The area follows a fractal model, $A_\mathrm{int}(\ell) \approx L_\mathrm{box}^2(\ell/L_\mathrm{box})^{-d}$, with an excess fractal dimension $d = 1/2$ whose origin is unclear (eq. 33; 2.2.7). The diffusion is numerical. Entropy acts nearly as a passive scalar, for which a Godunov scheme diffuses with $\alpha_\mathrm{num} \approx v_\mathrm{t}(L)\Delta x$ (eq. 34), and the measured $v_\mathrm{diff}$ follows $0.1\,[v_\mathrm{t}(L_\mathrm{int})\Delta x/t_\mathrm{cool,min}]^{1/2}$ over two decades (eq. 35, Fig. 8). The converged total cooling may thus be a coincidence of two numerical artefacts, numerical diffusivity and numerical viscosity, rather than a sign of resolved physics. Telling the two apart needs other methods, such as moving meshes, or an understanding of $d$, for which even scalar mixing without cooling quotes values from 1/3 to 2/3. At a low density contrast, $\chi = 10$ with fast cooling, the inflow suppresses the folding of the interface, $A_\mathrm{int}(\Delta x)$ stays at its minimum $L_\mathrm{box}^2$, and the total cooling then depends on resolution (Appendix A).

Resolved layers keep a physical dip of thermal pressure at intermediate temperatures, supported by vertical turbulent stress ([Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) 2026, §5.5, Appendix C). Averaging the vertical momentum equation gives $\mathrm{d}(\langle P\rangle + R_{zz} + \langle\rho v_z\rangle\langle v_z\rangle)/\mathrm{d}z = 0$, with the Reynolds stress $R_{zz} = \langle\delta v_z\,\delta(\rho v_z)\rangle$, as [Sharma et al.](#sharma-et-al-2025) (2025) showed (eq. 36; 2.6.3). Underresolved runs have too little $R_{zz}$, and their gas at intermediate temperatures is therefore overpressured. Fast cooling, with a larger inflow and more turbulent support, deepens the dips. Below the peak temperature, the gas follows the distribution of an isobaric cooling flow of constant mass flux, $\mathrm{d}V/\mathrm{d}\log T \propto T/(\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}x)$, fixed by the cooling function (eq. 37). Mixing matters little there, and the distribution hardly depends on resolution, at least without conduction.

The authors ask whether the cancellation is physical ([Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) 2026, §6.2). If viscosity sets the area and thermal diffusivity the inflow, the dissipation depends on their ratio, the Prandtl number $\mathrm{Pr} = \nu/\alpha$, which is about 1 for numerical and turbulent dissipation alike. This view, limited by diffusion, contrasts with the view of scalar mixing, limited by mixing, in which the large-scale mixing rate fixes the transfer, and the present runs cannot tell them apart. Magnetic fields act as an effective viscosity and change Pr, and the total cooling of magnetised layers does depend on resolution. In the explicitly viscous runs of [Marin-Gilabert, Gronke and Oh](#marin-gilabert-et-al-2025) (2025), viscosity raises the cooling when cooling is fast, perhaps because those runs lie in the regime of Appendix A, and leaves it unchanged when cooling is slow, which may point to an effective Pr driven towards 1. Studies with explicit conduction, such as [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021; 2.2.8), found the cooling unchanged until conduction alters the turbulence at the integral scale, a result limited by mixing that a limited dynamic range might also explain. The authors caution that new physics in mixing layers should not lean on the resolution independence of purely hydrodynamic runs in place of a study of resolution. Conduction could also turn the one-way street from hot to cold gas into a loss of cold mass.

### 2.3 The survival and growth of cold gas

#### 2.3.1 Survival with radiative cooling

Radiative cooling is a particularly important addition to the crushing of clouds ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). [Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994, §2.3.1) already alluded to it, and later work studied it further ([Mellema, Kurk and Röttgering](#mellema-et-al-2002) 2002). Detailed simulations with strong cooling ([Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) 2015; [Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016; [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017) still concluded that all cold gas is eventually mixed into the wind. The review traces this to simulations that did not follow the cloud long enough, had too small a domain, did not track the cloud to capture its tail, or treated only clouds small enough to be destroyed. The two-dimensional simulations of [Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) (2010), and later those of [Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) (2016), [Armillotta et al.](#armillotta-et-al-2017) (2017) and [Gritton, Shelton and Galyardt](#gritton-et-al-2017) (2017), partly in three dimensions, showed that cooling can halt the disruption of some clouds and even make their cold mass grow.

[Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018) formulated a quantitative criterion: a cloud survives to be entrained if $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} < 1$ ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, eq. 7). Here $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}$ is the cooling time of the mixed gas at the geometric means of the temperatures and densities of the two phases, in the spirit of [Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990) (2.2.2). Since $t_\mathrm{cc} \propto r_\mathrm{cl}$ while the cooling time depends only on the gas, the criterion becomes a threshold of size, $r_\mathrm{cl} > r_\mathrm{crit}$: only clouds larger than a critical radius survive. For typical conditions of winds and the ISM, $r_\mathrm{crit}$ is of order a parsec, $r_\mathrm{crit} \approx 2\,\mathrm{pc}\;T_\mathrm{cl,4}^{5/2}\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind}\,P_3^{-1}\,\Lambda_\mathrm{mix,-21.4}^{-1}\,(\chi/100)$ (footnote 12). Here $T_\mathrm{cl,4} = T_\mathrm{cl}/10^4\,\mathrm{K}$, $P_3 = nT/(10^3\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}\,K})$, $\Lambda_\mathrm{mix,-21.4} = \Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})/(10^{-21.4}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}})$, and $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind}$ is the Mach number of the wind with respect to its own sound speed. Since $r_\mathrm{crit} \propto 1/n$, the threshold can also be written as a fixed column density of the cloud (footnote 13). **[GK]** The scalings follow from eq. 7 with $T_\mathrm{mix} = \chi^{1/2} T_\mathrm{cl}$, $n_\mathrm{mix} = P/(k_\mathrm{B} T_\mathrm{mix})$ and $v_\mathrm{wind} = \mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind}\,c_\mathrm{s,hot}$, where $c_\mathrm{s,hot} \propto (\chi T_\mathrm{cl})^{1/2}$.

#### 2.3.2 The debate on the criterion

The exact form of the criterion is still debated ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020) proposed $t_\mathrm{cool,hot}/t_\mathrm{life} < 1$, with the cooling time $t_\mathrm{cool,hot}$ of the hot medium and an empirical lifetime $t_\mathrm{life}$ of the cloud, which depends on several parameters but is generally about $10\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. [Sparre, Pfrommer and Ehlert](#sparre-et-al-2020) (2020) compared the two criteria and supported that of Li et al., while [Kanjilal, Dutta and Sharma](#kanjilal-et-al-2020) (2020) reached the reverse conclusion. Groups define survival differently, which matters because the cold mass can drop substantially in an initial phase, $t \sim \chi^{1/4} t_\mathrm{cc}$, before it grows again. A fixed cutoff, such as survival while the cold mass stays above 10 per cent of its initial value, avoids misreading an under-resolved later evolution, but may flag clouds as destroyed that would eventually grow. Fig. 5, adapted from [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020), shows the mechanisms at work in the regime of destruction, without cooling or with weak cooling.

Several studies have since argued that both criteria are somewhat unphysical ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022; [Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) 2023; [Farber and Gronke](#farber-gronke-2021) 2021). The mixed gas spans a range of temperatures, so that the picture of Begelman and Fabian is too simple (2.2.2). Near the temperature of the hot medium, moreover, mixing and not radiative cooling lowers the temperature, as simulations show in which clouds still grow when cooling is switched off above some temperature. The effective cooling time in eq. 7 should therefore be a weighted mean of $t_\mathrm{cool}$ between $T_\mathrm{cold}$ and $T_\mathrm{hot}$ ([Farber and Gronke](#farber-gronke-2021) 2021; [Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2022) 2022). These studies and the simulations of mixing layers show that the weighting peaks near the minimum of the cooling time, but its form is unknown. A criterion of survival that is better founded in theory and generally valid is still outstanding.

#### 2.3.3 What radiative cloud crushing has shown

The review draws five lessons from recent simulations of radiative cloud crushing ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2):

- Cold gas survives the acceleration by ram pressure if it cools efficiently enough, when some cooling time is shorter than some time of destruction. As the geometric criterion $r_\mathrm{cl} > r_\mathrm{crit}$, this lets clouds larger than several parsecs survive at the high pressures of the ISM and of winds, with a threshold that depends strongly on the environment ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018).
- Clouds that survive keep growing, also while they are entrained, and may gain much mass late in their evolution. Pulsations of the cold gas, whose pressure overshoots, drive the growth ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a; [Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2022) 2022). The transfer of mass follows the scaling of mixing layers, $\dot m \propto t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$ (2.2.3).
- The cold gas grows mainly in the wake of the cloud, in tails that reach $0.2$ to $0.5\,\chi r_\mathrm{cl}$, so that the domain must be long and the frame must follow the cloud (3.1).
- The transfer of mass also transfers momentum, which speeds up the acceleration and entrainment ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a; [Tonnesen and Bryan](#tonnesen-bryan-2021) 2021), even for molecular clouds of $\chi \sim 10^4$ ([Chen and Oh](#chen-oh-2024) 2024).
- Early studies, mostly in two dimensions, with short boxes, without cooling and over short times, could not see these results. Turbulent mixing and cooling need sufficient resolution, three dimensions and radiative cooling, and the long-term growth needs long boxes and long times.

#### 2.3.4 Further physics and realistic winds

Recent work connects several of these effects ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). [Hidalgo-Pineda, Farber and Gronke](#hidalgo-pineda-et-al-2023) (2023) confirmed that magnetic fields alone cannot entrain clouds of $\chi \gtrsim 100$. Together with cooling, however, they shift the criterion of survival by orders of magnitude, to $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} < 100$ for a plasma beta of about one in the wind. [Brüggen, Scannapieco and Grete](#bruggen-et-al-2023) (2023) found that anisotropic conduction together with cooling helps clouds survive in fields parallel to the flow, but has little effect in transverse fields, as the large scan of parameters of [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020) also shows. Gas below $10^4$ K, which some earlier simulations included ([Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017), has drawn new interest, to extend the criteria to colder gas and to add dust and molecules. These help to tell cold gas formed in the halo from gas carried there from the ISM.

Clouds of non-spherical, turbulent shape change the longer-term evolution little when they fall within the criterion of survival ([Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017; [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a; among others). Studies of more complex cold gas bridge the gap towards simulations of the full ISM ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.6). The criterion for single spherical clouds still needs generalising, for instance to clouds that shield each other, or that change the mixed gas because the wind engulfs many of them at different places.

The wind itself has drawn less attention ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). A constant, homogeneous wind is unrealistic for galactic winds, above all for the evolution beyond $t_\mathrm{drag}$, since an adiabatically expanding wind drops in pressure and density while its Mach number rises ([Chevalier and Clegg](#chevalier-clegg-1985) 1985). [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a) built this into their boundary conditions (see also [Dutta and Sharma](#dutta-sharma-2019) 2019). They found that the lower density of the wind slows the transfer of mass, but that the larger surface of the cold gas at lower pressure largely compensates. [Fielding and Bryan](#fielding-bryan-2022) (2022) built the terms of transfer between the phases into a semi-analytic model of a wind of two fluids, which [Nikolis and Gronke](#nikolis-gronke-2024) (2024) compared with simulations of cloud crushing. They showed that the changing background alters the clouds, and that the back-reaction of the cold gas on the hot medium can change the effect of the wind on larger scales considerably.

#### 2.3.5 Infalling clouds

A similar problem of entrainment applies to infalling cold gas, such as coronal rain, gas falling in the ICM or the high-velocity clouds in the halo of the Milky Way ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). The frames of a moving cloud and of a moving medium can be exchanged at some stages, but with an important difference. An outflowing cloud is gradually entrained, so that its shear decreases, while an infalling cloud reaches a terminal velocity and keeps a constant shear, so that it is never safe from destruction. A global box can follow the fall, but is generally too expensive for the details of mixing, and a domain that moves with the cloud, with modified boundary conditions, serves better ([Heitsch and Putman](#heitsch-putman-2009) 2009; 3.1). [Heitsch et al.](#heitsch-et-al-2021) (2021) showed in this way that the mixing and accretion of halo gas can dominate the mass of a high-velocity cloud. They leave gradients of metallicity and velocity along the stretched cloud, which may reveal its history of accretion.

[Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2023) (2023) found that the nearly constant shear at late times makes the criterion of survival stricter: the time to double the mass, $t_\mathrm{grow} \equiv m/\dot m$, must not exceed $t_\mathrm{cc}$ ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). The accretion of halo gas also sets a terminal velocity $v_\mathrm{t,grow} \sim g\,t_\mathrm{grow}$, with the gravitational acceleration $g$, which can lie well below the usual terminal velocity set by drag. The effect of magnetic fields on such clouds, and on their criterion of survival, is open ([Kaul et al.](#kaul-et-al-2025) 2025).

In sum, simulations of clouds in winds have shown how turbulent mixing, radiative cooling, magnetic fields and geometry shape the evolution and survival of cold gas in hot flows ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). They led to the criteria of survival, uncovered how cold gas grows and is entrained, and underpin the modern models of multiphase gas below the scale of the grid (§3.2). Open questions remain on realistic winds, on the interplay of several processes and on infalling clouds.

#### 2.3.6 Streams and jets

Cold streams that penetrate hot haloes are thought to feed galaxies around cosmic noon ([Birnboim and Dekel](#birnboim-dekel-2003) 2003; [Dekel and Birnboim](#dekel-birnboim-2006) 2006) ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.3). Some cosmological simulations show them, but their existence is debated, partly because these simulations resolve haloes too coarsely to capture the disruption of streams by hydrodynamic instabilities. Dedicated simulations of streams and jets share a set-up. A semi-infinite cylinder of radius $r_\mathrm{c}$ and density $\rho_\mathrm{c}$ lies in a background of density $\rho_\mathrm{b}$ that moves along it with the relative velocity $v$, usually periodic along the cylinder. A jet has $\rho_\mathrm{c} < \rho_\mathrm{b}$, a stream $\rho_\mathrm{c} > \rho_\mathrm{b}$, and the review treats streams, whose dynamics are the more multiphase.

Adiabatic streams overlap much with mixing layers (2.2), with the important difference that above the critical Mach number of eq. 1 they develop unstable body, or reflected, modes, which mix and destroy the stream in a similar way ([Padnos et al.](#padnos-et-al-2018) 2018, among others). However its destruction is defined, it takes about $t_\mathrm{disrupt} \sim \alpha\,r_\mathrm{c}/v$ (eq. 8), with a factor $\alpha$ found by experiment that depends on $v/(c_\mathrm{s,hot} + c_\mathrm{s,cold})$ ([Dimotakis](#dimotakis-1991) 1991). Since $\alpha \sim 10$ for $\chi \sim 100$, $t_\mathrm{disrupt} \approx t_\mathrm{cc}$ for the most common overdensity, and the criterion of eq. 7 and its critical radius hold (footnote 15). For $\chi \sim 1$, however, [Padnos et al.](#padnos-et-al-2018) (2018) found $\alpha \sim 4$ to 10, well above $\chi^{1/2}$. Magnetic fields, thermal conduction or self-gravity can slow the non-linear disruption.

With radiative cooling, streams can develop a multiphase structure, with cold cores inside warmer, turbulent layers ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.3). As for clouds, cooling can be efficient enough to prevent the disruption of the cold gas. Fig. 6, after [Mandelker et al.](#mandelker-et-al-2020) (2020), contrasts a stream that stays intact with cooling with one whose mixed gas soon dominates without it. Mandelker et al. found the criterion $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} < t_\mathrm{disrupt}$ (eq. 9), equivalent to eq. 7 but for the time of destruction. It too becomes a critical radius, below which cold gas is mixed and destroyed faster than it cools, so that only segments of a stream with $r \gtrsim r_\mathrm{crit}$ persist and possibly grow. If galaxy-scale and cosmological simulations do not resolve $r_\mathrm{crit}$, their streams may disrupt artificially or survive too easily, and their predictions of cold inflow, of the fuelling of galaxies and of the structure of the CGM become qualitatively wrong. Over longer times, the inflow onto the stream scales as $t_\mathrm{cool}^{-1/4}$, as in mixing layers (2.2.3). Where jets meet an ambient medium such as the ISM, shocks and contact discontinuities form a complex interface of turbulent mixing and secondary instabilities, which matters for the efficiency of the feedback of AGN in the ICM and the CGM.

#### 2.3.7 Growth and entrainment in a hot wind

[Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018, §1, §2) revisit the problem of entrainment for clouds that cool fast, $t_\mathrm{cool,cl} \ll t_\mathrm{cc}$, with the cooling time $t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$ of the cloud. A cloud of radius $r_\mathrm{cl}$ in a wind of speed $v_\mathrm{wind}$ is destroyed within $t_\mathrm{cc} \sim \chi^{1/2} r_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{wind}$, shorter by $\chi^{1/2}$ than the drag time $t_\mathrm{drag} \sim \chi r_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{wind}$ that the wind needs to accelerate it, with $\chi$ from about 100 in galaxies to 1000 in clusters (2.1.1). [Zhang et al.](#zhang-et-al-2017) (2017) concluded that hot winds cannot accelerate cold gas to the observed speeds. Non-thermal forces, such as radiation pressure or cosmic rays, or cold gas that condenses out of an adiabatically cooling wind, are the alternatives the authors name. Cooling prolongs the life of a cloud, but earlier simulations still found its cold gas mixed away before entrainment.

Mixing by the KH instability turns cold gas into hot ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, §2). After [Begelman and Fabian](#begelman-fabian-1990) (1990; 2.2.5), the wind and the cloud feed the mixing layer at $\dot m_\mathrm{wind} \sim \eta_\mathrm{wind} v \rho_\mathrm{wind}$ and $\dot m_\mathrm{cl} \sim \eta_\mathrm{cl} (\rho_\mathrm{cl}\rho_\mathrm{wind})^{1/2} v$, with deposition efficiencies $\eta$ and the speed $v$ of the mixing. For $\eta_\mathrm{cl} \sim \eta_\mathrm{wind}$ the mixed gas has $T_\mathrm{mix} \sim (T_\mathrm{wind} T_\mathrm{cl})^{1/2}$ (eq. 1) and, in pressure balance, $\rho_\mathrm{mix} \sim \chi^{1/2} \rho_\mathrm{wind}$. Its cooling time is $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} \sim \chi\,[\Lambda(T_\mathrm{cl})/\Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})]\,t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$, where $\Lambda(T_\mathrm{cl})/\Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})$ is about 0.1 to 1 for $\log_{10} T_\mathrm{cl}$ from 4.2 to 4.6. The mixed gas thus decides the fate of the cloud. If $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} < t_\mathrm{cc}$, the mixing layer cools fast enough to replenish the cold gas, and the new cold gas, partly drawn from the wind, already carries momentum, which speeds up entrainment. The requirement $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} < \alpha$ becomes a minimum radius, $R > v_\mathrm{wind} t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/(\chi^{1/2}\alpha) \approx 2\,\mathrm{pc}\;T_\mathrm{cl,4}^{5/2}\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind}\,(P_3\Lambda_\mathrm{mix,-21.4})^{-1}\,(\chi/100)\,\alpha^{-1}$ (eq. 2), with $v_\mathrm{wind} = \mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind} c_\mathrm{s,wind} \sim \mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind} c_\mathrm{s,cl}\chi^{1/2}$ and the scaled quantities of 2.3.1. Clouds larger than a parsec usually satisfy it in galaxies. Against the length of fragmentation $l_\mathrm{M} \sim c_\mathrm{s,cl} t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$ of [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018; 2.4.3), $R/l_\mathrm{M} \approx 10\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind}\,(\chi/100)\,\{[\Lambda(T_\mathrm{cl})/\Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})]/0.1\}\,\alpha^{-1}$ (eq. 3), so growing clouds must be much larger than the fragments, although even a swarm of droplets of size $l_\mathrm{M}$ can coagulate into larger clumps in a wind. **[GK]** With $t_\mathrm{cool} = 3k_\mathrm{B}T/(2n\Lambda)$, $n = P/(k_\mathrm{B}T)$ and a mean molecular weight of 0.6, the fiducial values of eq. 2 give about 2.6 pc, close to its 2 pc.

The simulations, in three dimensions at 8 cells per cloud radius (2.6.9, 3.8), span $t_\mathrm{cool,cl}/t_\mathrm{cc}$ from $10^{-6}$ to $10^{-1}$ at $\chi$ of 100, 300 and 1000 ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, §4, Fig. 1). The mass of dense gas, $\rho > \rho_\mathrm{cl}/3$, divides by $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc}$. Clouds with $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \gtrsim 1$ keep less than 1 per cent of their mass by $t \sim 15\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, whatever $\chi$. Clouds with $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \lesssim 1$ keep their mass and exceed it by up to an order of magnitude. All clouds first lose mass for about $0.2\,t_\mathrm{drag}$, then stabilise and grow. The growth is faster with faster cooling, but seems to saturate as $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \to 0$, perhaps because the efficiency of mixing, and so $t_\mathrm{cc}$, then limits it. Near the threshold the growth is slow: at $\chi = 1000$ the dense mass regains $m_\mathrm{cl}$ only after about $25\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. Without heating, the wind itself cools when $t_\mathrm{cool,wind} \lesssim t_\mathrm{drag}$, a conservative limit. To include runs beyond it, cooling is switched off above $0.6\,T_\mathrm{wind}$ in all runs of Fig. 1, which leaves the default run unchanged (Fig. 3).

The velocity of the cold gas, weighted by a dye, approaches that of the wind within about $t_\mathrm{drag} = \chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{cc}$, sooner with faster cooling and much sooner at high $\chi$ (Fig. 1). The shear falls because hot gas that already comoves cools, rather than because existing cold gas is accelerated. At $\chi = 1000$ the velocity difference drops over $t/t_\mathrm{drag} \sim 0.2$ to 0.5, while the cold mass grows about tenfold. In this limit the velocity tends to $v_\mathrm{wind}\,m_\mathrm{cold}/(m_\mathrm{cold} + m_\mathrm{cl})$, with $m_\mathrm{cold}$ the newly formed cold mass, as for an inelastic collision that conserves momentum.

Density slices at $\chi = 100$ show the mechanism (Fig. 2). In both regimes a reverse shock is driven into the cloud. With slow cooling, $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim 8$, the tail mixes quickly to below about $\rho_\mathrm{cl}/10$, and by $12\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the dense mass has fallen to 5 per cent. With fast cooling, $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim 0.08$, the mixed gas in the tail cools and grows denser. The tail thickens to about the initial size of the cloud, with a density near $\rho_\mathrm{cl}$, by $6\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, and the dense mass reaches $2.6\,m_\mathrm{cl}$ by $12\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. As the warm gas in the tail cools, its pressure gradients draw in hot gas, which mixes and cools in turn. This focusing by cooling redirects the cold gas of low entropy stripped from the sides of the cloud towards the tail, while in adiabatic cloud crushing stripped gas moves away from the axis and mixes away. The point where the tail re-forms lies a few cloud radii downstream, so that a domain that is too short misses it, and the cloud then appears to disintegrate.

The authors ascribe the destruction in earlier radiative studies either to inefficient cooling, $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \gtrsim 1$, in [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2015) (2015) and [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) (2017), or to short domains in [Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) (2009) and [Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) (2015), whose cooling was efficient ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, §5.1; 2.6.9). Earlier two-dimensional simulations had seen the cold mass grow by a few per cent in the wake ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) 2010), and the loss of mass slow with the size of the cloud, with a three-dimensional run that grew by about 20 per cent ([Armillotta, Fraternali and Marinacci](#armillotta-et-al-2016) 2016; [Armillotta et al.](#armillotta-et-al-2017) 2017). Gronke and Oh add a quantitative criterion, tested in a three-dimensional sweep, and growth by an order of magnitude.

Further tests leave the picture standing ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, §5.2, Fig. 3). Halving $T_\mathrm{cl}$ changes little. Lowering the floor to $0.03\,T_\mathrm{cl}$ removes the initial loss of mass, since the cloud first cools and contracts, and slows the later growth, since the denser cloud mixes more slowly, as for a higher $\chi$. The equilibrium curve of [Wiersma, Schaye and Smith](#wiersma-et-al-2009) (2009), with a lower floor, changes the evolution but keeps the growth. Heating that returns the radiated energy uniformly each step changes nothing, nor does switching off cooling above $0.6\,T_\mathrm{wind}$. Periodic transverse boundaries lower the mass slightly after about $7\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, since shocks re-enter and disturb the tail. A wind of $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind} = 3$, as in the driving region of the wind of [Chevalier and Clegg](#chevalier-clegg-1985) (1985), drives a bow shock that compresses the cloud and its tail, which slows mixing and growth without changing the trend. Less idealised clouds and winds, anisotropic conduction, magnetic fields and cooling curves with heating are left for later work.

#### 2.3.8 The shape of the cooling curve

In the plane of pressure and entropy of [Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) (2022, §2.2; 2.5.3), cooling adds $\dot K_\mathrm{cool} = K\dot e/e = -K/t_\mathrm{cool}$ for optically thin gas (Fig. 2). Where $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing} + \dot K_\mathrm{cool}(p_0, K) \gg 0$ over $[K_\mathrm{cl}, K_\mathrm{w}]$, the cold phase should be destroyed, and where the sum is far below zero over a large part of that range, a cold phase should survive. By analogy with [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018; 2.3.7), growth is expected when $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing,char}(K_\mathrm{mix}) < |\dot K_\mathrm{cool}(p_0, K_\mathrm{mix})|$, with $K_\mathrm{mix} \sim (K_\mathrm{cl} K_\mathrm{w})^{1/2} = \chi^{5/6} K_\mathrm{cl}$. Since $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing,char} \sim K/t_\mathrm{cc}$, and $|\dot K_\mathrm{cool}|$ falls roughly inversely with $K$ under conditions of the ISM, the criterion $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} < t_\mathrm{cc}$ nearly guarantees that cooling wins over all of $[K_\mathrm{cl}, K_\mathrm{mix}]$ (§5.2). With a mean molecular weight that depends on density and temperature, the critical radius scales as $\mu_\mathrm{cl}^{-5/2}$, and the tables of Grackle for solar metallicity give $\mu_\mathrm{cl} = 0.827$ and $R_\mathrm{cl,crit} \sim 6$ pc for the fiducial values with $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} = 1$ (footnote 1).

Runs with cooling at 64 cells per cloud radius compare slow cooling, $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \approx 9$ at $T_\mathrm{cl} = 10^4$ K, with fast cooling, $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \approx 0.2$ at $T_\mathrm{cl}$ of $4 \times 10^4$ K or $10^4$ K ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022, §5.1, Table 1). Slow cooling barely changes the adiabatic course and only slows the spread to high entropy. With fast cooling, a reservoir of gas stays at $(p_0, K_\mathrm{cl})$, and after a transient the distribution settles into a nearly steady, bistable state of long-lived cold and hot gas (Figs. 8 to 10). The cold phase first loses mass, then the transfer reverses, and it recaptures what it lost. The criterion of Gronke and Oh predicts every fate correctly (Fig. 11). In the fast runs, the share of the cold gas that began in the cloud drops as the velocity difference falls, the mark of an inelastic collision in which mixing drives the acceleration. In the adiabatic and slow runs that share stays near 1, and ram pressure accelerates the cloud. The $p$–$K$ distribution of the fast runs dips below $p_0$ near $\log_\chi(K/K_\mathrm{cl}) \sim 0.2$, likely an artefact of underresolved cooling, since the dip almost vanishes where $\ell_\mathrm{cool} = \min(c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool})$ is resolved (§5.1; 2.6.3).

The ratio $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc}$ does not fix the evolution ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022, §5.2). At nearly the same ratio, the cloud at $10^4$ K takes at least twice as long as that at $4 \times 10^4$ K to grow and to be entrained. At $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \approx 1$, a cloud of 1000 pc at $4 \times 10^4$ K grows like the fast run at $10^4$ K, while a cloud of 9 pc at $10^4$ K is destroyed (Fig. 12), so that the cooling below $T_\mathrm{mix}$ can change even survival. In the warmer cloud, $|\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}/\dot K_\mathrm{cool}|$ is about ten times smaller for $0.1 \lesssim \log_\chi(K/K_\mathrm{cl}) \lesssim 0.6$: the cooling time near the cold phase sets how fast the cloud grows (Fig. 13). Broken power laws test this systematically (§5.3, Fig. 14). With $\mathcal{T} = T/T_\mathrm{mix}$ and $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl} = t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$, the ratio $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{cool,mix}$ is $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl}^{-1}$ at low $\mathcal{T}$, $\mathcal{T}^2$ in the middle and $\alpha\mathcal{T}^{3.8}$ above $\mathcal{T} = \alpha^{-5/9}$, with $\alpha = 0.138259$ and a fixed $\mu = 0.6$ (eq. 7). Four runs with $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl}$ of 1, 2, 6 and 60, all at $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} = 0.1576$, show that rapid growth sets in sooner as the break moves to lower temperature: $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl} \le 2$ resembles the run at $10^4$ K, $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl} \ge 6$ that at $4 \times 10^4$ K (Figs. 15 to 17). While $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}$ decides whether cooling acts, the shape of the curve below $T_\mathrm{mix}$ decides when rapid growth begins. The authors speculate that a weighted mean of the cooling time between $T_\mathrm{cl}$ and $T_\mathrm{mix}$ controls it, a characteristic cooling time of the cold phase related to $t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$ or $\min t_\mathrm{cool}$. The onset may mark the change from the growth of the tail to the entrained phase of [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a). **[GK]** Continuity of the segments of eq. 7 places the lower break at $\mathcal{T} = \eta_\mathrm{mix,cl}^{-1/2}$, as Fig. 14 shows, where eq. 7 prints $\eta_\mathrm{mix,cl}^{1/2}$.

Cooling of the wind itself matters little ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022, §6.3.1, Appendix A.1). Switching it off above $0.6\,T_\mathrm{w}$ hardly changes the growth, here as in [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018), and mainly lowers the equilibrium pressure by 30 per cent. The authors therefore doubt that the cooling time of the wind, which underlies the criteria of [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020) and [Sparre, Pfrommer and Ehlert](#sparre-et-al-2020) (2020), governs survival (2.3.2). They ascribe the larger critical radii of those studies to choices of set-up (§6.3.2). Sparre et al. let the gas cool to $T_\mathrm{cl}/2$, and the contraction impedes growth and may let clouds in winds of $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} \sim 4.5$ shatter. Short boxes may misclassify growing clouds, shocks reflected from transverse boundaries may heat the gas artificially, and definitions of survival, by a mass that never falls below 10 per cent or by a positive rate of growth at $12.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, depend on when growth starts. Thermal conduction might yet raise the weight of the cooling time of the wind. Clouds near thermal equilibrium, where the characteristic cooling time of the cold phase is longest, start growing late, which gives destructive processes more time (§6.3.3). They need radii somewhat above the criterion of Gronke and Oh, the more so in winds with turbulence driven from outside. Cooling below $T_\mathrm{cl}$, finally, reduces the initial loss of mass but slows the later growth, since the cloud contracts (Appendix A.1, Fig. 18).

#### 2.3.9 A criterion from the crossing of the cloud

[Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) (2023, §1, §3.1) phrase survival as a race between a dynamical time $\tau_\mathrm{dyn}$ and a cooling time $\tau_\mathrm{cool}$. Earlier criteria, which disagree by large factors on the smallest surviving cloud, differ in both. Most take for $\tau_\mathrm{dyn}$ the crushing time, while studies of single shear layers find it closer to the shear time, which depends differently on $\chi$ (2.2.7, 2.2.8). A cloud grows only if it takes in fresh hot gas, so a parcel of hot gas must mix and cool before it is carried past the cloud. That takes somewhat longer than $t_\mathrm{shear} = R_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{w}$, since the mixing of momentum lowers the relative velocity and the parcel travels somewhat farther than $R_\mathrm{cl}$ (Fig. 2). The cloud thus survives when $\alpha t_\mathrm{shear} \gtrsim \tau_\mathrm{cool}$ (eq. 1), with $\alpha$ between a few and about 10. The crushing time, $t_\mathrm{cc} = \chi^{1/2} t_\mathrm{shear}$, remains the time of complete destruction, a different process. For $\tau_\mathrm{cool}$, which should be a weighted mean over the cooling curve (2.3.8), the authors take the refinement of [Farber and Gronke](#farber-gronke-2021) (2021), $t_\mathrm{cool,minmix} = t_\mathrm{cool}((\chi e_\mathrm{min,cool} e_\mathrm{cl})^{1/2}, p)$. Here $e = p/[(\gamma - 1)\rho]$ is the specific internal energy, and $e_\mathrm{min,cool}$ that at the minimum of $t_\mathrm{cool}$ between $e_\mathrm{cl}$ and $e_\mathrm{w}$. The internal energy keeps the formulas simple when the mean molecular weight varies, since $e_\mathrm{w} = \chi e_\mathrm{cl}$ while $T_\mathrm{w} < \chi T_\mathrm{cl}$ (footnote 4). With $\alpha \approx 7$ from the simulations, the critical radius is $R_\mathrm{crit} = c_\mathrm{s,cold}\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}\,\chi^{1/2}\,t_\mathrm{cool,minmix}/\alpha$, with the sound speed of the cloud $c_\mathrm{s,cold} = [\gamma(\gamma - 1) e_\mathrm{cl}]^{1/2}$ (eqs. 2, 3).

The earlier criteria compare cooling with destruction rather than with advection ([Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) 2023, §3.2). That of [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018), refined by Farber and Gronke, gives $R_\mathrm{crit} \sim c_\mathrm{s,cold}\,\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}\,t_\mathrm{cool,minmix}$ (eqs. 4, 5). [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020) require $f t_\mathrm{cc} > t_\mathrm{cool,w}$, with an empirical lifetime from $[f/(9 \pm 1)]^{10/3} = \eta\,(2R_\mathrm{cl}/1\,\mathrm{pc})(n_\mathrm{w}/10^{-2}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}})(v_\mathrm{w}^2/10^4\,\mathrm{km^2\,s^{-2}})$ and $\eta = 1$ (eq. 6). [Sparre, Pfrommer and Ehlert](#sparre-et-al-2020) (2020) improved it for $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} > 1$ with $\eta = 2(\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}/1.5)^{-2.5}$, which yields a critical radius with a normalisation of 72.2 pc (eq. 7). For $T_\mathrm{cl} \approx 5 \times 10^3$ K and $100 \le \chi \le 10^4$, where $\mu_\mathrm{w}$ is constant and $t_\mathrm{cool} \propto e^{2.4} p^{-1}$ (footnote 8), the three criteria scale as $\chi^{1.2}\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} p^{-1}$ (Gronke, Oh and Farber), $\chi^{1.85}\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}^{2.23} p^{-1}$ (Li and Sparre) and $\chi^{1.7}\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} p^{-1}$ (new) (eqs. 8 to 10).

The runs span $\chi$ from 100 to $10^4$ and $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}$ from 1.5 to 6, with clouds at $T_\mathrm{cl} \approx 5 \times 10^3$ K, about the thermal equilibrium at $p/k_\mathrm{B} = 10^3\,\mathrm{K\,cm^{-3}}$ under the ultraviolet background at redshift 0, and some at $4 \times 10^4$ K for comparison with Gronke and Oh ([Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) 2023, §2, §4, Figs. 3, 4). The cold phase is gas denser than $\rho_\mathrm{mix} = \rho_\mathrm{cl}/\chi^{1/2}$, and a cloud counts as destroyed when its cold mass falls below 1 to 10 per cent of the initial value. The new criterion matches the runs for any such threshold and reproduces the steep dependence of $R_\mathrm{crit}$ on $\chi$, as does that of Li and Sparre, while that of Gronke, Oh and Farber is too shallow. The authors read the success of Li and Sparre as a cancellation: weighting the cooling of the hot wind, which many runs switch off without changing the result, makes up for the wrong dependence of $t_\mathrm{cc}$ on $\chi$. In the Mach number, relaxed definitions of destruction favour the shallow scaling of the new criterion and strict ones an intermediate scaling. The supersonic runs of Sparre et al. agree, and only their subsonic runs, at $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} = 0.5$, call for a steeper dependence, since subsonic winds behave differently. The weaker dependence on $\chi$ that Gronke and Oh found came from their clouds at $4 \times 10^4$ K, near the peak of the cooling curve, where $t_\mathrm{cool,minmix} = t_\mathrm{cool,mix}$, and from radii sampled in steps of about a factor of 10, too coarse to tell $t_\mathrm{cc}$ from $t_\mathrm{shear}$. Farber and Gronke varied $T_\mathrm{cl}$ at fixed $\chi$, which cannot tell them apart either. The definitions of destruction, often blamed for the discrepancies, matter less than the choices of $T_\mathrm{cl}$, $R_\mathrm{cl}$ and $\chi$ in the runs.

In convenient units, $R_\mathrm{crit} \approx 618\,\mathrm{pc}\;\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}\chi_3^{1.5}T_\mathrm{cl,4}^{1.5}\,(\hat p_3\Lambda_\mathrm{minmix,-21.4})^{-1}\,(T_\mathrm{min,cool}/10^{4.25}\,\mathrm{K})$, within about 30 per cent of the full form with a variable $\mu$ ([Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) 2023, §5, eq. 11). Here $\chi_3 = \chi/10^3$, $\hat p_3 = (p/k_\mathrm{B})/(10^3\,\mathrm{K\,cm^{-3}})$, $\Lambda_\mathrm{minmix,-21.4} = \Lambda(T_\mathrm{minmix})/(10^{-21.4}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}})$, and $T_\mathrm{min,cool}$ is the temperature of the minimum cooling time between $T_\mathrm{cl}$ and $T_\mathrm{w}$. The criterion should hold for any uniform $t_\mathrm{cool}(T)$ with no local maximum between $T_\mathrm{cl}$ and $T_\mathrm{w}$, at most one minimum between $T_\mathrm{cl}$ and $T_\mathrm{mix}$, and slow cooling of the wind (footnote 10). It is most meaningful for clouds in thermal equilibrium; at the fiducial pressure, $T_\mathrm{min,cool} = 10^{4.25}$ K, $T_\mathrm{cl} \approx 5 \times 10^3$ K and $c_\mathrm{s,cold} \approx 8.5\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$. At lower pressures, clouds would need unreasonably large sizes to survive, while at the higher pressures where starbursts launch outflows, $p/k_\mathrm{B} \gtrsim 10^4\,\mathrm{K\,cm^{-3}}$, the clouds are colder and the criterion applies. For gas colder than about $10^3$ K, $\psi t_\mathrm{shear} \gtrsim t_\mathrm{cool,max}$, with a constant $\psi$ and the maximum cooling time between $T_\mathrm{cl}$ and $T_\mathrm{min,cool}$, may govern survival. The comparison with $t_\mathrm{shear}$ agrees with the eddy turnover time that governs shear layers, and simulations in tall boxes of more realistic interactions find sizes of clouds consistent with the criterion. Idealised runs of cool clouds in supersonic winds make robust predictions only in a narrow range of conditions, since self-gravity, cooling of the wind and self-shielding take over elsewhere (Figs. 3, 4). Subsonic flows, magnetic fields, conduction, external turbulence and gravity, geometry and ensembles of clouds remain to be studied.

#### 2.3.10 Survival and growth in turbulence

[Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) (2022, §1, §2) place cold clouds not in a wind but in a hot medium stirred to a turbulent Mach number $\mathcal{M} = v_\mathrm{turb}/c_\mathrm{s,hot}$, in the regime where the hot gas cools slowly. They expect survival when $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} < \alpha t_\mathrm{cc}$, with $t_\mathrm{cc} \sim \chi^{1/2} r_\mathrm{cl}/v_\mathrm{turb}$ (eq. 1), as in wind tunnels, where $\alpha \sim 1$ (2.3.7), but leave $\alpha$ free. The critical radius of the wind tunnel, $r_\mathrm{crit,w}$, is about 50 times $\ell_\mathrm{shatter} \sim c_\mathrm{s,cl} t_\mathrm{cool,cl}$ for their fiducial values (eqs. 2, 3). A surviving cloud should grow at $\dot m \sim v_\mathrm{mix} A_\mathrm{cl}\rho_\mathrm{hot}$ (eq. 4), with the mixing velocity of fast-cooling layers, $v_\mathrm{mix} \propto u'^{3/4}(L_\mathrm{cold}/t_\mathrm{cool,c})^{1/4}$, where the turbulent velocity $u'$, the integral scale $L_\mathrm{cold}$ and the cooling time $t_\mathrm{cool,c}$ belong to the cold medium (eq. 5; 2.2.7, 2.2.8). Growth scales with the area of the cold gas, as a reaction in combustion scales with the surface of its reactant. For a monolithic cloud, $A_\mathrm{cl} \sim (m/\rho_\mathrm{cl})^{2/3}$, and the mass grows as a power law, $m = (1 + at)^3$. A cloud that keeps fragmenting to a scale $\tilde r$ has $A \propto m$ instead, and grows exponentially, $m \sim m_0 \exp(t/t_\mathrm{grow})$ with $t_\mathrm{grow} \sim \chi\tilde r/v_\mathrm{mix}$, while a fractal surface, $A \propto V^{D/3}$ with $2 < D < 3$, lies in between. With the effective cooling time of [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021), $\tilde t_\mathrm{cool} \sim (l\,t_\mathrm{cool,c}/u')^{1/2}$ (eq. 6; 2.2.8), and a turbulent pressure continuous across the interface, $u' \sim \mathcal{M} c_\mathrm{s,c}(l/L_\mathrm{box})^{1/3}$, the growth time becomes $t_\mathrm{grow} \equiv m/\dot m \sim \chi\tilde t_\mathrm{cool} \sim \chi\mathcal{M}^{-1/2}(l/\ell_\mathrm{shatter})^{1/2}(l/L_\mathrm{box})^{-1/6}\,t_\mathrm{cool,c}$ (eq. 7). It depends only weakly, as $l^{1/3}$, on the length $l$ that characterises the interface, and $l \sim r_\mathrm{cl}$ serves throughout, so that the growth should be exponential.

The simulations confirm survival above a size ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, §4.1, Figs. 1, 2). At $\mathcal{M} \sim 1$ and $\chi \sim 100$, clouds of $50\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ are destroyed within a few $t_\mathrm{cc}$, clouds of $500\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ survive and grow, and clouds of $200\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ survive or die depending on the random seed of the stirring. A surviving cloud does not stay compact but fragments into droplets that eventually fill the box. Growth follows the exponential model for the largest clouds and the monolithic power law for a surviving cloud of $200\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ that grows a tail, both with a fudge factor of about 0.5 in $t_\mathrm{grow}$. Small droplets do not shield each other (§4.2, Fig. 3): up to 1000 droplets of $5\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ all disintegrate under forcing throughout the box, while the growth of large droplets does not depend on their number. Shielding may matter only in bulk flows, as for the cloudlets of [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018; 2.4.4). Stronger stirring speeds up both the destruction of small droplets and the growth of large ones, and the measured growth times follow the Mach scaling of eq. 7 but are about half the prediction (§4.3, Figs. 4 to 7). Over all runs at $\chi \sim 100$, survival requires $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \lesssim \alpha$, with a weak dependence of $\alpha$ on the Mach number and $\alpha^{-1} \sim 3$ at $\mathcal{M} \sim 1$, close to the criterion of the wind tunnels but stricter (Fig. 8).

At $\chi \sim 1000$ and the same $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim 0.06$, survival becomes marginal ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, §4.4, Fig. 9), which at face value fits the criterion of [Li et al.](#li-et-al-2020) (2020) and [Sparre, Pfrommer and Ehlert](#sparre-et-al-2020) (2020) better. The authors explain it otherwise. In wind tunnels, dense clouds first lose much of their mass and are reborn from the mixed gas once entrained, a phase that turbulence, changing in direction and magnitude, makes less likely. At $\mathcal{M} \sim 0.1$, clouds of $\chi \sim 1000$ survive easily, and at fixed size $t_\mathrm{grow} \propto \chi^{1/2}$, as predicted (Fig. 10). Near the threshold, survival hinges on whether a large enough clump remains: of three runs at $200\,\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$, only the one whose largest cloud is not broken up survives, although another forms many more droplets (§4.5, Fig. 11).

The growth of mass matches the cooling luminosity $L$ through $\dot m_L = \frac{2}{5}\frac{\mu m_\mathrm{p}}{k_\mathrm{B} T_\mathrm{h}}\frac{L}{\mathcal{M}^2 + 1}$ for many sizes and Mach numbers, and the factor $1 + \mathcal{M}^2$ of [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019) is needed to infer growth from observed emission ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, §4.8.1, eq. 8, Fig. 18). The velocity structure functions of all gas and of the cold gas follow each other with the Kolmogorov slope of 1/3, steeper on small scales through numerical dissipation, and the cold gas moves somewhat slower (§4.8.2, Fig. 19). The transfer of mass thus couples the phases well, and the cold gas traces the motions of the hot gas. In the CGM, where inflows and outflows drive turbulence of $\mathcal{M} \sim 0.1$ to 1, cold gas could therefore survive and grow, with a doubling time $t_\mathrm{grow} \sim 530\,\mathrm{Myr}\,(\chi/100)[L/(10\,\mathcal{M}\,\mathrm{kpc})]^{1/2}T_\mathrm{cl,4}^{3/4}(P_3\Lambda_\mathrm{cl,-23})^{-1/2}$, with $\Lambda_\mathrm{cl,-23} = \Lambda(T_\mathrm{cl})/(10^{-23}\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^3\,s^{-1}})$ (§5.2). Winds would then need to deposit less cold gas in the halo than is usually assumed. The study leaves out magnetic fields, cosmic rays and conduction, uses idealised stirring, and its boxes of 10 to $80\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ are too small for the long-term evolution (§5.5).

### 2.4 The characteristic scale of cooling

#### 2.4.1 Shattering and splattering

Beyond whether and where thermal instability acts (2.7.1), simulations of multiphase media have studied the longer-term, non-linear evolution of the condensing gas and its morphology ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.4). Early work pointed to fragmentation aided by cooling, which makes small cold clouds in a hot medium ([Hennebelle and Pérault](#hennebelle-perault-1999) 1999; [Burkert and Lin](#burkert-lin-2000) 2000). [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018) showed that clouds smaller than the shattering length $l_\mathrm{shatter} \equiv \min(c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool})$ (eq. 11) stay in sonic contact with their surroundings and cool isobarically. Larger clouds, $r \gg l_\mathrm{shatter}$, cannot cool as a whole in pressure balance. Cooling isochorically, as was commonly assumed, they would be strongly underpressurised between $t \sim t_\mathrm{cool}$ and the sound-crossing time $t \sim r/c_\mathrm{s}$. McCourt et al. suggested instead that such clouds fragment as they cool, eventually into clumps of size $l_\mathrm{shatter}$ that stay in sonic contact. They showed the resulting morphology of the cold gas in large two-dimensional simulations and related it to observations of small fragments of cold gas. At about the same time, [Cornuault et al.](#cornuault-et-al-2018) (2018) described a similar fragmentation in streams, but evaluated the cooling length $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ at the conditions behind a shock rather than at its minimum, and did not test their theory with simulations (footnote 18). The simulations of McCourt et al. show a range of sizes of clumps, which may reflect imperfect fragmentation or rapid coagulation (footnote 19).

The nature of fragmentation driven by cooling has since been debated ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.4). [Waters and Proga](#waters-proga-2019b) (2019b) gave a counterpoint to shattering, summarised again in [Waters and Proga](#waters-proga-2023) (2023). From the linear theory of thermal instability and simulations in one dimension, they showed that large clouds can oscillate as they cool. They suspected that the fragmentation of [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018) is numerical, since similar artefacts appeared in their own one-dimensional simulations, and conjectured that a rebound is followed by fragmentation. Three-dimensional simulations show such a process ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020b) 2020b; [Yao et al.](#yao-et-al-2024) 2024) and extend the criterion of fragmentation, $r \gg l_\mathrm{shatter}$, by a dependence on the final overdensity. The cold clumps then form at the end of a violent pulsation, which supports the picture that [Waters and Proga](#waters-proga-2019b) (2019b) called splattering. Fig. 7 contrasts the two pathways: a cloud far out of pressure balance that pulsates violently and sheds its outer layers, and a continuous fragmentation in pressure balance into ever smaller clouds of size $\sim c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, which McCourt et al. envisaged as shattering.

#### 2.4.2 Open questions on fragmentation

The review lists the open questions ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.4):

- Dimensionality. Some studies, especially earlier ones, ran in one dimension, while the fragmentation of a surface aided by the KH instability, or fragmentation in a turbulent medium ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, among others), occur only in several dimensions. [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018) consider shattering unlikely in one dimension (2.6.8), yet [Das, Choudhury and Sharma](#das-et-al-2021) (2021) support and [Waters and Proga](#waters-proga-2019b) (2019b) oppose shattering with one-dimensional simulations. [Farber and Gronke](#farber-gronke-2023) (2023) found that the rotation of clumps continues their fragmentation after the expansion, which they call splintering (footnote 20).
- Further physics, chiefly thermal conduction. The Field length might set the characteristic scale after all, but conclusions differ widely, mainly because the effect of tangled magnetic fields is uncertain ([Burkert and Lin](#burkert-lin-2000) 2000; [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) 2018; [Waters and Proga](#waters-proga-2019b) 2019b; among others).
- The coagulation of droplets, during the cooling and later, and its relation to the criterion of fragmentation. Coagulation in multiphase media has long been known, but only recently have studies turned to coagulation driven by cooling, in which clumps are carried along by the hot gas that accretes onto them ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2023) 2023, among others). It is slow, but depends strongly on the distance between droplets, and may matter in the early stages of fragmentation.
- Initial conditions. [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018) used a truncated Gaussian random field, more recent studies simpler top-hat or solenoidal perturbations, which may change the evolution and its outcome.

The latest literature is inconclusive, which terms that differ between studies do not help. Open are whether a continuous fragmentation like that of Jeans occurs, whether multiphase gas has a characteristic scale, and how far turbulent motions alone fragment clouds, without shattering driven by cooling.

#### 2.4.3 A characteristic scale for cold gas

[McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018, §1, §2.1) argue that cold gas at about $10^4$ K often forms not monolithic clouds but a mist of tiny cloudlets spread through a much larger volume, with hot gas between them. Gas that cools below about $10^6$ K falls rapidly to $10^4$ K through line emission (Fig. 2), which suggests two phases. A cooling perturbation of size $R_0 \gg c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ cannot contract as fast as it loses its pressure support. If it cooled isochorically, as is commonly assumed, it would stay far out of pressure balance until its sound-crossing time, long after one cooling time (Fig. 3). Such a state is far from force balance and therefore unstable. The faster path to equilibrium is to split into pieces of size $\sim c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, small enough to cool isobarically. Out of sonic contact with each other, the pieces contract independently and draw apart. The dynamics of the splitting are left open, but the unbalanced pressure should drive motions or instabilities on many scales (footnote 3). Above $10^4$ K, $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ scales roughly as $T^3$ and shrinks steeply as the gas cools, so each piece may split again, until the gas reaches the minimum of $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$. For the cooling curve of [Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) (1993), $\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet} \sim \min(c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}) \sim 0.1\,\mathrm{pc}\,(n/\mathrm{cm^{-3}})^{-1}$ (eq. 1), the $l_\mathrm{shatter}$ of 2.4.1. The column through one cloudlet, $N_\mathrm{cloudlet} = n\,\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet} \sim 10^{17}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$ (eq. 2), hardly depends on the environment, as [Voit and Donahue](#voit-donahue-1990) (1990) also found. The scale is uncertain by a factor of about 10, through shock heating among other processes, but lies orders of magnitude below the sizes of cold structures and the scales that simulations and observations usually resolve.

The authors liken shattering to fragmentation by the Jeans instability, with contraction driven by cooling and external pressure instead of self-gravity (§2.1). Regions larger than $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ cannot communicate pressure in time, as regions larger than the Jeans length cannot establish hydrostatic equilibrium. Just as the Jeans length of isothermal gas shrinks with contraction until the gas becomes optically thick, the cooling length shrinks along the cooling curve until its minimum near $10^4$ K. The scale therefore belongs to the cold phase, at that minimum. [Hennebelle and Pérault](#hennebelle-perault-1999) (1999) and [Burkert and Lin](#burkert-lin-2000) (2000) also found a scale $\sim c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, but evaluated it in the hot gas, before cooling, and let large perturbations cool isochorically before being compressed as a whole, so that $l_\mathrm{cold} \sim (c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool})_\mathrm{hot}\,\delta^{-1/3}$, with the contraction ratio $\delta \equiv \rho_\mathrm{cool}/\rho_\mathrm{hot}$. The cloudlets of McCourt et al. can be much smaller, and their size should not depend on the initial temperature of the cooling gas.

A small scale lets a little cold gas cover a large area (§1, Fig. 1). For a region of size $D$ filled with cloudlets of size $\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet}$, the ratio of the covering fraction $f_A$ to the volume filling fraction $f_V$ is $f_A/f_V \sim D/\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet}$, where $f_A$ counts the cloudlets met along a line of sight and can exceed 1 (footnote 1).

#### 2.4.4 Shattering in two-dimensional simulations

[McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018, §2.2) test the picture with two-dimensional hydrodynamic simulations, which they offer as suggestive only, since several numerical errors are not controlled (2.6.8). Adiabatic gas dynamics is free of scale, but cooling imprints one: objects much smaller than $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ evolve differently from larger ones. Simulations usually span no more than two decades between the smallest resolved scale, of about 10 cells, and the domain, which is matched to the region of interest. Deeply nested AMR spans more, but does not suit a fog of fast cloudlets spread through the domain (footnote 4). The simulations are therefore built to capture $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$. Their set-up is given in 3.7.

In the first set, an isobaric perturbation at about $10^6$ K cools to $10^4$ K in a background of about $10^7$ K, which stays hot during the run, so that no heating is needed (§2.2.1, Fig. 4). In a domain of $10^5\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, about a kiloparsec, the perturbation shatters into tiny unresolved fragments, and regions that look diffuse turn out to hold cloudlets with a small filling fraction. In a domain of $10\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ it contracts into a single cold mass. In the intermediate domain of $10^3\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ the gas shatters first, but mixing cools the hot gas between the fragments, lowers its pressure and draws the cloudlets together into one blob. The authors suspect this coagulation to be an artefact, since the mixing is unresolved and turbulence, absent here, could suppress it.

In the second set, a cloud at $10^4$ K moves through a wind at $10^7$ K with a Mach number of 1.5, as in radiative cloud crushing (2.1.1), at up to about 600 cells per cloud radius, against the 32 to 64 of typical three-dimensional simulations ([Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) 2015, among others; §2.2.2, Fig. 5). The initial radius runs from $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ to $10^3\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, the size of large high-velocity clouds or of the resolution of cosmological zoom simulations in haloes. The largest cloud is stripped into a turbulent wake full of small unresolved knots of cold gas. At $10^2\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ the fragments are marginally resolved and track $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$. At $10\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ the cloud fragments without shattering into tiny pieces, and at $R_0 \lesssim c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ its wake holds no dense knots and evolves much as without cooling. Clouds much larger than $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ thus fragment, and clouds of about that size do not. A wind at $10^6$ K, with a density contrast of 100, disrupts the cloud differently but gives virtually the same sizes of clumps (Fig. 9). This agrees with a cascade of fragmentation, and not with models from linear theory, which set the scale by the hot gas. Raising the Mach number in that wind threefold, to 4.5, the velocity of Fig. 5, enlarges the cloudlets about twofold, so that heating plays a secondary part.

An isolated cloudlet would still be eroded within its crushing time, $\sim (\rho_\mathrm{cloud}/\rho_\mathrm{wind})^{1/2}\,\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet}/v_\mathrm{wind}$, the $t_\mathrm{cc}$ of 2.1.1, which is short (§2.2.2). The authors argue that a system of cloudlets behaves otherwise: in the wake of a large cloud the cloudlets soon move with the hot gas and shield each other, which reduces the shear. A third simulation follows many cloudlets of $64^2$ cells each, with a filling fraction of about $2 \times 10^{-2}$, in a wind (Fig. 6). They are disturbed and partly disrupted, but do not mix rapidly into the wind. The cold gas coalesces and breaks apart again, its clumping factor stays high, and it ends up largely comoving with the hot gas, helped by the shielding, by mixed gas that cools again in the wakes and perhaps by streaming instabilities. For cloudlets with $R_0 \sim c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{crush} \sim v/c_\mathrm{s}$ (eq. 3), so cloudlets should survive preferentially where the flow is subsonic, as the velocities in the simulation suggest. Whether this holds in three dimensions is left open. **[GK]** With $t_\mathrm{crush} \sim \chi^{1/2} R_0/v$ and $\chi^{1/2} = c_\mathrm{s,hot}/c_\mathrm{s,cold}$ in pressure balance, the sound speed in eq. 3 is that of the hot gas when $R_0 \sim c_\mathrm{s,cold}\,t_\mathrm{cool}$.

#### 2.4.5 Evidence and caveats of shattering

[McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018, §3) read several puzzles of cold gas as evidence for shattering. Absorption towards background quasars finds cold gas in the haloes of galaxies, chiefly at redshifts of about 2 and above, that is dense ($\sim 1\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$), with path lengths of 1 to 100 pc and volume filling fractions of $10^{-3}$ to $10^{-4}$, yet lies in nearly every sightline within the virial radius, and fluorescent emission shows it filling the halo (§3.1, Table 1). A fog of cloudlets with $f_A/f_V \sim R_\mathrm{vir}/\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet} \gtrsim 10^6$ explains both. The densities agree with pressure balance in the hot halo, the columns always exceed $10^{17}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$, and below about $10^{18}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$ the lines split into components of thermal width. Smooth lines far broader than thermal, in haloes and near quasars, would need supersonic turbulence that shocks and heats the cold gas quickly, while the random motions of many entrained cloudlets give them naturally (§3.2, §3.3). High-velocity clouds show structure down to the limits of resolution, and absorbers of about $10^{17}\,\mathrm{cm^{-2}}$ with thermal widths (§3.4). In galactic winds, cold gas moves at high speed close to the base (§3.5). For a single cloud, acceleration takes $t_\mathrm{acc} \sim (\rho_\mathrm{cloud}/\rho_\mathrm{hot})\,R_\mathrm{cloud}/v_\mathrm{rel}$ (eq. 6a), longer by $(\rho_\mathrm{cloud}/\rho_\mathrm{hot})^{1/2}$ than destruction, $t_\mathrm{crush} \sim (\rho_\mathrm{cloud}/\rho_\mathrm{hot})^{1/2}\,R_\mathrm{cloud}/v_\mathrm{rel}$ (eq. 6b), which is the problem of entrainment ([Zhang et al.](#zhang-et-al-2017) 2017; 2.1.1). Shattering lowers the column through the cold gas and raises its ratio of surface to mass, like a parachute unfurling, which strengthens the drag and couples the phases. This assumes that little gas mixes. The authors expect mixing to be strongly inhibited once $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ is resolved and numerical instabilities are controlled, since cooling suppresses the small-scale mixing of the KH instability ([Mellema, Kurk and Röttgering](#mellema-et-al-2002) 2002; [Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009). In a laboratory experiment, a bow shock fragmented by thermal instability into clumps of order $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ (§4; [Suzuki-Vidal et al.](#suzuki-vidal-et-al-2015) 2015).

The authors list the assumptions that future work must test (§4.1). The mean free path in the hot gas, about 3 pc in the CGM, exceeds the size of the cloudlets, with $\sigma \equiv \lambda_\mathrm{mfp}^\mathrm{(hot)}/\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet} \sim 300\,(T/10^6\,\mathrm{K})^2$ in pressure balance (eq. 7), so that a fluid description of the hot gas on the scale of the cloudlets is in doubt, while it holds in the cold gas (footnote 13). They assume it holds, pointing to draped magnetic fields, plasma instabilities that shorten the mean free path, the success of MHD in galaxy clusters and the observed small structures, and leave the test to particle-in-cell simulations. Conduction would evaporate bare cloudlets. In the regimes of [Cowie and McKee](#cowie-mckee-1977) (1977), set by $\sigma_0' \equiv 1.84\,\lambda_\mathrm{mfp}^\mathrm{(hot)}/\ell_\mathrm{cloudlet}$, the cloudlets lie in the saturated or, more often, the two-fluid regime. A saturated flux $Q \sim 5 P c_\mathrm{s}$ heats a cloudlet within $t_\mathrm{heat} \sim (1/15)(c_\mathrm{s}^\mathrm{(cold)}/c_\mathrm{s}^\mathrm{(hot)})\,t_\mathrm{cool}$ (eq. 8a), and hot electrons that penetrate it heat it by Coulomb collisions within $t_\mathrm{heat} \sim 10^{-2}\,t_\mathrm{cool}\,(T_\mathrm{hot}/10^7\,\mathrm{K})^{3/2}$ (eq. 8b), both far below the cooling time. Three-dimensional simulations with diffusive conduction agree ([Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016). The authors therefore assume conduction to be suppressed by magnetic fields or plasma instabilities. Conduction is observationally unconstrained, and even a monolithic absorber of about 20 pc needs it suppressed, since it lies far below the mean free path, $(250\,\mathrm{pc})\,T_7^2 n_{-3}^{-1}$, and the Field length, given as $(80\,\mathrm{kpc})\,T_7^2 n_{-3}^{-1/2}$, with $T_7 = T/10^7\,\mathrm{K}$ and $n_{-3} = n/10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$. The cold gas outshines the hot gas, $L_\mathrm{cold}/L_\mathrm{hot} \sim 100$ for a density contrast $\delta \sim 10^3$ and a cold mass fraction $f_M \sim 10^{-2}$ (eq. 9). If conduction fed this radiation, the hot gas would cool within $t_\mathrm{cool}^\mathrm{(hot)} \sim (\delta/f_M)\,t_\mathrm{cool}^\mathrm{(cold)} \sim 5 \times 10^7$ yr (eq. 10), and the hydrostatic atmosphere would collapse. Cold clouds that evaporate and re-form continuously would thus cool the halo too efficiently. **[GK]** At fixed temperature the Field length scales as $n^{-1}$ by its definition (2.6.3), so the exponent $-1/2$ appears to be a slip.

Magnetic pressure could inhibit shattering where it, rather than thermal pressure, supports the cold gas, and for $\beta \ll 1$ allow isochoric cooling without shattering. More likely, fields make shattering anisotropic. Photoionisation strongly reduces cooling below about $10^5$ K in tenuous gas that is optically thin to ionising radiation, as at low redshift. If shattering stops there, $N_\mathrm{cloudlet}$ rises by an order of magnitude. Some absorbers at low redshift, with low inferred densities and large columns, fit a monolithic absorber better. Collisions and coagulation may spread the cloudlets over a range of masses, as with fragments of the Jeans mass, and underresolved simulations are prone to artificial coagulation. Below $10^4$ K the authors conjecture that gas cools isobarically without shattering further, since the minimum of $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ lies near $10^4$ K. Cosmological simulations, with a resolution of about a kiloparsec in haloes, cannot resolve shattering, and may therefore mix and destroy cold gas artificially (§4.2). The authors advocate a model of the cold gas below the grid, like those of stars, with source and sink terms and a drag force that couples it to the hot phase (2.6.6). The poor mixing of older SPH or low-resolution codes may even represent the two phases better (footnote 18).

#### 2.4.6 Clouds of droplets in turbulence

In the turbulent boxes of [Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) (2022, §4.6; 2.3.10), the cold gas splits into droplets, identified as regions of at least 16 cells below $2T_\mathrm{floor}$ (footnote 8). Once droplets are numerous, their cumulative mass distribution tends to $N(>m) \propto m^{-1}$, or $\mathrm{d}N/\mathrm{d}m \propto m^{-2}$, about equal mass per logarithmic interval (Figs. 12, 13). Half the mass may sit in droplets smaller than the initial cloud, but these dominate the covering fraction. The projected area and the surface of a droplet grow with its volume as $A \propto V^{\delta}$, with $\delta \approx 0.8$, steeper than the Euclidean $2/3$ and close to $D/3 = 0.83$ for the fractal dimension $D \approx 2.5$ of mixing layers (2.2.7). Lagrangian tracers show that growth comes from many small clumps rather than a few large ones, since clumps up to about twice the initial radius supply over half of it (Fig. 14). Breakups and coagulations, transitions to below half or above twice the prior size, make up only about 10 per cent at any size, and most growth is proportional, $\dot m \propto m$ (Figs. 15, 16). A heuristic argument from the social sciences turns proportional growth above a lower barrier into Zipf's law, a cumulative slope of $-1$, but it assumes a pure power law in normalised units, and slopes can differ (§5.4). Monte-Carlo simulations of the histories of the tracers show that coagulation into the largest clump, with the measured fraction of 0.01 to 0.1, turns the log-normal distribution of pure proportional growth towards the power law, over a dynamic range wider than the simulations cover (Fig. 20, Appendix B).

The authors therefore picture the cold CGM differently from the fog of [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018; 2.4.3), whose droplets lie below $r_\mathrm{crit}$ and would not survive ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, §5.3). They propose clouds of droplets around a core larger than $r_\mathrm{crit,turb}$ (2.6.11) that keeps the system alive, more like terrestrial clouds. Such clouds of droplets have large covering but small filling fractions and small relative velocities. They may thus fit the narrow absorption lines, with turbulent widths of 10 to $30\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, and the constraints of fast radio bursts against a mist of 0.1 pc clouds, better than a fog that fills the halo.

### 2.5 Measures of mixing

#### 2.5.1 Thermal state, kinematics and morphology

Most simulations of multiphase gas ask, in some form, for the thermal state of the medium ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.3). The full distribution of the gas in the plane of temperature and density captures it (Fig. 2, right). In practice it is often binned into ranges of temperature, which give the mass and the volume of each phase. The ranges differ between studies and between fields, which makes their results harder to compare. For the evolution in time, the fluxes between the phases, of mass, $\dot m$, or of momentum, $\dot p$, are of particular interest, and at the coarsest level whether a phase exists or vanishes.
