The kinematics of the phases, how they move with respect to each other, is measured by the mean difference of velocity between two phases, $\Delta v$, weighted by mass or taken at given places, or by the distribution of the gas in temperature and velocity ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.3). The turbulent motions are analysed through velocity structure functions (VSF) and spectra of kinetic power. The spatial distribution and morphology of the phases link simulations to observations, through the filling fractions of area and volume, the distributions of the sizes of clumps and the coherence of the phases, which also show how gas fragments and mixes. Codes of radiative transfer, such as SKIRT ([Camps and Baes](#camps-baes-2015) 2015) and Trident ([Hummels, Smith and Silvia](#hummels-et-al-2017) 2017), turn simulations into synthetic spectra, to be compared with the lines of sight towards quasars and with other spectral diagnostics.

The review counts the variety of diagnostics among the challenges common to the field ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.3). It differs between communities that analyse their results differently, and still more between simulations of different kinds, so that it remains open how set-ups of different scales can inform each other. Models below the grid (2.6.6), adaptive refinement and so-called handshake simulations may bridge the scales. Since observations remain the benchmark, models of radiative transfer and tools for synthetic observations are essential for quantitative comparisons.

#### 2.5.2 Global quantities of a shocked cloud

[Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994, §3) compress each simulation into global quantities, integrals over the cloud material of mass $m_\mathrm{c}$. From the moments $\langle r^2 \rangle$, $\langle z \rangle$ and $\langle z^2 \rangle$, weighted by the density $\rho_\mathrm{c}$ of cloud material (eqs. 3.4–3.6), they define the effective radius across the axis, $a = [\frac{5}{2}\langle r^2 \rangle]^{1/2}$, and along it, $c = [5(\langle z^2 \rangle - \langle z \rangle^2)]^{1/2}$ (eqs. 3.7, 3.8), whose factors make them the true radii of a uniform ellipsoid, and the aspect ratio $c/a$. Further quantities are the mean density $\langle \rho_\mathrm{c} \rangle = m_\mathrm{c}/V_\mathrm{c}$, with the volume $V_\mathrm{c}$ occupied by cloud material, the mean velocity of the cloud relative to the shocked intercloud gas, its dispersions along and across the axis, and the circulation $\Gamma = \int \boldsymbol{\omega} \cdot d\boldsymbol{A}$. The mean density is hard to compute for $\chi > 10$, since at late times much of the cloud mass lies in zones that also hold intercloud gas, whose share of the volume the two-fluid code knows less well than their mass. Four times describe the evolution. The drag time $t_\mathrm{drag}$ is the time by which the relative velocity has fallen by a factor $e$. The destruction time $t_\mathrm{dest}$ is the time by which the mass of the core, the gas connected to the main fragment on the axis, has fallen to $1/e$ of the initial mass. The time $t_\mathrm{m}$ is the time at which the radius across the axis first comes within 10 per cent of its largest value. The mixing time $t_\mathrm{mix}$ is the time at which the mix fraction, the fraction of the cloud mass in zones that hold both cloud and intercloud material, reaches one half (§8). The destruction time can jump when a few zones decide whether the core splits, while the mixing time, although its definition depends on the resolution, converges (2.6.7).

#### 2.5.3 Mixing in pressure–entropy space

[Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) (2022, §2.1) reduce the state of a cloud in a wind to the distribution of its mass over pressure $p$ and entropy $K = p\rho^{-\gamma}$, with $\gamma = 5/3$. Pressure suits a problem that stays nearly isobaric, apart from transient perturbations seeded by the wind. Entropy, unlike temperature, needs no mean molecular weight, so that it describes the dynamics free of scale, and it changes only through irreversible processes: shocks and mixing raise it, cooling lowers it, and compression or expansion leave it unchanged. Without shocks, fluid elements thus move continuously through the $p$–$K$ plane. The cloud starts at $K_\mathrm{cl}$ and the wind at $K_\mathrm{w} = \chi^{5/3} K_\mathrm{cl}$, and plotting $\log_\chi(K/K_\mathrm{cl})$, which runs from 0 to $5/3$, removes most of the dependence on $\chi$ (Figs. 1, 3).

The model follows the gas that starts in the cloud, marked by a passive scalar of total mass $M_\mathrm{ps}$, through its distribution $\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K$ and the mean Lagrangian rate of change $\dot K(K, t)$ of its fluid elements at each entropy ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022, §2.2). Without cooling, mixing dominates $\dot K$, written $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$, and since the problem is free of scale, $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$ in units of $K_\mathrm{cl}/t_\mathrm{cc}$ depends only on $\gamma$, $\chi$, $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}$, the plasma beta $\beta$ and the initial geometry. The conservation of mass gives a continuity equation in entropy, $\partial_t(\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K) + \partial_K(\dot K\,\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K) = 0$ (eq. 3). Integrated over bins of $K$ between snapshots $t_\mathrm{cc}/2$ apart, it yields the fluxes $\dot K\,\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K$ at the edges of the bins from the changes of their masses, starting from an empty lowest bin, and division by an interpolated profile gives $\dot K$ (§3.2, eqs. 4 to 6). Measurements are discarded where the profile changes too much between the snapshots, in bins with less than 1 per cent of the initial mass, and once 1 per cent of the scalar has left the domain.

Runs without cooling at 8 cells per radius vary $\chi$ from 100 to 1000, $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w}$ from 0.75 to 4.5 and the $\beta$ of a transverse field from 10 to 1000 ([Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) 2022, §4, Table 1). The initial shock hardly raises the entropy of the cloud. Mixing is its main source and moves the gas steadily from $K_\mathrm{cl}$ to $K_\mathrm{w}$, and most of the gas falls below the common threshold $\rho_\mathrm{cl}/3$ by about $4.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Fig. 3). The pressure follows a common course: large perturbations from the shock, partial damping by $2.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, a second dip between 3.5 and $4.5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, then calm. The underpressured gas is held up by the vorticity of the shock, of the shear layer and of the vortex rings, and in the magnetised runs by magnetic stress. With time in units of $t_\mathrm{cc}$ and entropy in $\log_\chi$, the profiles $\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K$ nearly coincide for all $\chi$ and all supersonic winds, while a subsonic wind of $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} = 0.75$ mixes differently (Fig. 5). $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$ follows roughly a power law in $K/K_\mathrm{cl}$, of slope near 1 for $0.3 \lesssim \log_\chi(K/K_\mathrm{cl}) \lesssim 1.4$ (Fig. 6), so that the time in which mixing doubles the entropy, $K/\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$, lies within a factor of about 2.5 of $t_\mathrm{cc}$ (Fig. 7). For hydrodynamics, $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$ hardly depends on time and can be replaced by a characteristic $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing,char}(K)$. Transverse fields depart from this course after about $3\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. At $\beta = 10$ the mixing slows markedly and the slope of $\dot K_\mathrm{mixing}$ flattens, presumably as the field is tangled, which the model captures where $t_\mathrm{cc}$ alone does not.

The authors see three uses of the model (§6.1). It describes destruction where $t_\mathrm{cc}$ fails, as under magnetic draping or for networks of small clouds without a single radius. It compares cooling with mixing (2.3.8). And it can be extended, for instance by the velocity along the wind as a third axis, or by following the gas of the wind as well. It drops the geometry, however (§6.2). A turbulent cloud is destroyed faster without cooling, which would predict stricter conditions for growth, whereas turbulent clouds survive as spherical ones do and grow faster ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a). How the model treats phases with different non-thermal support is also open.

### 2.6 Numerical resolution and convergence

#### 2.6.1 The difficulty of multiphase media

The contrasts of temperature in astrophysics, and the contrasts of density that come with them, range from about $10^2$ to $\gtrsim 10^{10}$, which strains numerical methods and makes adaptive techniques such as smoothed particle hydrodynamics (SPH) problematic ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.2). The phases have different length scales, so the problem is multi-scale. In the CGM and the ICM, cold gas can moreover lie in very small structures spread over a large volume. Adaptive mesh refinement (AMR), which adapts the size of the cells to the local density, then becomes inadequate or too expensive. Observations find cold structures in the CGM as small as tens of parsecs. With at least eight cells across a structure (footnote 5), a halo of the mass of the Milky Way would need $(8 \times 100\,\mathrm{kpc}/10\,\mathrm{pc})^3 \approx 5 \times 10^{14}$ resolution elements, of order $10^{15}$, against about $10^{11}$ in the largest simulations of fixed resolution to date (footnote 6, §3.1). Multiphase quantities such as the mass of cold gas are therefore not converged in typical cosmological simulations ([Faucher-Giguère et al.](#faucher-giguere-et-al-2016) 2016), as simulations that refine the CGM systematically have shown (§2.8.3).

The physics adds to the difficulty ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.2). Turbulence mixes and destroys the phases, and radiative cooling and heating exchange mass and momentum between them. Thermal conduction and viscosity shape the temperature and the turbulence, metals, molecules and their chemistry set the cooling, and magnetic fields and cosmic rays add pressure and act on the mixing directly. The nature of the heating and of the drivers of turbulence matters as well. Since no simulation can yet treat all the scales and processes together, most address particular questions or systems.

#### 2.6.2 No universal criterion

For gravitational collapse, the criterion of [Truelove et al.](#truelove-et-al-1997) (1997) sets a clear and widely adopted demand on resolution, but no single criterion holds for multiphase flows ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §1.3). The scale to resolve depends on the quantity of interest. Converged rates of mass transfer, morphologies or kinematics generally need the characteristic scale of the process resolved by at least a few cells. §2 of the review names these scales together with the diagnostics that they make converge, and the subjects of this synthesis take them up in turn. **[GK]** The criterion of [Truelove et al.](#truelove-et-al-1997) (1997) asks that the Jeans length span at least four cells.

#### 2.6.3 Mixing layers

In mixing layers, the largest eddies limit mixing and cooling (2.2.3), so that larger simulations need only the driving scale of a layer resolved for its rate of mass transfer to converge ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.1). A comparison with observations of ions such as O VI needs in addition the Field length, after [Field](#field-1965) (1965), to be resolved, or its effect to be added in post-processing. With the conduction of [Spitzer](#spitzer-1962) (1962), the Field length shrinks steeply towards low temperatures, as $T^{5/2}$ by the review, so a simulation may leave it unresolved below a cutoff temperature. [Tan and Oh](#tan-oh-2021) (2021) showed that the distribution of temperature converges only above this cutoff (footnote 10). [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020) showed that a layer that does not resolve the smallest value of $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, the shattering length that §2.4 of the review treats, develops numerical dips of pressure across it (footnote 9). Such simulations must also reach a steady state in a box wide enough across the flow (2.2.2). The Field length, $\lambda_\mathrm{F} = [\kappa T/(n^2\Lambda)]^{1/2}$, on which conduction balances cooling, grows at fixed pressure as $(T^{3+n}/\Lambda)^{1/2}$ for a conductivity $\kappa \propto T^n$, so as $T^{11/4}\Lambda^{-1/2}$ for Spitzer conduction ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, eqs. 14, 16; 2.2.8). **[GK]** The power $5/2$ that the review gives thus holds where $\Lambda \propto T^{1/2}$, as for bremsstrahlung.

The mixing layers of [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019, §3.3, Fig. 13) give the same columns of C IV, O VI and Si IV at $128 \times 128 \times 384$ cells and at twice that resolution, also with strong cooling. With magnetic fields, the finer runs peak lower, since they resolve the vorticity better and so amplify the field and suppress the mixing earlier, but their final columns agree (Fig. 14). The authors add no explicit conduction, so numerical diffusion brings the mixed gas in a cell to one temperature (footnote 11). They note that explicit conduction would be needed to guarantee convergence, as in simulations of thermal instability ([Koyama and Inutsuka](#koyama-inutsuka-2004) 2004), but find their results converged, likely through turbulent diffusion of heat.

[Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020, §4, Fig. 5) compare resolutions from 16 to 512 cells per streamwise length $L$. At low resolution the pressure–entropy distribution dips by up to 30 per cent at the low and intermediate entropies where cooling peaks, while finer runs cool isobarically. The authors trace the dips to numerical diffusion, which broadens the cooling layer artificially. The dips grow with $\xi$ and $\chi$ and can drive spurious turbulence, perhaps also in the coarser simulations of [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) (2020a). The total cooling is right within a factor of 2 even at the lowest resolution, in either direction, and converged for $\Delta x \lesssim L/128$. The paper does not phrase the requirement through $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, as footnote 9 of the review does.

#### 2.6.4 Convergence without the Field length

[Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021, §3.2, Fig. 3) test the conventional view that thermal fronts converge only with explicit conduction and a resolved Field length ([Koyama and Inutsuka](#koyama-inutsuka-2004) 2004) on steady one-dimensional fronts, over four orders of magnitude in resolution. With conduction, the surface brightness converges to the analytic value once the Field length is resolved, and grows as $Q \propto \Delta^{1/2}$ at coarse cells $\Delta$. Without conduction only numerical diffusion balances cooling, and $Q \propto \Delta^{1/2}$ at all resolutions, vanishing as $\Delta \to 0$. Shortening the Field length by weaker conduction makes $Q$ depend on resolution once $\Delta \gtrsim \lambda_\mathrm{F}$. Shortening it by a cooling 128 times stronger leaves the mean $Q$ close to the converged value up to $\Delta \sim 100\,\lambda_\mathrm{F}$, and instead makes it oscillate in time, the more the coarser the grid. An unresolved front, a stiff source term, thus adds variance that time averages remove, while numerical diffusion adds a bias. The criterion for convergence is therefore not $\Delta < \lambda_\mathrm{F}$ but that numerical diffusion stay below thermal diffusion, $D_\mathrm{num} < D_\mathrm{cond}$, with $D_\mathrm{cond} \sim \kappa/(\rho c_\mathrm{P})$ (§6.2). The authors relate this to stiff relaxation systems, whose wave speeds may be spurious or merely dispersed, and to the treatment of stiff source terms in Athena++. Since the Field length grows steeply with temperature, $Q$ stays nearly right as long as $\lambda_\mathrm{F}$ at $10^5$ K is resolved, here about 30 times that at $10^4$ K.

In three dimensions the inflow and the surface brightness converge without any conduction ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §5.6, Fig. 17). From a quarter to twice the fiducial cell of 0.78 pc, the fiducial cooling is converged. With the strongest cooling, coarser grids add oscillations of period about $t_\mathrm{cool}$ and an apparent broadening of the interface, but the mean $Q$ stays near the converged value. The authors argue that this holds whenever the outer scale of the turbulence is well resolved, $L \gg \Delta$, so that the turbulent diffusivity $u' L$ exceeds the numerical $v\,\Delta$. It is then the outer scale of the turbulence that must be resolved, not the width of the laminar front, which relaxes the demands of simulations of galaxies by up to orders of magnitude, although that scale itself may be of parsecs (§6.2). The fronts converge by the same argument with conduction too, as long as explicit diffusion exceeds the numerical. **[GK]** §6.2 writes the inflow of a front without conduction as $v_\mathrm{in} \sim (D_\mathrm{num} t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$, which is a length; $(D_\mathrm{num}/t_\mathrm{cool})^{1/2}$, as in §4.1, gives the stated $\Delta^{1/2}$.

#### 2.6.5 Clouds in winds

What convergence means for the crushing of clouds, and when it is reached, has long been debated ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). [Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994) found that their two-dimensional, adiabatic simulations needed about 100 cells per cloud radius for accurate results, by the review 100 to 200 (2.6.7). [Nakamura et al.](#nakamura-et-al-2006) (2006, fig. 1) showed that the shape and displacement of the cloud converge to within 10 per cent of their finest simulation for $\gtrsim 100$ cells per radius. Experiments with the Nova laser offered a benchmark beyond comparisons with finer simulations ([Klein et al.](#klein-et-al-2000) 2000, [2003](#klein-et-al-2003); footnote 14). For radiatively cooling clouds, [Yirak, Frank and Cunningham](#yirak-et-al-2010) (2010) found no clear convergence of the same global quantities up to 1536 cells per radius, which they ascribed to changes in the structure of the radiative shock.

In three-dimensional radiative simulations, scans of resolution typically span 8 to 128 cells per radius ([Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009; [Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) 2015; [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017). The detailed morphology, such as the number of fragments ([Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009), stays unconverged, but the overall rate at which the phases mix converges, although its evolution can be stochastic and non-monotonic in the resolution. Studies that find growth of the cold mass accordingly report modest demands: about 8 cells per radius appear to capture the growth ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018; [Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2022) 2022). These tests were mostly run for transonic flows, $\mathcal{M} \sim 1.5$, and overdensities $\chi \sim 100$. [Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) (2022) showed that the growth converges less readily at higher Mach numbers, but no systematic study exists. As for mixing layers (2.6.3), the distribution of temperature converges much less readily, and likely needs the Field length to be resolved ([Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2022) 2022).

The review asks how these demands map onto the critical radius $r_\mathrm{crit}$ (2.3.1), below which cold gas is quickly mixed away and above which it can grow ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). If $r_\mathrm{crit}$ sets the minimum size of long-lived structures, a simulation that does not resolve it may evolve qualitatively wrongly in the long term, so that $r_\mathrm{crit}$ may offer a criterion of convergence founded in physics. The same holds for the critical radius of streams (2.3.6).

#### 2.6.6 The gap to galaxies and models below the grid

Even with growing computing power, about four orders of magnitude separate the resolutions feasible today from those needed to resolve the multiphase gas of a single halo like that of the Milky Way ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.1; 2.6.1). To resolve both the Field length, which governs the distribution of gas at intermediate temperatures (2.6.3) and can lie below a parsec, and the inflows of cold gas through haloes of hundreds of kiloparsecs takes more than six orders of magnitude in length, or 18 in mass (§3.2). Large simulations therefore need models below the grid, as they do for feedback and star formation. Classical models of the multiphase ISM were early examples. Newer ones treat the phases more explicitly, often as several fluids, and reach into the CGM and the ICM: two-phase schemes in smoothed particle hydrodynamics, a cold phase decoupled from the hot medium, cold particles embedded in cells of hot gas, and two interpenetrating fluids. Particles can represent several bulk flows in a cell but struggle with the dispersion of velocities. Two fluids represent the dispersion and any filling fraction, but only one mean velocity per phase.

The review names four challenges for such models ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.2). A model must give converged results whether or not the colder phase is resolved, which calls for comparisons with fully resolved simulations of idealised set-ups and for coupling terms founded in physics with few free parameters. Its coupling terms rest on small-scale simulations whose uncertainties, such as the effects of viscosity and conduction on mixing, carry over, and current models omit magnetic fields and mostly treat two phases. It must work together with the models of feedback and star formation of cosmological simulations and stay affordable without much tuning. Since it does not follow the unresolved morphology of the cold gas or the gas at intermediate temperatures, tracers such as O VI must be painted on in post-processing.

#### 2.6.7 Resolution in the shock–cloud problem

[Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994) measure resolution by $R_n$, $n$ zones of the finest level per initial cloud radius (§3, Table 1). Convergence studies, announced for a second paper, showed that about 100 zones per radius are needed to represent the hydrodynamics adequately, about three times more than earlier calculations used, and that $R_{120}$ gives accurate results (abstract, §1, §3). Radiative losses need still more, since they compress the cloud more. Their runs span $R_{30}$ to $R_{240}$, most at $R_{60}$ or $R_{120}$. Some quantities converge and others do not. The circulation converges, since numerical viscosity does not affect it significantly, while the peak vorticity, set by the thickness of the shocks and shear layers, scales as $(\Delta x)^{-1}$ from $R_{30}$ to $R_{240}$ (§7.2). The mixing time converges although its definition rests on the grid, because the large-scale growth of the instabilities sets the time of fragmentation (§8). The Richtmyer–Meshkov instability, seeded by the mesh, loses importance as the resolution grows (§5.1). **[GK]** The second paper of the series that appeared, [Nakamura et al.](#nakamura-et-al-2006) (2006), treats smooth cloud boundaries (2.6.5) rather than this study of convergence.

#### 2.6.8 Resolving shattering

[McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018, Appendix B) find shattering particularly sensitive to resolution, which may explain why not every simulation with cooling fragments to the grid scale. They define clumps as contiguous cells with at least half the initial density of the cloud, and measure their size as the square root of their area, in cells (footnote 19). Behind a cloud of $R_0 = 10^4\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, mixed gas at about $10^5$ K is thermally unstable and cools into clumps at roughly the grid scale (Fig. 8). Raising the resolution fourfold, from 150 to 600 cells per cloud radius, leaves the distribution of sizes unchanged in units of cells, so that all clumps shrink with the cells. Even clumps of about 10 cells across are thus unresolved, their fragmentation set by the grid, and only fragments of about 20 cells or more can split. The cutoff depends on the method, but should be typical of current grid codes. About 150 cells per cloud radius are needed to see the repeated fragmentation that leads to shattering in two dimensions, and somewhat fewer in three, according to work then in preparation, against the 32 to 64 of typical simulations of cloud crushing. Since most studies follow clouds with $R_0 \gg c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, their cloudlets are essentially always unresolved. The resolution study at 150, 300 and 600 cells per radius, for clouds of 30 and $100\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, does not converge, nor is it expected to, since gradients stay unresolved and no explicit dissipation is included (Fig. 7). The knots of cold gas nevertheless appear to track $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$.

The authors weigh whether shattering could be numerical (Appendix B). Grid codes of Godunov type suffer an artificial instability that likely overestimates destruction and mixing ([Lecoanet et al.](#lecoanet-et-al-2015) 2015), which the simulations do not control, since that would be prohibitively expensive even in two dimensions. Three findings point to a physical process. The size of the cloudlets does not depend on the density contrast, on which the shear instabilities and their errors depend strongly. It changes little with resolution, whereas the errors of this set-up grow with it. And shattering occurs at a physical length and time, $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ and $t_\mathrm{cool}$, which can be resolved in a small enough domain, while numerical errors should grow fastest at the grid scale. The diffusive integrator chosen (3.7) also resists numerical instabilities, and the less diffusive default gives similar results. Simulations with other codes and set-ups show similar fragmentation driven by cooling ([Mellema, Kurk and Röttgering](#mellema-et-al-2002) 2002; [Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009; [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017, among others). The high-resolution two-dimensional simulations of [Audit and Hennebelle](#audit-hennebelle-2005) (2005) fragment rapidly to the grid scale, an upper limit since they were not designed to resolve $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$. The one-dimensional runs of single modes of [Burkert and Lin](#burkert-lin-2000) (2000) may impose too much symmetry to fragment, and stop after about one cooling time, while shattering likely takes somewhat longer. Whether fragmentation is possible in one dimension at all is unclear, and shattering likely is not.

#### 2.6.9 Resolution and domain of growing clouds

[Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018, §3, §5.2) run a suite at 8 cells per cloud radius, since they aim at the global evolution of the cold mass, and test convergence up to 64, the resolution of the latest three-dimensional studies ([Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017). They call 8 cells too few for the detailed evolution. [Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994) and [Nakamura et al.](#nakamura-et-al-2006) (2006) suggest $\gtrsim 100$ cells in two dimensions (2.6.5), [Yirak, Frank and Cunningham](#yirak-et-al-2010) (2010) found the structure of the shocks unconverged even beyond 1000, and [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018) ask for $c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ to be resolved (2.6.8), which these runs barely do for the mixed gas and not at all for the cold gas. For the default run, at $\chi = 100$ and $t_\mathrm{cool,cl}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim 10^{-3}$, the mass differs in detail at 8, 32 and 64 cells per radius, but doubles by about $8\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ in all three (Fig. 3). The growth is faster at higher resolution, which suggests that mixing drives it and that the low resolution underestimates it. A drastic change at still higher resolution cannot be ruled out, but the cloudlets of McCourt et al. survived and were entrained in two dimensions (2.4.4).

The domain matters as much ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) 2018, §4, §5.1). It must hold the tail where the cold gas re-forms. Mixed gas travels $t_\mathrm{cc} v_\mathrm{wind}$ before the cloud would be destroyed and $t_\mathrm{drag} v_\mathrm{wind}$ before it comoves, $\chi^{1/2}$ and $\chi$ cloud radii, which bound the length needed from below and above. A moving frame does not relax the lower bound, since the wind pushes the cloud only $\sim (v_\mathrm{wind}/t_\mathrm{drag})\,t_\mathrm{cc}^2 \sim r_\mathrm{cl}$ before it would be destroyed. The tails measured about $40\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ at $\chi = 100$ and $250\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ at $\chi = 1000$, nearer the upper bound, for $t_\mathrm{cool,cl}/t_\mathrm{cc} \ll 1$, and less efficient cooling needs longer domains. [Scannapieco and Brüggen](#scannapieco-bruggen-2015) (2015) tracked the cloud but kept only $8\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ downstream of its centre at $\chi \ge 300$. [Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) (2009) used a box of about $30\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ without tracking, while the run closest to theirs, at $\chi = 1000$ and $t_\mathrm{cool,mix}/t_\mathrm{cc} \sim 10^{-3}$, grew a tail of about $250\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ and had shifted its frame by about $45\,r_\mathrm{cl}$ by $10\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, when the growth set in.

#### 2.6.10 Convergence of the cloud–wind runs of Abruzzo et al.

[Abruzzo, Bryan and Fielding](#abruzzo-bryan-fielding-2022) (2022, Appendix C) repeat four set-ups, without cooling, with slow cooling and with the two fast coolings, at 8, 16, 32 and 64 cells per cloud radius. The cold mass, the share of it that began in the cloud and its mean velocity converge remarkably, so that the net effect of mixing depends only weakly on resolution (Fig. 22), in line with the modest demands of 2.6.5. The details do change. The rate of change of entropy of single fluid elements varies wildly, and the profiles $\mathrm{d}M_\mathrm{ps}/\mathrm{d}K$ vary most in bins with less than 1 per cent of the initial mass (Figs. 23, 24). Measurements of $\dot K$ hold where the gas in a bin began mostly in the cloud, as in the adiabatic and slow runs, and less so in fast cooling, where gas from the wind joins the cold phase. The cooling length $\ell_\mathrm{cool}$ is resolved at all resolutions only in the slow run, and barely, by 1.7 cells, in the fast run at $10^4$ K at 64 cells per radius. Clouds larger than $\ell_\mathrm{cool}$ can shatter (2.4), but by [Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020b) (2020b) only in winds of $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} \gtrsim 1.6$ at $\chi = 100$ when the gas cannot cool below $T_\mathrm{cl}$, so that these clouds should not shatter. Resolving $\ell_\mathrm{cool}$ may still matter through its link to growth. The integrator matters as well (Appendix A.2). The dimensionally split PPM integrator with third-order reconstruction, ported from Enzo, grows the cold mass faster than VL+CT (3.9), but tends towards the converged curves as the resolution rises, and the trends between runs hold for either integrator.

[Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2023) (2023, §5) find 16 cells per cloud radius enough for the convergence of the cold mass in most cases. Convergence is harder at $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} = 6$ ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a) or at $\chi = 10^4$ ([Abruzzo, Fielding and Bryan](#abruzzo-fielding-bryan-2022) 2022). At $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{w} = 6$, the cold mass of the run taken from the latter converged at low resolution, and its minimum scales monotonically with $R_\mathrm{cl}$. The runs at $\chi = 10^4$ may not have converged, but the conclusions do not rest on them.

#### 2.6.11 Convergence in turbulent multiphase media

The turbulent runs of [Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) (2022, §3, §4.7) aim at 16 cells across the cold gas, with checks from about 4 to 64. Their outcome is stochastic: the same set-up with another random seed of the stirring can grow at very different rates or die, so that effects on growth can be studied only statistically (Fig. 17). Near the threshold, at $r \sim 3\,r_\mathrm{crit}$, both the stochasticity and the dependence on resolution are large, and runs at 1/8 of the mass resolution destroy cold gas faster (footnote 7). For $r \gg r_\mathrm{crit}$ both fall markedly, although $r_\mathrm{crit}$ is not resolved. Since the droplets carry equal mass per logarithmic interval (2.4.6), the total cold mass is only logarithmically sensitive to its lower cutoff. Convergence thus needs a sufficient dynamic range between the largest clumps and that cutoff, set by $r_\mathrm{crit}$ or by the grid, rather than a resolved $r_\mathrm{crit}$.

For $T_\mathrm{cold} \sim 10^4$ K, $\chi \sim 100$ and $\mathcal{M} \lesssim 1$, the survival criterion becomes $r > r_\mathrm{crit,turb} \equiv 8\,\mathrm{pc}\;T_\mathrm{cl,4}^{5/2}(P_3\Lambda_\mathrm{mix,-21.4})^{-1}\,\mathcal{M}\,[f(\mathcal{M})/0.25]^{-1}(\chi/100)$, with the empirical $f(\mathcal{M}) \sim 10^{-0.6\mathcal{M}}$ ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022, §5.1, eq. 9). Its dependence on $\mathcal{M}$ is opposite to that of classical cloud crushing, where faster winds compress the cloud and prolong its life. The authors ascribe this to a more violent fragmentation and dispersion in stirred boxes: the time a cloud needs to recover from a shock, about $t_\mathrm{grow}$, grows against the interval between shocks, $t_\mathrm{eddy}$, as $\mathcal{M}^{1/2}$. A cloud smaller than $r_\mathrm{crit,turb}$ is destroyed anyway and need not be resolved, but seeds above it must be. Cosmological simulations, with resolutions of several hundred parsecs in the CGM, far above $r_\mathrm{crit,turb}$, are accordingly unconverged in their cold gas. In the sheets and filaments of the intergalactic medium, at lower pressure, $r_\mathrm{crit,turb} \sim 80$ pc at $\mathcal{M} \sim 1$, nearly within reach. Stratified discs need about 5 pc to resolve the warm and hot phases, close to the criterion, and simulations of clusters converge in total cold mass but not in the number of clumps, as a distribution $\mathrm{d}N/\mathrm{d}m \propto m^{-2}$ implies. The criteria hold for cold gas made by mixing from seeds that already exist, and making the seeds, by thermal instability or at radiative shocks, may demand more. Convergence without a resolved Field length, although numerical diffusion sets the fronts, agrees with [Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021): mixing, not diffusion, limits the transfer of mass (2.6.4).

#### 2.6.12 The resolution dependence of cloud–wind simulations

[Leary, Schneider and Richie](#leary-et-al-2024) (2024) test how resolution affects adiabatic clouds in a subsonic and a supersonic wind, at the low resolutions that matter for cosmological simulations. The growth rate of the KH instability in simulations depends on the smallest resolved scale, so mixing should depend on resolution, and finer grids are known to show more structure and more cloudlets ([Cooper et al.](#cooper-et-al-2009) 2009; [Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2017) 2017). Clouds of $\chi = 100$ in winds of 100 and $1000\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ are run at 4, 8, 16, 32 and 48 cells per cloud radius (3.11). Cloud material is gas denser than a third of the initial density of the cloud, and its mass and mass-weighted velocity are measured every $0.2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (eqs. 2, 3). Instabilities destroy the cloud within several $t_\mathrm{cc}$ at every resolution, but the trend with resolution is not monotonic (Fig. 1). In the subsonic wind, the cloud at 16 cells per radius survives longest and reaches the lowest terminal velocity, 23 per cent of the wind speed, and the destruction follows an almost parabolic trend around it. In the supersonic wind, the cloud at 16 cells is again the last to be destroyed, but the velocities order more monotonically, with the coarsest grids accelerating fastest. The finest runs reach the highest speeds, 35 per cent of the wind speed at 32 cells in both winds, and the results seem to converge at 32 cells. The early acceleration agrees at all resolutions, while the late velocities diverge. The authors read this as a change of mechanism. Ram pressure, which depends only on the area of the cloud, dominates at first, and mixing, which depends on resolution, takes over later. Since ram pressure scales as $\rho v^2$ and the rate of mixing only linearly with the shear velocity, ram pressure weighs a hundred times more in the faster wind, which may explain why the trends of the two winds differ.

[Leary, Richie and Schneider](#leary-et-al-2026) (2026, §1, §2) extend the study to clouds with radiative cooling and add a simple model of acceleration by ram pressure. The ram pressure of the wind, $P_\mathrm{ram} \sim \rho_\mathrm{w} v_\mathrm{w}^2$, pushes on the projected area of the cloud with a force $F_\mathrm{ram} \sim \pi R_\mathrm{cl}^2 P_\mathrm{ram}$. It accelerates the cloud at $\mathrm{d}v_\mathrm{cl}/\mathrm{d}t \sim \rho_\mathrm{w} v_\mathrm{w}^2/(\rho_\mathrm{cl} R_\mathrm{cl})$ and would entrain it within $t_\mathrm{ent,ram} = \rho_\mathrm{cl} R_\mathrm{cl}/(\rho_\mathrm{w} v_\mathrm{w})$, the drag time of 2.1.1, longer than $t_\mathrm{cc}$ by $\chi^{1/2}$ (eqs. 1 to 5). With the cooling time of the mixed gas defined as $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} = k_\mathrm{B} T_\mathrm{mix}/[n_\mathrm{mix}\Lambda(T_\mathrm{mix})]$ (eq. 6), the radiative runs have $t_\mathrm{cool,mix} = 9.39$ kyr against a $t_\mathrm{cc}$ of 4890 kyr in the subsonic and 489 kyr in the supersonic wind, ratios of $1.9 \times 10^{-3}$ and $1.9 \times 10^{-2}$ (Table 1).

The adiabatic runs repeat the note in more detail ([Leary, Richie and Schneider](#leary-et-al-2026) 2026, §3.1, §3.2, Figs. 1 to 3). In the subsonic wind, the remaining mass at first grows monotonically with resolution: by $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the cloud at 4 cells per radius has lost over half its mass, the cloud at 48 cells less than 30 per cent. After $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the order breaks down. The cloud at 16 cells keeps dense gas until $10\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, at about 20 per cent of the wind speed, while all others are destroyed by $7\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ after reaching 30 to 40 per cent. Until $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the velocities agree at all resolutions and follow the prediction of ram pressure, slightly below it because momentum also goes to gas too thin to count as cloud. In the supersonic wind, a bow shock forms, the cloud feels the post-shock state, and high Mach numbers suppress the KH instability ([Mandelker et al.](#mandelker-et-al-2016) 2016). The dependence on resolution is subtler there, and all clouds are destroyed within $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ of each other, by $7\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. A strong shock makes the wind four times denser and four times slower, which cuts the ram pressure fourfold, and the clouds follow that lower prediction for the first $t_\mathrm{cc}$ before mixing accelerates them faster. In both winds the finest grids reach the highest speeds, a sign that mixing, which grows with resolution, accelerates the gas.

With cooling, the subsonic runs agree at all resolutions ([Leary, Richie and Schneider](#leary-et-al-2026) 2026, §3.3, Figs. 4 to 6). The clouds grow to about 2.5 times their initial mass before gas leaves the box at 4 to $5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, and up to 4 times later, slightly more at 48 cells. They pass 60 per cent of the wind speed before gas leaves the box and reach about 80 per cent, with velocities that track each other and follow ram pressure for the first $t_\mathrm{cc}$. In the supersonic wind, nearer the regime of destruction, the clouds break up more, especially at high resolution, and the mass stays near its initial value until gas leaves the box at about $9\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ (§3.4). At first the finest grids gain mass and the coarsest lose it, but after $5\,t_\mathrm{cc}$ the trend reverses, and mass is lost the faster the higher the resolution. The velocities then rise with resolution, to 35 per cent of the wind speed at 48 cells against 25 per cent at 4. The early velocities exceed the prediction for the post-shock wind, likely because the post-shock region is underresolved and the cloud feels a mixture of the conditions before and behind the shock.

The authors conclude that ram pressure on the projected area accelerates the clouds independently of resolution for 1 to $2\,t_\mathrm{cc}$, after which mixing, sensitive to the smallest resolved scale, takes over ([Leary, Richie and Schneider](#leary-et-al-2026) 2026, §4.1). In the supersonic adiabatic wind, the force of ram pressure is 25 times that in the subsonic one, which may explain the smaller spread between resolutions there. Cooling in the wake lowers the pressure and keeps the radiative clouds in narrow filaments, while adiabatic debris disperses sideways. At 16 cells per radius in the subsonic wind, a combination of instabilities, with a plume of the Rayleigh–Taylor instability ahead of the cloud, may let a dense core survive longer, an effect they expect to weaken for inhomogeneous clouds. On resolution (§4.2), they recall that mixing layers need about four cells across the interface ([Koyama and Inutsuka](#koyama-inutsuka-2004) 2004; [Robertson et al.](#robertson-et-al-2010) 2010; [Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) 2020), that resolving $\ell_\mathrm{shatter}$ might be needed if shattering raises the area for mixing ([McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) 2018), and that growth in cloud crushing converged at 8 cells per radius regardless ([Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2020a) 2020a). In the subsonic regime with fast cooling, 4 cells per radius suffice for growth and acceleration. The KH instability, whose growth depends on the smallest resolved scale, drives destruction, while cooling dominates the entrainment of hot gas into the layer ([Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) 2019), so that destruction should need more resolution than growth. The adiabatic runs do not converge, and the radiative run in the supersonic wind has not converged even at 48 cells. For cosmological simulations of the CGM at low redshift, with subsonic flows and fast cooling, a few cells per cloud radius may thus capture the acceleration and growth of massive clouds, as in suites with cells of about 100 pc in the CGM that count clouds of at least 10 cells. Simulations of galaxies that resolve supersonic winds at high pressure need more. Magnetic fields, which inhibit fragmentation ([McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2015) 2015), and conduction, which reduced the effects of resolution on thermal instability once its Field length spanned at least 3 cells ([Koyama and Inutsuka](#koyama-inutsuka-2004) 2004), might lessen the dependence on resolution (§4.3). No single requirement of resolution exists: it varies between growth and destruction and with the Mach number of each set-up (§4.4).

#### 2.6.13 The turbulent Field length as a criterion of resolution

[Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) (2026, §5.3) measure resolution through the share $f_\mathrm{int}$ of gas at intermediate temperatures, $1.5\,T_\mathrm{cold} < T < T_\mathrm{hot}/1.5$, found $8\,\Delta x$ from gas at the peak temperature $T_\mathrm{pk}$ (eq. 30, Fig. 4). The distance of $8\,\Delta x$ is the smallest scale on which numerical viscosity leaves the turbulence intact. Once $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$ (2.2.9) is resolved, 80 per cent or more of that gas is intermediate, while unresolved layers show sharp transitions with $f_\mathrm{int} \approx 0$ (Fig. 9). The rise is gradual, and $f_\mathrm{int} \approx 40$ per cent already at $t_\mathrm{eddy}(8\Delta x) \approx 10\,t_\mathrm{cool,min}$. The phase structure converges in stages (§5.4, Fig. 10). When $c_\mathrm{s,pk} t_\mathrm{cool,min}$, the distance a sound wave crosses at the peak in the minimum cooling time, is unresolved, gas cools before it can be re-pressurised (2.6.3). Gas above $T_\mathrm{pk}$ is then under-represented and overpressured, which roughly balances in the total emission but distorts its distribution in temperature, while gas below $T_\mathrm{pk}$ is more robust. Since the turbulence of the mixed gas is subsonic, $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb} < c_\mathrm{s,pk} t_\mathrm{cool,min}$, and resolving it is the stricter condition. Doing so changes the distribution of cooling by about 50 per cent once the sound crossing is resolved.

A resolved phase structure thus needs the turbulent mixing ($\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$), the thermal pressure support ($c_\mathrm{s,pk} t_\mathrm{cool,min}$) and the turbulent pressure support resolved, the last of which is met first, since the largest eddies provide most of it ([Lancaster et al.](#lancaster-et-al-2026) 2026, §6.1). Resolving $c_\mathrm{s,pk} t_\mathrm{cool,min}$ gives a reasonable phase structure here, but emission and absorption, and especially lines affected by radiative trapping near the interface, may need $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$. Runs at low resolution may thus give the right total cooling but not the spectrum, since they under-represent the intermediate phase. Even with $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$ resolved, $v_\mathrm{diff}$ and $A_\mathrm{int}(\Delta x)$ keep scaling with resolution, which only explicit dissipation that smooths the interface at some scale could stop. The structure functions agree (Appendix B). With slow cooling, $\xi = 3$, the turbulence is isotropic on small scales and $\lambda_\mathrm{F,turb}$ is resolved. With fast cooling, $\xi = 1000$, the inflow dominates the turbulence down to the grid scale, and the layer is not one of true turbulent mixing but an inflow driven by the numerical diffusion of an advected surface.

#### 2.6.14 Resolution of fountain clouds

[Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) (2010, §3) weigh what their two-dimensional runs can show (2.7.2). Numerical viscosity truncates the hierarchy of KH vortices at a few cells, below which the fluid is perfectly mixed where reality holds a fractal foam. The flow parameters that best mimic reality at a given resolution may then themselves depend on resolution, as bare quantities do under renormalisation. Without cooling, the rate of mass loss does not depend on resolution, since the stripped gas is heated within a few turnovers of the large eddies, whatever the smallest vortices do (Fig. 3). With cooling, the structure of the wake becomes crucial. Finer grids, with a longer hierarchy of vortices, disperse the stripped gas more effectively and so favour evaporation over condensation, although the medium and fine grids agree (Fig. 4). The divide between evaporation and condensation in pressure and metallicity can therefore not be fixed with confidence, only shown to lie near the conditions of the Galactic corona. Two dimensions also flatten the spectrum of the turbulence and so make mixing more effective than in three, and the runs may overestimate the pressure or metallicity of that divide (§4).

### 2.7 Fountain clouds and the condensation of the corona

#### 2.7.1 Thermal instability in haloes

Thermal instability makes a multiphase medium directly: local overdensities cool slightly faster than the thermally stable background, grow denser and cool faster still ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.4; [Field](#field-1965) 1965). The details are intricate. In a stratified hot atmosphere that is stable against convection, condensation was long thought impossible, since large-scale inflows advect the perturbations before they can grow ([Balbus and Soker](#balbus-soker-1989) 1989, among others). [Sharma et al.](#sharma-et-al-2012) (2012) and [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2012) (2012) showed that thermal instability is possible when heating suppresses these inflows and the ratio of the cooling to the free-fall time lies below a constant of order unity, $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{ff} \lesssim O(1)$ (eq. 10). The reviews of [Donahue and Voit](#donahue-voit-2022) (2022) and [Faucher-Giguère and Oh](#faucher-giguere-oh-2023) (2023) treat thermal instability in full, and Gronke and Schneider focus on recent simulations, which modify the criterion in several ways:

- Fast uplift of a parcel of gas keeps its cooling time nearly constant while its free-fall time grows strongly, which can satisfy eq. 10 and trigger the instability. Early work discussed this ([Shapiro and Field](#shapiro-field-1976) 1976; [Bregman](#bregman-1980) 1980), and simulations on the scale of haloes have since shown it ([Li and Bryan](#li-bryan-2014) 2014, among others).
- In a rotating halo the effective free-fall time is longer, which makes the halo more prone to the instability ([Sobacchi and Sormani](#sobacchi-sormani-2019) 2019).
- Local overdensities shorten the cooling time and raise the probability of precipitation ([Choudhury, Sharma and Quataert](#choudhury-et-al-2019) 2019). Eq. 10 then holds locally, with the overdensity in the cooling time, while the global criterion at a given radius has a lower threshold.
- Turbulence forms such overdensities. [Gaspari et al.](#gaspari-et-al-2018) (2018) proposed $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{eddy} \lesssim 1$, with the turnover time $t_\mathrm{eddy}$ of eddies on a relevant scale in the ICM, and analogous criteria hold in the ISM. [Mohapatra et al.](#mohapatra-et-al-2023) (2023) combined it with eq. 10 into a new empirical relation. This criterion of precipitation differs from the criterion of survival in a turbulent medium, which relates to the time of destruction of cold gas and resembles eq. 7 ([Gronke et al.](#gronke-et-al-2022) 2022; footnote 16).
- Even weak magnetic fields, down to $\beta \sim 100$, make precipitation easier, since they suppress buoyant oscillations on scales below about $v_\mathrm{A} t_\mathrm{cool}$, and the cold gas that forms is more filamentary ([Ji, Oh and McCourt](#ji-et-al-2017) 2017).
- Cosmic rays that heat the gas change the linear criterion ([Kempski and Quataert](#kempski-quataert-2020) 2020), and in the non-linear stage the morphology and dynamics of the multiphase gas. Their non-thermal pressure can lower the overdensity of the cold gas considerably. Methods of two moments for the transport of cosmic rays have recently made more realistic transport possible in multiphase simulations (footnote 17).

The theory stands on solid ground, but its application to cosmological simulations is less certain ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.4). In the FIRE and Illustris-TNG50 simulations, the ratio $t_\mathrm{cool}/t_\mathrm{ff}$ of a parcel of gas in a halo predicts its thermal evolution poorly ([Esmerian et al.](#esmerian-et-al-2020) 2020; [Nelson et al.](#nelson-et-al-2020) 2020), perhaps because the hot medium in FIRE flows in as a cooling flow. Observations remain the final benchmark.

#### 2.7.2 Fountain clouds that condense the corona

[Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) (2010, §1) argue that disc galaxies like the Milky Way sustain their star formation, which needs an accretion of about $1\,M_\odot\,\mathrm{yr^{-1}}$ over 10 Gyr, by condensing gas from a corona at the virial temperature, and that the galactic fountain carries out the transfer. The neutral gas a kiloparsec or more above the disc, 10 to 25 per cent of the H I of such galaxies, consists of clouds on ballistic orbits launched by supernovae, which return within about 100 Myr. Models of the fountain fit the rotation and the inflow of this gas in NGC 891 and NGC 2403 only if the clouds gain mass from gas of low angular momentum on a timescale of about 650 Myr ([Fraternali and Binney](#fraternali-binney-2006) 2006, [2008](#fraternali-binney-2008)). Fraternali and Binney sought its source in cool streams, since coronal gas flows past a cloud much faster than it cools. Marinacci et al. argue instead that the interaction itself cools the corona. In the corona of [Fukugita and Peebles](#fukugita-peebles-2006) (2006), at $1.8 \times 10^6$ K, the cooling time of gas in pressure balance falls from about 300 Myr at $2 \times 10^6$ K to 8 Myr at $5 \times 10^5$ K and 0.4 Myr at $2.5 \times 10^5$ K (§2.1, Fig. 1). About $4.5 \times 10^9\,M_\odot$ of H I passes through the $2 \times 10^8\,M_\odot$ of the lower corona each gigayear, so mixing 4.5 per cent of it would halve the coronal temperature and bring its cooling time down to the orbital time. The clouds, with $\rho_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{h} \approx 200$, lose their momentum to ram pressure in that many times the time to cross their own length, and a corona of a twentieth of their mass cannot keep it; the disc therefore likely accretes the coronal gas the clouds meet (§2.2). Only a disc that forms stars can grab coronal gas this way, and without one, cooling coronal gas feeds the central black hole, as in the cooling flows of clusters (§2.3).

Gas stripped from a cloud of mass $M_\mathrm{c}$ at a rate $\alpha M_\mathrm{c}$ mixes in a wake of cross-section $A_\mathrm{w} = \beta(M_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{c})^{2/3}$, with $\beta \sim 1$, with coronal gas of density $\rho_\mathrm{h}$ and temperature $T_\mathrm{h}$ ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) 2010, §2.4). Neglecting cooling during the mixing and taking pressure balance, the mixture has $T_\mathrm{m} = T_\mathrm{h}[1 + (M_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{c})^{1/3}\alpha/\beta v]/[1 + (M_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{c})^{1/3}(T_\mathrm{h}/T_\mathrm{c})\alpha/\beta v]$ (eqs. 1 to 3), well below $T_\mathrm{h}$ since $T_\mathrm{h}/T_\mathrm{c} \gtrsim 200$, which shortens its cooling time severalfold. The wake turns into H I within the flight of the cloud if $\alpha$ exceeds $\alpha_\mathrm{crit} = \beta v T_\mathrm{c}/[(M_\mathrm{c}/\rho_\mathrm{c})^{1/3} T_\mathrm{h}]$ (eq. 4), that is, if the cloud loses a fraction $T_\mathrm{c}/T_\mathrm{h}$ of its mass while it travels its own length. Below this rate the stripped gas joins the corona and lowers its cooling time, which in turn lowers $\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$ for later wakes. Cooling during the mixing would lower the estimate further. Dimensional analysis places the actual rate near $\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$: with $\dot M_\mathrm{c} = -b\pi r^2\rho_\mathrm{h} v$ and $b \sim 1$, $\alpha = (9\pi/16)^{1/3}(b/\beta)\,\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$ (eq. 5). A cloud that travels a distance $L$ then mixes away completely if it is lighter than $M_\mathrm{crit} = (9\pi/16)(bL\,T_\mathrm{c}/T_\mathrm{h})^3\rho_\mathrm{c}$ (eq. 6), so that it travels about $T_\mathrm{h}/T_\mathrm{c}$ times its size before destruction. **[GK]** Eq. 6 follows for a rate of mass loss held at its initial value. With $\dot M_\mathrm{c} \propto M_\mathrm{c}^{2/3}$, as eq. 5 implies, the cloud lasts three times longer and the threshold mass is 27 times lower.

Two-dimensional simulations follow a cloud of radius 100 pc at $10^4$ K, or $5 \times 10^3$ K in the densest run, moving at $75\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ through a corona at $2 \times 10^6$ K with a particle density of $10^{-3}$ or $2 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$, at metallicities from primordial to solar, each with and without cooling ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) 2010, §3, Table 1; 3.13). Gravity and the variation of the coronal density along the orbit are neglected, as is conduction. At rest such clouds would be stable against evaporation ([Nipoti and Binney](#nipoti-binney-2007) 2007), and conduction that damps the KH instability ([Vieser and Hensler](#vieser-hensler-2007) 2007) matters little if magnetic fields suppress it. Without cooling, ram pressure flattens the cloud into a pancake that sheds vortices from its edges. About $200\,M_\odot$, 2 per cent of the cloud, is heated above $5 \times 10^5$ K in 25 Myr at every resolution, close to $\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$ for $\beta$ of 0.1 to 0.3 (Figs. 2, 3). The rate grows slowly as the pancake widens, and scales as $M_\mathrm{c}^{-1/3}$, as a run with a radius of 200 pc confirms. A cylinder in two dimensions loses mass along an edge of $\frac{8}{3}r$, a sphere along $2\pi r$, so the measured rates are about 0.42 of the true ones, and in three dimensions $\beta$ would lie near 1. On trajectories of about 7 kpc in 100 Myr, clouds lighter than about $10^3\,M_\odot$ mix away before they return to the disc. With cooling, the mass of gas below $5 \times 10^5$ K first grows at every metallicity, the faster the higher the metallicity (Fig. 4). At $10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ it keeps growing over 25 Myr only for $[\mathrm{Fe/H}] = -0.5$, and declines later at lower metallicities. At twice the density, $[\mathrm{Fe/H}] = -1$ suffices for a growth that accelerates over 50 Myr (Fig. 5), which, extrapolated to the halo of the Milky Way, gives an accretion of about $0.5\,M_\odot\,\mathrm{yr^{-1}}$. Cooling makes the wake longer and narrower (Fig. 6), and accretion persists down to $n_\mathrm{h} \approx 4 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ at about solar metallicity.

Hydrodynamics rather than gravity shapes the cool gas in the wake ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2010) 2010, §3.3, Figs. 7, 8). Less than 1 per cent of it lies more than 1 kpc behind the cloud, but it spreads over a range of velocities comparable to the speed of the cloud: eddies carry a few hundred $M_\odot$ faster than the cloud, and slightly more backwards. Its densest part falls from $70\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ at the cloud to about $20\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ 0.5 kpc behind it, a gradient of about $80\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,kpc^{-1}}$, so velocity gradients along observed complexes need not be gravitational. The trail of a single cloud in another galaxy would be too faint to detect, falling two orders of magnitude in antenna temperature within 0.5 kpc and $25\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, but together the trails add to the extraplanar H I (§4). In the Milky Way they may explain the tadpole shapes of high-velocity clouds and the elongated structures in surveys of H I. Unlike the earlier model, in which clouds grow, here each cloud loses mass while cloud and wake together gain it. Condensation depends on a roughly even mixture of cloud and coronal gas, and real clouds are up to ten times more metal-rich than the corona, so the runs at half the metallicity of real clouds are the most realistic. Earlier studies of ablation agree ([Murray et al.](#murray-et-al-1993) 1993; [Heitsch and Putman](#heitsch-putman-2009) 2009, among others). Murray et al. found faster ablation, at the growth rate of the KH instability, which exceeds $\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$ by about $(T_\mathrm{h}/T_\mathrm{c})^{1/2}$; the authors trace the difference to their definition of cloud gas by position rather than temperature. Conditions at the base of the corona should adjust until the disc accretes: if $\alpha_\mathrm{crit}$ lay above the actual rates, the density and metallicity of the corona would rise until it fell below them (§5).

#### 2.7.3 Fountain clouds and the rotation of the corona

[Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2011) (2011, §1, §2) extend the runs of 2.7.2 to relative speeds from 50 to $200\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, from a corona that nearly corotates with the disc to a static one, over 60 Myr. The code now lets the metallicity evolve, with the cloud at solar metallicity and the corona at a tenth of it, and lets the mean molecular weight vary with temperature. The earlier run at a uniform $0.3\,Z_\odot$ behaves much like the new one at the same density and speed. Drag between H I clouds and a lagging corona had been proposed to explain why extraplanar gas rotates more slowly with height, but [Fraternali and Binney](#fraternali-binney-2008) (2008) showed that the corona would soon be spun up and the drag would vanish within an orbital time of a cloud. Hot gas is here gas above $10^6$ K and cold gas gas below $3 \times 10^4$ K, a choice that hardly matters (§3).

With cooling, the wake mixes coronal and cloud gas efficiently and cools, so that the cold mass grows, while ram pressure decelerates the cloud and accelerates the corona ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2011) 2011, §3.1, Figs. 1 to 3). Momentum passes mostly to gas at intermediate temperatures, between about $10^5$ and $5 \times 10^5$ K, where cooling peaks, and as this gas cools, the momentum it took from the cloud stays in the cold phase instead of reaching the corona. The centroid velocity of the cold gas, its total momentum along the motion divided by its mass, falls to about $0.75\,v_0$ after 60 Myr for every $v_0$ with cooling (§3.2, Fig. 4). This lies below the drag law of a rigid body, $v(t) = v_0/(1 + t/t_\mathrm{drag})$ with $t_\mathrm{drag} = M_\mathrm{cl}/(v_0\sigma\rho_\mathrm{h})$ for a cross-section $\sigma$, which fits only adiabatic runs at low speed (eqs. 1, 2), since stripping and the flattening of the cloud raise the drag. Adiabatic runs follow one curve in units of $v_0$ and $R_\mathrm{cl}/v_0$. With cooling, the departure begins at the same physical time, about 20 Myr, for all speeds, as condensing coronal gas, initially at rest, dominates the deceleration. The corona gains less momentum with cooling: by 50 Myr the ratio of the dissipative to the adiabatic gain is about 0.8 at $200\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, 0.5 at 100 and below 0.1 at $75\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, where the transfer stops altogether after about 20 Myr (§3.3, Fig. 5). Below a threshold speed $v_\mathrm{th}$, the corona thus absorbs no momentum from the clouds. It is about $75\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ at $10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$, 85 at twice and 50 at half that density, so that $v_\mathrm{th} \sim 50$ to $85\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ for coronae of 0.5 to $2 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ a few kiloparsecs above the plane. Without cooling, momentum passes to the corona efficiently at every speed (§5).

A static corona would therefore gain momentum quickly, from clouds that cross it at about $200\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, until clouds move through it at about $v_\mathrm{th}$ ([Marinacci et al.](#marinacci-et-al-2011) 2011, §4.1). With a mean vertical speed of the clouds of about $40\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, the corona lags the clouds by $v_\mathrm{lag} \approx (v_\mathrm{th}^2 - \langle v_z\rangle^2)^{1/2} \approx 65\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$. It then lags the disc by about $95\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ at a height of 2 kpc, a coronal rotation of about $125\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ for a solar rotation of $220\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, and the lag would grow from about 70 to $120\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ between 0.5 and 4 kpc. Isothermal coronae in equilibrium in the potential of the Milky Way, rotating at up to 100 or $150\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, are flattened into peanut shapes with axis ratios of 0.5 to 0.7 (Fig. 6). The interaction also explains why the rotation of extraplanar gas declines with height (§4.2). A cloud that reaches $\langle z_\mathrm{max}\rangle = 2$ kpc at $\langle v_z\rangle = 40\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$ takes $t_\mathrm{orb} \approx 50$ Myr (eq. 4), and its loss of speed $\Delta v$ in that time gives a gradient $\Delta v_\varphi/\Delta z = \Delta v/\langle z_\mathrm{max}\rangle$ (eq. 5). This is about $-20\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,kpc^{-1}}$ for a static corona, $-7$ for one spinning such that clouds move through it at $75\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, and only $-3$ for the same case without cooling (eqs. 6 to 8). With the change of radius along the orbit, which accounts for about half of the observed $-15\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}\,kpc^{-1}}$ of the Milky Way, cooling and drag together come close to it, in support of the accretion by fountain clouds of Fraternali and Binney. Three-dimensional and eventually global simulations, with the clouds on orbits in the potential of the galaxy, are the next steps (§5).

## 3. Software architecture

The software architecture records the codes and set-ups of the simulations, as far as the papers describe them. §1 and §2.1 of the review name no code of the simulations that they discuss. 2.2.1 describes the set-up of the shear layer, and 2.5 the codes of radiative transfer that §1.3 names.

### 3.1 Wind tunnels

In a wind tunnel, a hot wind blows over a cold cloud, usually spherical ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §2.2). Since the cold gas grows mainly in the wake, in tails of up to $0.5\,\chi r_\mathrm{cl}$ (2.3.3), the domain must be long. Techniques of cloud tracking change the frame of reference continuously to keep the cloud inside it ([McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2015) 2015; [Brüggen and Scannapieco](#bruggen-scannapieco-2016) 2016, among others), and [Dutta and Sharma](#dutta-sharma-2019) (2019) describe them in detail. Boundary conditions can impose an expanding wind (2.3.4). Infalling clouds are followed in a domain that moves with the cloud, whose boundary conditions are modified accordingly (2.3.5).

### 3.2 Codes and hardware

The review traces the growth of the field to that of computing ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.1). For many multiphase problems, such as mixing layers, turbulent boxes and outflows, refinement by mass fails, since instabilities must be resolved in gas that may be thinner than the median, and adaptive refinement makes robust studies of convergence hard or impossible. Apart from devices for particular set-ups, such as cloud tracking or refinement on the cooling length in the CGM, these simulations need expensive domains of fixed resolution or of targeted static refinement, a main reason why many early models ran in one or two dimensions. Their physics, however, is mostly local. MHD, optically thin cooling and many models of radiative transfer and of the transport of cosmic rays need only a local stencil, which suits massive parallelism.

Clock speeds stopped growing in the mid-2000s, so that power came from more cores, served by a first generation of parallel codes, among them FLASH, RAMSES, GADGET, Athena, PIERNIK, AREPO and Enzo ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.1). Graphics processing units (GPUs), with thousands of slow cores on a chip and most of their transistors spent on arithmetic rather than control, then opened a wide gap in peak performance over CPUs (Fig. 12). High-level programming models such as CUDA brought them into science from the early 2010s. The codes GAMER and Cholla ([Schneider and Robertson](#schneider-robertson-2015) 2015) showed large speed-ups early, provided that the data were managed carefully and the GPUs had enough work. Newer codes for modern machines include DISPATCH, Phantom, QUOKKA, Parthenon, SWIFT and AthenaK. They make simulations of more than $10^{10}$ resolution elements feasible, and parameter studies at lower resolution cheap.

Challenges remain ([Gronke and Schneider](#gronke-schneider-2026) 2026, §3.1). Adaptive time-stepping, which lets elements take different steps instead of all following the fastest signal, saves much time, but is hard to balance across nodes and to port to GPUs, since the elements then follow different paths of control. Languages for GPUs are not standardised: CUDA is proprietary and runs only on NVIDIA hardware, other vendors offer HIP or SYCL, and frameworks such as Kokkos, RAJA, OpenACC and OpenMP make code portable across CPUs and GPUs, although with fewer features. Data are a last challenge, since a snapshot of tens of billions of elements fills terabytes. Analysis must therefore move into the running simulation, through tools that are often specific to one code, through modular libraries that plug into several, or through public archives.

### 3.3 The code of Klein, McKee and Colella

The code of [Klein, McKee and Colella](#klein-et-al-1994) (1994, §3) is two-dimensional and axisymmetric, a second-order Godunov scheme on a local adaptive mesh ([Berger and Oliger](#berger-oliger-1984) 1984; [Berger and Colella](#berger-colella-1989) 1989). A rectangular grid covers the domain, and nested rectangular meshes refine it, in three levels with a factor of 4 in each direction, so that a cell of level 1 holds 256 cells of level 3. Estimates of the local truncation error by Richardson extrapolation decide where to refine, and the maximum level is also set directly: every cell that holds cloud material is refined to level 3, while intercloud gas away from the cloud stays at lower levels. The code carries two fluids and maintains the interface between cloud and intercloud gas throughout, with a no-slip condition at the interface, and the refinement acts on both fluids. A planar shock runs along the axis of symmetry. The calculation runs in the frame of the shocked intercloud gas, in which the cloud eventually comes to rest, so that initially the shock moves at $v'_\mathrm{b} = (\frac{1}{4} + \frac{3}{4M^2})\,v_\mathrm{b}$ and the cloud at $-\frac{3}{4}(1 - M^{-2})\,v_\mathrm{b}$, for $\gamma = 5/3$ (eqs. 3.1, 3.2). The domain is $16a_0$ long and $3a_0$ in radius, and at $R_{120}$ the cloud radius spans 7.5 cells of level 1. The runs, listed in Table 1, cover contrasts of 3 to 400 and Mach numbers of 10, 100 and 1000, and mostly last about four crushing times, the standard case $9.66\,t_\mathrm{cc}$. They ran on a Cray Y-MP 8/128 at the Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory (acknowledgements).

### 3.4 The mixing layers of Ji, Oh and Masterson

[Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019, §2) use the code FLASH ([Fryxell et al.](#fryxell-et-al-2000) 2000) with its directionally unsplit staggered-mesh solver for ideal MHD, a finite-volume Godunov method of high order with constrained transport, and an equation for the entropy with the cooling rate as its source (eqs. 3a–3e). The domain is 100 pc wide in the direction of the flow, $x$, and in $y$, and 300 pc tall across the interface, $z$, on $128 \times 128 \times 384$ cells. Its sides are periodic and its top and bottom open. Density and velocity change from the cold to the hot phase as hyperbolic tangents of half-width $a = 2.5$ pc (eqs. 4, 5), and a vertical velocity of a hundredth of the shear, $\delta v\,e^{-(z/a)^2} \sin(2\pi x/\lambda) \sin(2\pi y/\lambda)$, seeds the KH instability (eq. 6). Periodic sides capture the saturated state, a section of the wake behind a cloud, but only modes that fit the box, so boxes twice as wide checked the cases with thick layers. Long boxes with open ends would follow the spatial growth of the layer, but not its saturated state. The tall box keeps the inflowing hot gas at its initial velocity, and the results do not change when the cold gas moves instead of the hot.

A solver for NEI follows all ions of He, C, N, O and Si, started in equilibrium, with photoionisation rates for the optically thin metagalactic background of Haardt and Madau at redshift 0 (eqs. 7, 8). The cooling uses equilibrium curves with photoionisation, computed with Cloudy on 121 densities from $10^{-8}$ to $10\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and interpolated (Fig. 1), rather than the losses of the time-dependent ions, which would cost too much memory. The cooling is integrated exactly after [Townsend](#townsend-2009) (2009), with each curve fitted by power laws in 40 bins of temperature (eq. 9), so that the cooling time does not limit the step. The runs used XSEDE and the analysis the package yt (acknowledgements).

### 3.5 The fractal mixing layers of Fielding et al.

[Fielding et al.](#fielding-et-al-2020) (2020, §3) use Athena++ ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2020) 2020) for three-dimensional hydrodynamics on a static Cartesian mesh, with $E = P/(\gamma - 1)$ and $\gamma = 5/3$, without gravity. Cold, dense gas and hot, thin gas in pressure equilibrium shear along $x$ at $v_\mathrm{rel}$, joined smoothly as in [Lecoanet et al.](#lecoanet-et-al-2015) (2015), with the gradient along $z$. White noise on the grid scale and a sinusoidal $v_z$ with the wavelength $L$ of the box and an amplitude of $v_\mathrm{rel}/25$, decaying away from the interface, seed the instability. The box is periodic in $x$ and $y$, of size $L$, and $10L$ tall, with boundaries in $z$ that hold density, pressure and streamwise velocity fixed and give $v_y$ and $v_z$ zero gradients. Static refinement concentrates the resolution within $|z| \le 1.5L$, mostly at $\Delta x = L/128$, with resolutions from 8 times coarser to 4 times finer, and $L/512$ in the fiducial run. The cooling function is log-normal, peaked at $T_\mathrm{mix} = (T_\mathrm{hot} T_\mathrm{cold})^{1/2}$ and a hundred times lower at $T_\mathrm{cold}$ and $T_\mathrm{hot}$, which approximates a CGM curve but frees the results from a particular one, and $t_\mathrm{cool}$ denotes the cooling time at $T_\mathrm{mix}$. Since the losses scale as $\rho^2\Lambda$, the shortest cooling time lies somewhat below $T_\mathrm{mix}$, an offset of order unity against eq. 6. The survey spans $\chi$ from 10 to 1000, $\mathcal{M}$ from $10^{-1}$ to $10^{0.5}$ and $\xi$ from $10^{-3}$ to $10^2$, plus adiabatic runs, each for at least $60\,t_\mathrm{sh}$, with the fiducial run at $\xi = 10$, $\chi = 100$ and $\mathcal{M} = 10^{-1/2}$.

### 3.6 Fronts and mixing layers of Tan, Oh and Gronke

[Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) (2021, §2) use Athena++ on Cartesian grids with the HLLC Riemann solver. Their cooling follows the equilibrium curve of [Gnat and Sternberg](#gnat-sternberg-2007) (2007) for solar metallicity, fitted by power laws in 40 bins from a floor of $10^4$ K and integrated exactly after [Townsend](#townsend-2009) (2009). In one dimension a heating rate that depends on density makes the medium thermally bistable, with stable phases at $10^4$ and $10^6$ K (Fig. 1), while in three dimensions a fixed floor replaces it and gas above half the hot temperature does not cool. Cooling and conduction are scaled by factors $\Lambda_0$ and $\kappa_0$ (eqs. 6, 7), and the surface brightness, $Q \approx \frac{5}{2} P v_\mathrm{in}$ (eq. 8), serves as the measure, since it does not depend on the frame. Conduction uses a constant conductivity of $10^6\,\mathrm{erg\,cm^{-1}\,s^{-1}\,K^{-1}}$, the Spitzer value at $8 \times 10^4$ K, rather than $\kappa_\mathrm{sp} = 5.7 \times 10^{-7}\,T^{5/2}$ (eqs. 3, 4). It is integrated by a method of two moments after [Jiang and Oh](#jiang-oh-2018) (2018), which evolves the heat flux with a finite propagation speed $V_\mathrm{m}$, physically that of free electrons, about 45 times the sound speed, and returns to ordinary conduction as $V_\mathrm{m} \to \infty$ (eq. 5). Its step then scales as $\Delta x$ rather than $(\Delta x)^2$, without the global matrix inversion of implicit schemes, with operator splitting, a two-step van Leer integrator and the source term added implicitly. A diffusion couple tests the module against the solution with an error function (App. A).

The one-dimensional fronts span $-100$ to 300 pc, or $-400$ to 1200 pc at low resolution, and start from the steady solution, centred at $10^5$ K, between gas of $10^4$ K and $10^{-2}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and gas of $10^6$ K and $10^{-4}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$, with open ends ([Tan, Oh and Gronke](#tan-et-al-2021) 2021, §3.1). The three-dimensional layers follow [Ji, Oh and Masterson](#ji-et-al-2019) (2019), but with the shear along $y$ and the normal along $x$, on a box of $300 \times 100 \times 100$ pc and $384 \times 128 \times 128$ cells of 0.78 pc, about the smallest Field length when conduction is on, periodic across and open along the normal (§5.1). The shear, $v_y = \frac{1}{2} v_\mathrm{shear} \tanh(x/a)$ with $a = 5$ pc and $v_\mathrm{shear} = 100\,\mathrm{km\,s^{-1}}$, is perturbed by $\delta v_x = A\,e^{-x^2/a^2} \sin(k_y y) \sin(k_z z)$ with $A$ a hundredth of the shear and wavelengths of about the box (eqs. 33, 34). With conduction, $V_\mathrm{m}$ is about 15 times the hot sound speed. The runs last at least 20 Myr, and quantities are measured in their second half.

### 3.7 The shattering simulations of McCourt et al.

[McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2018) (2018, Appendix A) solve the equations of ideal hydrodynamics in two dimensions with Athena ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008; [Gardiner and Stone](#gardiner-stone-2008) 2008), on a fixed Cartesian grid of uniform resolution, so that the wakes of disrupting clouds are resolved. High Mach numbers and sharp gradients make conservative codes prone to crashes, so the van Leer integrator of Athena is used, with second-order reconstruction in primitive variables, more diffusive and robust than the default integrator. Runs with the default CTU integrator and third-order reconstruction also shatter, and the authors suspect that, if anything, their choice overestimates mixing and the destruction of cloudlets. The cooling follows a piecewise power-law fit to the curve of [Sutherland and Dopita](#sutherland-dopita-1993) (1993) for collisional ionisation equilibrium at solar metallicity (Fig. 2), integrated exactly after [Townsend](#townsend-2009) (2009). That scheme writes the operator-split energy equation as $t_\mathrm{cool}\,\mathrm{d}T/\mathrm{d}t = -T_0\,\Lambda(T)/\Lambda(T_0)$, with $T_0$ the temperature at the start of the step (eq. 11), and separates the variables, with a closed form for power laws. Cooling times far below the dynamical time then do not limit the time step. A floor at $10^4$ K keeps cloudlets from cooling and contracting further.

For thermal instability (Appendix A.1), the code units $\rho \sim 1$ and $T \sim 1$ stand for $n \sim 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{cm^{-3}}$ and $T \sim 10^7$ K. A Gaussian random field $\delta$, with a power spectrum $\propto k^{-1}$ between $2\pi/L$ and $40 \times 2\pi/L$, where $L$ is the shortest side of the domain, and normalised to unit RMS, perturbs the density as $\rho \to \rho\,e^{2+\delta}$ (eq. 12a). Densities above 10 are then flattened to $10 + (\rho - 10)^{1/2}$ (eq. 12b), which keeps the mass from concentrating in a small fraction of the domain. The pressure stays uniform, so $T \propto 1/\rho$, and about half of the domain starts near $10^6$ K at $\rho \sim 10$. This seeds no tiny structure such as shattering might produce, and gives a large perturbation that is easier to resolve. The runs last about one cooling time of the gas at $10^6$ K, on domains of $10^5$, $10^3$ and $10\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$ with $4096 \times 2048$, $512 \times 256$ and $256 \times 128$ cells. The small domains cost most, through their short sound-crossing time.

The cloud crushing (Appendix A.2) follows [McCourt et al.](#mccourt-et-al-2015) (2015), without magnetic fields and in two dimensions. After [Shin, Stone and Snyder](#shin-et-al-2008) (2008), a passive scalar marks the cloud, and the frame is boosted after every step to keep the cloud inside the domain (3.1), which shrinks the grid and reduces truncation errors by minimising the velocity of the cloud relative to the grid. A steady wind of $v_x = 1.5\,c_\mathrm{s}$, with the sound speed of the hot medium, enters at the upstream boundary. The downstream boundary is open, with zero gradients, and the transverse boundaries are periodic, for simplicity and stability. The domain spans $6.666 \times 26.666\,R_\mathrm{cloud}$, on $2048 \times 8192$ cells at the standard 300 cells per radius, and the figures show its upper half. The run of entrainment (Appendix A.3) uses a background of $10^6$ K and many cloudlets of about $10\,c_\mathrm{s} t_\mathrm{cool}$, each resolved by $64^2$ cells, on $4096 \times 16384$ cells. It outlasts the cooling time of the hot gas, so the energy lost to cooling after each call of the integrator of Townsend is returned at a constant rate per unit volume, a crude feedback that keeps the hot phase.

### 3.8 The wind tunnel of Gronke and Oh

[Gronke and Oh](#gronke-oh-2018) (2018, §3) solve the equations of hydrodynamics in three dimensions with Athena 4.0 ([Stone et al.](#stone-et-al-2008) 2008) on a uniform Cartesian grid. A spherical cloud of radius $r_\mathrm{cl}$ sits in a steady hot wind of $\mathcal{M}_\mathrm{wind} = 1.5$, transonic like galactic winds, with $\chi = \rho_\mathrm{cl}/\rho_\mathrm{wind}$ of 100, 300 or 1000.
